## A NOTE ON METHOD AND OBLIGATION

Three things should be said before the first chapter, because they govern everything
that follows.

**On evidence.** This book makes a great many historical claims, and each of them is
meant to be checkable. Where the evidence is strong, I say so plainly. Where it is
contested, I say who contests it. Where it does not exist, I say that too, and I have
tried never to let a resemblance do the work of a document. The reader will encounter,
repeatedly, sentences that begin *there is no surviving evidence that* — and will, I
hope, come to find them more interesting than the sentences that begin *it has long
been said*.

**On ritual.** Freemasonry is a body of men and women under obligation. I have not
reproduced private ritual material, and I have not invented any. Everything quoted or
described in these pages comes from sources that have been public for between sixty and
three hundred years: printed constitutions, the eighteenth-century exposures, published
catechisms, and the twentieth-century publications of orders that no longer exist. Where
I describe a ceremony, I am describing a documented historical text, not a current
working — and Masonic ritual varies enormously by jurisdiction, by century, and by
degree. No single system described here should be taken as universal. There is no such
thing as *the* Masonic ritual, and one of the pleasures of the subject is discovering
how thoroughly that is so.

**On sympathy.** I am not a debunker. The men and women in this book were, with a few
memorable exceptions, sincere. They believed they had found something; many of them were
changed by it; some of them were changed by things they had built themselves without
knowing it. Treating their claims as historical propositions — which is to say, testing
them — is not a form of contempt. It is the only way I know to take a person seriously
enough to find out what actually happened to them.

# INTRODUCTION
## The Room You Cannot See Into

There is a building in almost every town in the English-speaking world that you have
walked past without looking at. It has no display window. Its door is usually locked.
Above the door, or set into the brick, there is a symbol: two draftsman's instruments
crossed, a square and a pair of compasses, sometimes with a letter G between them.
Nothing else identifies it. In a small American town it may be the second-largest
building on the main street. In an English market town it may be a converted chapel. In
Paris or Philadelphia or Edinburgh it may be enormous, columned, and frankly
extraordinary, a temple built in the nineteenth century by men who had decided that
their meeting hall should look like the front of a building in Karnak.

Inside, a few times a month, something happens that almost no outsider has seen and that
almost every outsider has an opinion about.

That gap — between how little is known and how much is asserted — is the subject of this
book.

---

For three hundred years, two stories have been told about what goes on in that room.

The first story says that Freemasonry is the surviving vessel of an ancient wisdom
tradition: that the square and compasses are Egyptian, or Kabbalistic, or Templar; that
the ritual encodes knowledge that predates Christianity; that somewhere in the higher
degrees a real secret is kept. This story is told with admiration by occultists and with
alarm by conspiracists, but it is structurally the same story. Both versions require the
lodge to be a container for something very old.

The second story says that Freemasonry is a fraternal club with an unusually elaborate
initiation ceremony: that it raises money for children's hospitals, that its symbolism
is Victorian moral allegory of no great depth, and that anyone who finds something
stranger in it has been reading too much. This story is told with relief by many
Freemasons and with condescension by many historians, and it, too, is structurally
single: it requires the lodge to be a container for nothing much at all.

I spent several years expecting to arrive at some sensible middle position between them.
I did not arrive there. The evidence took me somewhere else, and the somewhere else is
the reason this book exists.

---

Here is the difficulty with the first story, stated as plainly as I can manage.

If you go looking for the route by which ancient Egyptian, Hermetic, Kabbalistic or
Templar teaching entered Freemasonry, you will not find it. Not because it is hidden —
because it is not there. The documents that survive from the formative centuries of the
Craft are a medieval trade legend, a set of Scottish administrative statutes from 1598
and 1599, a handful of seventeenth-century diary entries, a few catechisms written down
in the 1690s, and a printed constitution of 1723 composed by a Presbyterian minister for
a circle of Newtonian gentlemen in London. There is no missing manuscript. There is no
suppressed chapter. There is a great deal of evidence, and what it documents is
something other than an ancient survival.

And here is the difficulty with the second story.

If Freemasonry is nothing but moral allegory and charity dinners, then somebody needs to
explain why, in 1761, a Bordeaux merchant named Martinez de Pasqually was conducting
theurgic invocations of angelic intelligences inside a structure of Masonic degrees, and
why the man who systematized his work went on to found a Masonic rite that still exists.
Somebody needs to explain why the Gold- und Rosenkreuz, the most politically influential
occult order of the eighteenth century, required that a candidate be a Freemason before
it would teach him alchemy. Somebody needs to explain why the three men who founded the
Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn in 1888 — the order from which very nearly all modern
Western ceremonial magic descends — were all Freemasons, and why the order they designed
has, beneath its Egyptian costume and its Hebrew letters, the unmistakable skeleton of a
lodge.

Both stories are wrong. They are wrong in the same way. They both assume that if
Freemasonry and magic are related, the relationship must run *backward* — from
antiquity, through a hidden channel, into the present.

The evidence says the traffic runs the other way.

---

The argument of this book can be put in a single sentence, and I would rather put it at
the beginning than pretend to suspense I do not feel.

**Freemasonry did not inherit the Western magical tradition. Freemasonry built the
vehicle that carried it — and then, for a hundred and fifty years, people who climbed
aboard that vehicle insisted it was ancient.**

What Freemasonry supplied to the modern world was not a doctrine. It was a *form*: a
graded, secret, theatrical, portable method of initiation, built out of entirely
ordinary materials in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, and — crucially —
almost empty of fixed content. A Masonic degree is a machine with a shape and no cargo.
It has a darkened room, an obligation, a staged revelation, a symbolic death, a
restoration of light, and a new name for something the candidate already half knew. It
does not tell you what any of that means. It has never told anyone what any of it means.

That emptiness is why it worked. Fill the form with moral instruction and you have the
Craft of 1723. Fill it with crusader nostalgia and you have the Rite of Strict
Observance of 1754. Fill it with angelic operations and you have the Élus Coëns of 1761.
Fill it with alchemy and you have the Gold and Rosy Cross. Fill it with a reconstructed
Kabbalah and a deck of tarot cards and you have the Golden Dawn of 1888. Fill it with
Thelema and you have the Ordo Templi Orientis under Aleister Crowley. And — this is the
part that the occult history usually omits, and that the fraternal history usually
mentions only in passing — carry it across the Atlantic and hand it to a free Black
leatherworker in Boston in 1784, and you get African Lodge No. 459, and a form of
associational life that would matter to Black America for two centuries.

The same machine, over and over, doing what it does, meaning whatever its users needed
it to mean.

---

This book is therefore three things at once, and I should say what they are.

It is, first, a history. It moves from the Greek Hermetic texts of the second century to
Aleister Crowley's death in 1947, through Scottish lodge minutes, London taverns,
Parisian salons, Charleston degree rituals, Boston petitions, and a Victorian coroner's
library of two hundred and eighty-six occult books. I have tried to make it a history
you can see and hear, because the people in it were not abstractions and the rooms they
met in were real rooms.

It is, second, a training in method. From Chapter 5 onward the reader has a toolkit —
provenance, chronology, transmission, intertextuality, institutional continuity,
anachronism — and I use it in the open, chapter after chapter, letting some claims
survive and watching others come apart. By the end you should be able to evaluate a
historical claim about a secret tradition without me. That is the point. The subject is
infested with confident nonsense, and the antidote is not a list of correct answers; it
is a habit of asking *by what route, in what year, through whose hands*.

It is, third, an argument about a word. *Magic* is not a stable thing. In Iamblichus it
names something its practitioners insist is emphatically not magic. In Ficino it is
medicine. In Agrippa it is a philosophy of nature in three stories. By 1700 it has
become chiefly a term for what respectable knowledge is not. And then in 1854 a defrocked
French seminarian who had spent the 1840s writing socialist pamphlets picks the word up
again and gives it a meaning it had never quite had: an inner science of will,
imagination and symbol, aimed at the transformation of the operator. Within fifty years
that definition has conquered the field. Within sixty it has been carried into a lodge
in Charleston, South Carolina, largely without attribution, by a Confederate general
with a formidable library.

Which produces the possibility this book takes seriously without ever quite asserting:
that the deepest kinship between Freemasonry and magic is not spellcraft but
*engineering*. That both traditions treat ritual, symbol and sustained attention as
technologies for changing the person who uses them. If that is right, then the Victorian
occultists who looked at Freemasonry and saw a magical order were not simply deluded.
They were right about the mechanism and wrong about the ancestry. They found a working
machine and invented a pedigree for it, because in their century a machine without a
pedigree did not count as knowledge.

---

A word about what this book will not do.

It will not tell you that Freemasonry is secretly occult. It will not tell you that
Freemasonry has nothing to do with the history of magic. It will not disclose or invent
private ritual material; everything described here comes from sources that have been
publicly available for decades or centuries, and I have noted where jurisdictions differ
rather than pretending they do not. It will not diagnose the inner lives of men who died
before psychology existed. It will not assert that any magical operation works, or that
none does — that is not a historian's question, and the moment a historian starts
answering it he has stopped doing history. And it will not resolve every question it
raises. The final chapter before the epilogue is called "What Remains Unexplained," and
it is not a rhetorical flourish. There are twenty questions on that list. Several of them
I would give a great deal to answer.

What the book will do is follow the evidence into a room that people have been arguing
about since 1723, and describe what is actually in there.

It turns out to be more interesting than the treasure, and more interesting than the
disappointment. It is a piece of ritual engineering, three centuries old, that has
changed more lives than any of the ancient traditions it stands accused of stealing
from — and that has never once needed to be ancient in order to be real.

We begin, as the evidence does, with a man writing in his diary on a Friday afternoon in
October 1646, about something he had just done and never explained.

---

# PART I — THE DOOR

# CHAPTER ONE
## The Lodge at Midnight

It was not midnight.

That is the first thing to get out of the way, because almost everything written about
that afternoon has wanted it to be midnight, and it was not. The record is specific, and
the record says half past four.

Elias Ashmole kept a diary the way some men keep accounts. He noted the hour he was
born, the hours of his marriages, the hours at which he took physic and the hours at
which it worked. He was an astrologer, and to an astrologer a time is not a detail; it
is the thing that makes an event interpretable. So when he recorded what happened to him
at Warrington in Lancashire on 16 October 1646, he recorded the minute:

> 4H. 30' P.M. I was made a Free Mason at Warrington in Lancashire, with Coll: Henry
> Mainwaring of Karincham in Cheshire.

Then, as was his habit, he listed the men who were present: Richard Penket the Warden,
James Collier, Richard Sankey, Henry Littler, John Ellam, Richard Ellam, and Hugh
Brewer.[^1]

That is the entry. Eight names, a place, a date, and a time. It is, by a wide margin,
the best-documented moment in the early history of English Freemasonry, and it tells us
almost nothing.

---

Half past four on the sixteenth of October in the north of England: the light is going.
Not gone — the sun would set within the hour — but low and thin and coming in sideways,
the particular gray-gold of a Lancashire autumn afternoon. It had been a wet month. The
Mersey, which runs past Warrington on its way to the sea, would have been high.

I am reconstructing, and I want to flag that I am reconstructing, because this book is
going to ask the reader to distinguish between what a source says and what a writer has
supplied, and it would be dishonest to start by blurring the line myself. The diary does
not tell us what the weather was, or which building they met in, or whether there were
candles. It tells us the hour, and the hour tells us the light was failing. Everything
else in that paragraph is inference from a latitude and a date.

What we can say with confidence is stranger than the atmosphere.

Warrington in 1646 was a town of perhaps two thousand people. It had a bridge, a market,
and a recent memory of being fought over — it had been stormed and besieged during the
civil war, and the war, though nobody could be quite sure of it yet, had just ended.
Oxford had surrendered to Parliament in June. The King was in Scottish custody. The
country was in that peculiar suspended condition of a war that has stopped without
anything being settled.

What Warrington did not have, so far as anyone has been able to establish, was a stone
building trade of any consequence. There was no cathedral going up, no castle under
repair, no body of professional masons who would have needed a lodge in the original
sense of the word — a hut or shed beside a building site, where the men who cut stone
ate, slept, stored their tools, and controlled entry to their trade.

And the seven men listed in Ashmole's diary were not masons. They have been traced.
They were local gentry and men of modest property: a Penket, a Sankey, two Ellams,
names that belong to Warrington's landowning and mercantile families rather than to its
building trades.[^2] The one possible exception is Hugh Brewer, who may have been a
working man. The rest were not cutting stone, and neither was Ashmole, and neither was
the man made alongside him.

That man deserves a sentence of his own. Colonel Henry Mainwaring of Karincham had
fought in the war. So had Ashmole. They had fought on opposite sides. Mainwaring was a
Parliamentarian officer; Ashmole was a royalist who had held a commission in the King's
ordnance at Oxford and had been driven out of it by the surrender four months earlier.
They were, moreover, connected by marriage — Ashmole's wife was a Mainwaring.

So the scene, stripped of romance, is this. In a small Lancashire town, in the last light
of an October afternoon, a defeated royalist and a victorious parliamentarian officer
were admitted together into a society of men who were not stonemasons, in a place with
no stonemasonry to speak of, four months after a civil war that had killed a larger
proportion of the English population than the First World War would kill three centuries
later.

Nobody wrote down why.

---

This is the point at which most accounts of Elias Ashmole become confident, and it is
the point at which I want to slow down.

The temptation is very strong, and it comes in two flavours.

The first says: *look at the man*. Elias Ashmole was an alchemist. In 1652 he would edit
and publish the *Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum*, a collection of English alchemical
poetry that remains a serious scholarly landmark. He collected manuscripts of John Dee.
He cast horoscopes for himself and others with relentless diligence. He was fascinated by
the Rosicrucian manifestos, which had been published in Germany when he was a child, and
he copied out a letter of application to the Fraternity of the Rosy Cross — an
organization that, as we shall see, did not exist. He was, in short, precisely the kind
of seventeenth-century Englishman whom a modern reader expects to find in an esoteric
society. Therefore, the argument goes, Freemasonry in 1646 must have had something
esoteric in it. Why else would he join?

The second temptation says: *look at the war*. Here is a royalist and a parliamentarian,
admitted together, four months after the fighting stopped, into a society whose whole
apparatus — secrecy, obligation, brotherhood, a promise of mutual aid regardless of
faction — looks designed for exactly this moment. Therefore Freemasonry in 1646 must have
been a device for reconciliation, a neutral ground in a shattered country.

Both arguments are attractive. Both have been made by serious people. And neither is in
the diary.

Ashmole wrote thousands upon thousands of words about himself. He recorded his dreams,
his ailments, the sigils he cast, the exact hour at which a toothache began to ease. On
the subject of what happened to him at Warrington, and what he thought it was, and why
he wanted it, he wrote nothing at all. He recorded the fact with the same neutral
precision he gave to the weather.

And then — this is the detail that ought to unsettle anyone who wants a tidy explanation
— he seems to have done nothing with it for thirty-five years. The next time a Masonic
lodge appears in his diary is March 1682, in London, when he attended a meeting at
Masons' Hall and noted that he was the senior Fellow present, since it had been
thirty-five years since his admission.[^3] Between those two entries, over more than
three decades in which he wrote almost daily, there is silence.

Whatever Freemasonry was to Elias Ashmole, it was not a preoccupation. It was something
he did once, in the failing light of an October afternoon, and then remembered thirty-
five years later with what reads like mild antiquarian satisfaction.

---

So we have a question, and it is worth being precise about its shape, because the shape
of this question is the shape of the whole book.

We do not have the question *what secret did Ashmole learn?* There is no evidence he
learned a secret, and the earliest surviving descriptions of what a candidate learned in
a seventeenth-century lodge — Scottish catechisms written down fifty years later, which
we will look at closely — describe a word, a handgrip, and a short set of questions and
answers. Useful for recognizing a fellow craftsman on a strange building site. Not a
revelation.

We have, instead, a harder and more interesting question: *why did men who had nothing
to do with the building trade want to be admitted to the building trade's private
institution?*

Because that is what was happening, all over Britain, in the middle of the seventeenth
century. In Scotland, where the records are far better, non-operative members — men
described in the minutes as "gentlemen" — begin appearing in lodges of working masons
from 1600 onward. In England the evidence is thinner and later, but by the 1640s men
like Ashmole are being "accepted" into a craft they will never practice. Something about
the masons' lodge — its oath, its privacy, its brotherhood, its old written legends
tracing the craft back through Euclid to the building of Solomon's Temple — was
attractive to men who had no professional use for it at all.

That attraction is the engine of everything that follows in this book. Over the next
three hundred pages we will watch it pull in, successively: Scottish lairds and English
antiquaries; London Newtonians and Parisian aristocrats; a Bordeaux merchant conducting
angelic invocations; Prussian alchemists; a free Black leatherworker in Boston; a
Confederate general with a library of eight thousand volumes; a Victorian coroner; and
a poet who told the world he was the Beast of Revelation. They all wanted in, and they
all wanted in for different reasons, and the institution accommodated every one of them
without ever quite telling any of them what it meant.

---

There is a version of this book that answers the Ashmole question. It would say: he
joined because the lodge preserved the Hermetic and alchemical wisdom he had spent his
life pursuing, and he recognized it. It would note that he was an alchemist and a
Freemason, and treat the conjunction as a channel.

I am not going to write that sentence, and the reason is worth stating once, clearly,
here at the start, because it will govern fifty subsequent chapters.

Elias Ashmole was an alchemist and a Freemason. That is a documented fact about a man.
It is not a documented fact about an institution. It tells us that one person could
contain both interests; it does not tell us that the two things were connected, any more
than a modern astronomer who plays the cello demonstrates a link between astronomy and
chamber music. To get from *a man held both* to *the two were joined*, you need
something else: a text, a transmission, a route. You need to be able to say by what
means, in what year, through whose hands.

Nobody has produced that for 1646. Not for want of looking.

What we have instead is an absence with a very precise shape — and absences of precise
shape are, for a historian, among the most useful objects in the world. They tell you
where the answer is not, which is the only reliable way of narrowing down where it
might be.

---

The light goes out of that Warrington room at about half past five. The eight men go
home. Ashmole writes his entry, in the private shorthand of a man who records
everything, and then does not mention the subject again until he is sixty-five years
old.

What he had joined was not ancient, not Egyptian, and not — in any sense he would have
recognized — magical. It was a small, strange, locally-run association with medieval
roots in a trade he did not practice, and it was in the middle of becoming something
else entirely.

To understand what it became, and why a hundred years later men would be performing
theurgic operations inside its structures, and two hundred years later would be
reconstructing the Kabbalah in its temples, we need to sort out three things first.

We need to know what the word *magic* has actually meant to the people who used it,
because it has not meant one thing, and almost every confusion in this subject begins
with the assumption that it has.

We need to know what was actually secret in a Masonic lodge — and why publishing the
secrets, repeatedly, in cheap print, over four decades, did nothing whatever to the
institution.

And we need a method: a way of telling, when somebody says *this is very old*, whether
it is.

Those are the next four chapters. Then we can go back to the beginning, which is not
Egypt, and not Jerusalem, and not a Templar commandery, but a set of Greek philosophical
dialogues written in Roman Egypt in the second century of the Christian era, which
Europe would spend three hundred years mistaking for something eleven centuries older.

---

---

# CHAPTER TWO
## What We Mean When We Say Magic

In the year 1509 a young German soldier-scholar of twenty-three sat down to write a book
that would make him famous, notorious, and eventually miserable. Its title was *De
occulta philosophia* — *On Occult Philosophy* — and it proposed that the universe was a
single connected body in which everything corresponded to everything else, that a
trained mind could read those correspondences, and that a person who understood them
could act.

Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa called this *magia*. He also called it the highest of the
sciences, "the most perfect and chief science, that sacred and sublimer kind of
philosophy," and he meant it. To him, magic was not a deviation from natural philosophy.
It was natural philosophy, completed: the study of nature in its lower story, of the
heavens in its middle, and of the divine in its upper, all at once.

Two hundred years later, an Englishman using the same word would have meant a village
woman muttering over a sick cow, and would have used it to mean *fraud*.

A hundred and fifty years after that, a Frenchman who had trained for the priesthood and
then written revolutionary pamphlets would pick the word up again and mean something
neither Agrippa nor the Englishman would have recognized: a science of the will, in which
the operator's own imagination was the instrument and the operator's own transformation
was the point.

And fifty years after *that*, a young Englishman with money and an appetite for scandal
would add a *k* to the end of the word to distinguish what he did from stage conjuring,
and define it as "the Science and Art of causing Change to occur in conformity with
Will" — a definition so capacious that it includes baking bread, and which he cheerfully
noted did include baking bread.

Four men, four centuries, one word, four incompatible meanings. This is the problem.

---

Nearly every confusion in the subject of this book begins here. People argue about
whether Freemasonry is "magical" without establishing what either party means, and so
they argue past each other indefinitely. The Mason hears *magic* and thinks: spells,
demons, superstition — no, obviously not. The critic hears *magic* and thinks: ritual,
secrecy, symbolism, invocation — well, obviously yes. Both are responding accurately to
the word as they have received it. Neither is describing anything historical.

So before we go any further, we need to take the word apart.

---

### The trouble with the category

Let me put the hardest point first, because it is the one most often skipped.

A significant body of modern scholarship holds that "magic" is not a description of
anything in the world at all — that it is, and has almost always been, a term of
exclusion. Randall Styers has traced in detail how the category was constructed in the
modern period precisely as the negative space against which religion and science defined
themselves: magic is what *they* do, over there, wrongly.[^1] Bernd-Christian Otto and
Michael Stausberg, surveying two centuries of attempts to define it, reach a similar
conclusion about the scholarly definitions themselves: most of them turn out, on
inspection, to encode the definer's assumptions about what proper religion looks
like.[^2]

There is real force in this. Watch the word travel through a single culture and you will
see it move with the politics rather than with the practice. The same operation is
*prayer* when the monastery does it and *sorcery* when the widow does it. The same
astrological medicine is *natural philosophy* when a Florentine priest publishes it under
a Medici's protection and *witchcraft* when a cunning-woman charges sixpence for it. The
word does not track a method. It tracks who is permitted.

If that were the whole story, the honest response would be to stop using the word.

But it is not quite the whole story, and the reason is important. Some people called
themselves magicians. Agrippa did, deliberately and at length, and staked a reputation on
it. Éliphas Lévi did. Aleister Crowley built an entire public identity on it. When a
practitioner adopts the label, the label stops being purely an accusation and becomes,
whatever else it is, a piece of evidence about what that person thought they were doing.

So the working rule of this book is: *magic* is not a thing in the world. It is a word
people used, and the history of what they meant by it is a large part of the history we
are actually telling. Wherever the word appears here it will be qualified, and wherever
somebody in this book uses it, I will try to say what they meant.

---

### The varieties, and what distinguishes them

Here is the vocabulary, assembled roughly as an early modern reader would have
understood it. It is worth a little patience, because these distinctions do real work
later.

**Natural magic** is the manipulation of hidden properties in physical things — the
*occult* qualities, in the strict sense of *hidden*, not *sinister*. Why does a magnet
attract iron? Why does this herb reduce fever and that one kill? Before the mechanical
philosophy of the seventeenth century, these were genuinely mysterious, and the tradition
that studied them was called natural magic. Much of it is simply pre-modern science, and
a good deal of it worked.

**Celestial** or **astral magic** adds the heavens: the doctrine that the planets shape
terrestrial things, and that a practitioner can draw down a favorable influence by
assembling the right materials at the right time. Marsilio Ficino's medical advice to
melancholy scholars — sunlight, gold, music in the Solar mode — is astral magic, and he
published it under a cardinal's nose.

**Sympathetic magic** is the underlying logic of both: like affects like, and things once
in contact remain connected. The category is a nineteenth-century anthropologist's, not a
Renaissance practitioner's, and should be used carefully.

**Talismanic magic** is astral magic made portable: an image engraved on the correct
metal, at the correct hour, to hold an influence.

**Ceremonial magic** is where the temperature changes. Here the operator does not
manipulate properties but addresses *persons* — spirits, angels, intelligences, demons —
with names, seals, circles, and formulae. This is the magic of the medieval grimoires, of
the *Key of Solomon*, of John Dee and Edward Kelley kneeling at a table in the 1580s
trying to get the angels to speak clearly. It is also the direct ancestor of what the
Golden Dawn would do in a rented room in London three hundred years later.

**Theurgy** — literally "god-working" — is the most important term in this chapter and
the most frequently mangled. In the third and fourth centuries, the Neoplatonist
Iamblichus argued that ritual operations could raise the soul toward the divine, and he
was emphatic that this was *not* the low sorcery of the marketplace. Theurgy is
performed, it is ritual, it uses names and materials — and its practitioners spent
considerable energy insisting it was categorically different from magic. That insistence
is itself a permanent feature of this history. Almost everyone in this book who does
something we would call magic denies that it is magic. It is worth noticing how often,
and why.

**Thaumaturgy** is wonder-working: the production of visible marvels. Where theurgy aims
at the operator's ascent, thaumaturgy aims at the spectator's astonishment.

**Alchemy** and **astrology** sit adjacent to all of this, sometimes inside it and
sometimes proudly outside it. Both were, for most of their history, technical disciplines
with their own literatures, apprenticeships and standards. Neither should be flattened
into "magic."

And then there are the modern constructions. **Occultism** is a coinage of the
mid-nineteenth century, not an ancient category. **Magick** with a *k* is Crowley's, from
around 1904. **Hermetic Qabalah** is a Victorian and Edwardian system that shares a name,
and a fraction of a vocabulary, with Jewish Kabbalah. Using any of these terms for a
period before it existed is not shorthand. It is an anachronism, and anachronism is the
single most productive source of error in this entire field.

---

### The four sentences

There is one more piece of equipment to install before we go on, and it is the most
important in the book.

When somebody says "magic works," they may be saying any of five completely different
things. These get confused constantly, including by careful people, and once they are
confused an argument can run for a century without anyone noticing that the two sides are
not discussing the same proposition.

**The historical claim.** *Practitioners believed X.* This is a statement about the
contents of people's minds in the past, and it is established by ordinary historical
evidence — their books, their letters, their rituals. It carries no implication whatever
about whether X is true. When this book says that eighteenth-century Masons believed
their Craft descended from Solomon's Temple, that is a historical claim, and it is
straightforwardly demonstrable.

**The phenomenological claim.** *Practitioners reported experiencing X.* Also a
historical statement, but about experience rather than belief, and it requires
first-person testimony. When a Golden Dawn initiate wrote that during the Vault ceremony
she felt something she could not name, that report is evidence of the report. It is not
evidence of a cause.

**The psychological claim.** *A ritual of this kind may produce effect Y in participants.*
This is a modern theoretical statement, hedged, and it must never be turned into a
diagnosis of a specific dead individual. We can say what the literature on ritual and
memory suggests. We cannot say what happened in the mind of Elias Ashmole at half past
four on 16 October 1646, and any book that tells you otherwise is making it up.

**The sociological claim.** *A ritual of this kind creates social structure Z.* This is
often the best-evidenced of the five, because it leaves records: membership lists,
charity accounts, business partnerships, marriage patterns, political alignments. Much of
what Freemasonry demonstrably *did* in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries belongs
here.

**The metaphysical claim.** *A supernatural mechanism objectively exists and operates.*
This is not a historian's claim to make. It is not that I think it is false; it is that
nothing in my evidence could establish it either way. **The narrator of this book will
never assert one, in either direction.**

Those five sentences are the grammar of everything that follows. The single most common
failure in the literature on Freemasonry and the occult — in the credulous books and the
debunking books equally — is the silent slide from one to another: from *they believed*
to *they experienced* to *it happened*. Watch for it. I have tried very hard not to do
it, and where I have come close I would rather the reader caught me than not.

---

### Why this matters for a lodge

Return, with this equipment, to the room in Warrington.

Was what happened to Elias Ashmole magic?

The question now decomposes into several answerable ones. Did the ceremony involve the
manipulation of occult natural properties? No evidence of it. Did it address spirits by
name? No evidence of it — and, as we shall see, the earliest surviving ritual texts are
conspicuously free of anything of the kind. Did it involve ritual, secrecy, symbolic
objects, an oath, and a staged transition from ignorance to knowledge? Yes,
unambiguously, on the evidence of the catechisms written down within living memory.

So: not ceremonial magic. Not theurgy. Not astral or talismanic operation. But a
structured ritual technology aimed at transforming a person's status, and — this is the
part that will matter enormously later — built on a symbolic architecture of darkness and
light, death and raising, that would prove capable of holding almost any content poured
into it.

Agrippa would not have called that magic. Lévi, three hundred years later, absolutely
would have — and did. Crowley's definition includes it without effort.

Which tells us something about Freemasonry, and rather more about the word.

The word did not stay still. Between the 1640s and the 1850s it changed its meaning
under everybody's feet, moving from *the manipulation of nature's hidden properties* to
*the transformation of the operator through will, symbol and imagination*. And a
Freemasonry that was not magic under the first definition became, without altering a
single one of its rituals, magic under the second.

That migration is one of the two or three most important events in this book, and we will
watch it happen in Part VI, in the hands of a French ex-seminarian who had spent his
twenties in prison for writing about the poor.

But it did not happen out of nothing. The materials Lévi built with had been lying around
Europe for three centuries, and to understand where he got them we will have to go back
past the lodge entirely — past Warrington, past Scotland, past the whole institution of
Freemasonry — into a set of Greek dialogues that Europe read as ancient Egyptian
scripture for a hundred and forty-three years after a Swiss scholar had proved they were
nothing of the kind.

First, though, the secret. Because there is a widespread assumption that a society which
keeps secrets must have something worth keeping, and the history of Freemasonry offers an
unusually clean test of that assumption. The secrets were published — completely,
repeatedly, cheaply, and over four decades — and nothing happened at all.

That is worth understanding before we go any further.

---

[^2]: Bernd-Christian Otto and Michael Stausberg, eds., *Defining Magic: A Reader*

---

# CHAPTER THREE
## The Problem of the Secret

In 1730 a London printer issued a pamphlet of about thirty pages, priced at sixpence,
called *Masonry Dissected*. Its author was a man named Samuel Prichard, who described
himself on the title page as a "late Member of a Constituted Lodge," and who had done
something that ought, by every reasonable expectation, to have destroyed Freemasonry.

He printed the secrets.

Not some of them. All of them, as far as anyone can tell: the questions and answers of
the catechism, the words, the grips, the signs, the shape of the ceremony, and — for the
first time in print — the legend of the third degree, the story of the murdered architect
of Solomon's Temple. Prichard swore an affidavit before a magistrate that it was a "true
and genuine copy." It sold enormously. It went through three editions in under a
fortnight and was still being reprinted, pirated and translated decades later.[^1]

Freemasonry's response was to grow.

Over the following century the institution expanded across Europe, the Americas, and
eventually most of the inhabited world, acquired kings and emperors as members, built
enormous buildings, and became the model for hundreds of imitators. The secrets remained
available in any bookshop the entire time. They are available now, free, in ten seconds,
to anyone reading this sentence with a phone nearby.

Something is obviously wrong with our idea of what the secret was.

---

### The four things "secret" can mean

It helps to separate them.

**Informational secrecy** is the ordinary kind: a fact that is concealed because knowing
it confers advantage. A safe combination. A troop movement. The identity of a source.
This kind of secret is destroyed by publication, permanently and completely.

**Functional secrecy** is a fact concealed because concealment performs a job. The
original Masonic secrets were largely of this type. In the medieval and early modern
building trades, a mason arriving at a strange site needed to demonstrate that he was
what he claimed to be — trained, entitled, entered into the craft. A word and a grip did
that. The Scottish catechisms of the 1690s, our earliest ritual evidence, are substantially
concerned with exactly this: how to recognize a brother, what to say, what to answer.[^2]
This kind of secret is damaged by publication, but only in proportion to how many
impostors bother to read the pamphlet.

**Performative secrecy** is different in kind. Here the concealment is not protecting
information; it is *doing something* to the participants. A candidate who is told he
will learn something, made to wait, made to swear, and then told it, has had an
experience. The content is almost irrelevant to the experience. Anyone who has watched a
child be told a very ordinary fact under conditions of great ceremony knows that the
ceremony, not the fact, is what lands. This kind of secret is not damaged by publication
at all, because reading it in a pamphlet is not the same event as receiving it in a room.

**Boundary secrecy** is what the sociologist Georg Simmel described in 1906, in an essay
that remains the best short thing ever written on the subject. Simmel's insight was that
the secret society's secret is frequently *the fact of having one*. The content matters
less than the line it draws. A group with a boundary has an inside; people on the inside
are bound to each other by their shared position relative to the line; and the line is
maintained not by the difficulty of learning the secret but by the ritual of having been
admitted to it.[^3]

Prichard destroyed the first kind and dented the second. He could not touch the third or
the fourth, and those were the ones carrying the weight.

---

### What was actually secret

We can be fairly precise about this, because we have the texts.

The earliest surviving Masonic catechism, the Edinburgh Register House Manuscript, was
written down in 1696. It is short. It describes two degrees — Entered Apprentice and
Fellow Craft — and its contents are: a set of questions and answers about the lodge, its
form, its officers and its furnishings; a word; a sign; and a manner of giving them. Two
further Scottish texts from within about twenty years, the Chetwode Crawley and Kevan
manuscripts, agree closely.[^4]

That is the material. A word, a sign, some questions. It is not a philosophy. It is not
a cosmology. There is no doctrine in it, no theology beyond the ordinary piety of the
period, nothing that could reasonably be described as an esoteric teaching.

What there is, and what is easy to miss because we are looking for content, is
*structure*. The catechism is not an essay; it is a call and response, learned by heart,
performed between two people, in a specific order, in a specified space. The lodge is
described with an orientation and furniture. There is a form of admission. There is an
oath. There are consequences for breaking it, described in language of memorable
physical violence — and we should note in passing that the bloodcurdling penalties in the
Masonic obligations, which have provided anti-Masonic writers with three centuries of
excellent material, are symbolic formulae of a type found in other trade and legal oaths
of the period, and were never enforced by anybody.

So: what was secret was not a body of knowledge. It was a *procedure*, and membership of
the group that performed it.

This has an important consequence for everything that follows. When eighteenth- and
nineteenth-century writers began asserting that Freemasonry preserved ancient wisdom,
they were not lying about something they had seen. They were looking at a structure with
no specified content and supplying the content themselves. The lodge's silence about what
its symbols meant was not a cover for a hidden teaching. It was a vacancy — and vacancies
attract furniture.

---

### Why concealment produces authority

There is a mechanism here worth stating plainly, because it recurs in every chapter of
this book.

Concealment generates three things almost automatically.

It generates **value**. Anything withheld acquires worth in proportion to the effort
required to obtain it. A candidate who has waited, paid, been proposed, been balloted,
been blindfolded and been sworn will not conclude that what he has received is trivial.
He cannot conclude that, without concluding something unflattering about himself. This is
not manipulation; it is simply how people work, and it operates identically in
universities, guilds, professions and armies.

It generates **authority**. If I possess something you do not, and the only route to it
runs through me, I have a form of power that requires no coercion at all. Every graded
system in this book — Masonic, Rosicrucian, theosophical, magical — runs on this, and
every one of them, without exception, eventually suffers a crisis in which somebody
demands to know who is at the top and on what warrant.

And it generates **mythology**, which is the one that matters most here. An opaque
institution is a screen, and people project onto screens. What they project depends
entirely on what they already fear or hope. The anti-Masonic writer of 1798 looked at the
lodge's silence and saw revolutionary conspiracy. The Catholic controversialist of 1884
looked at the same silence and saw devil-worship. The occultist of 1888 looked at it and
saw a surviving initiatic tradition. The modern internet looks at it and sees a global
elite. In each case, the *content* of the projection tells you about the projector and
nothing about the lodge — and in each case the projection was possible only because the
institution declined to say what it meant.

Freemasonry, in other words, manufactured its own mythology as a byproduct of its
procedure. Nobody planned this. It is a consequence of the design.

---

### The problem this creates for a historian

Now the awkward part.

Secrecy does not only produce mythology. It produces holes in the evidence, and those
holes have a particular and treacherous property: they are shaped exactly like the thing
you are looking for.

If a tradition conceals its practices, then the absence of documentation for a practice
is not, by itself, evidence that the practice did not occur. That is a genuine
methodological difficulty and I do not want to wave it away. It is the strongest card in
the hand of anyone arguing for a hidden transmission, and it deserves a serious answer.

The answer has three parts.

First, **secrecy is never total, and it leaks in predictable directions**. Organizations
keep minutes. Members write letters. Rituals must be taught, which means they must be
transmitted, which means they exist somewhere in a recoverable form. Disgruntled members
publish. Governments raid. Widows sell papers. The eighteenth-century Bavarian Illuminati
were as secretive an organization as this history contains, and we possess their internal
correspondence because the Bavarian state seized it and printed it in 1787. When an
organization genuinely existed and genuinely operated, it almost always left a trail.
*Total* absence of evidence, over centuries, across multiple countries, is itself a fact
requiring explanation.

Second, **the claim of secrecy is testable in its own right**. If an order asserts that
it has transmitted a teaching in secret since antiquity, we cannot examine the secret —
but we can examine the assertion. When was it first made? By whom? What did they gain?
Did anyone contradict them at the time? Does the teaching contain anything that could
not have existed at the claimed date? That last question is the most powerful tool in the
book, and we will use it repeatedly. A symbol that turns on the English letter G cannot
be Egyptian. A Kabbalistic system that assigns tarot trumps to the paths of the Tree of
Life cannot predate the tarot.

Third, **the burden sits where it always sits**. A claim that something was transmitted
is a positive claim about the past. Positive claims require evidence. "You cannot prove
it did not happen in secret" is not an argument; it is the abandonment of argument, and
it would license anything whatever.

---

### The test case

Which brings us back to Prichard, and to the four decades that followed him.

Between 1723 and 1762 the Masonic secrets were published in English at least a dozen
times and in French at least as often. *A Mason's Examination* appeared in a London
newspaper in 1723. *The Grand Mystery of Free-Masons Discover'd* followed in 1724.
Prichard's *Masonry Dissected* in 1730 was the most complete and the most successful. In
France, the Abbé Pérau's *Le Secret des Francs-Maçons* appeared in 1742 and was itself a
bestseller. *Three Distinct Knocks* came in 1760, *Jachin and Boaz* in 1762 — the latter
going through edition after edition for the rest of the century.[^5]

These were not obscure documents. They were cheap, popular, widely translated, and
frequently accurate. Anyone in Europe who wanted to know what happened in a Masonic lodge
could find out for the price of a bottle of wine, at any point after 1730.

And Freemasonry's membership rose continuously through the entire period.

There is only one conclusion available. Whatever people were joining for, it was not the
information. They could have had the information for sixpence and stayed home. They
wanted the room, the ritual, the oath and the company — which is to say, they wanted
precisely the parts that a pamphlet cannot supply.

That is a fact about Freemasonry. It is also, as we will see in Part VII, a fact about
ritual in general, and it explains a great deal about why the form proved so durable and
so portable.

But the exposures are useful to us in a second way, and it is the reason this book can be
written at all. Because the secrets were published, we know what the ritual contained at
specific dates. We can watch the third degree appear. We can watch symbols enter and
others fall away. We can date things.

That capacity — to say *this element is present in 1730 and absent in 1723* — is the
difference between history and speculation in this field. In the next chapter we will use
it, and reconstruct, from those published sources, what it was actually like to be
admitted.

---

# CHAPTER FOUR
## The Night of the Initiation

What follows is a reconstruction, and I want the reader to know exactly what kind.

Every element in it is drawn from documents that were published between 1723 and 1762 —
the exposures described in the last chapter — and from the Scottish catechisms of the
1690s. Nothing is invented. But no single source describes a whole evening, the sources
disagree with one another in places, and practice varied by lodge, by country and by
decade. What I have assembled is a composite: a plausible English ceremony of the middle
of the eighteenth century, built from published evidence, in which every individual
component is attested somewhere and the sequence is my own arrangement.

It is not a transcript. No such transcript exists. And it is not the ritual of any lodge
working today, in any jurisdiction, anywhere — those have changed considerably, and they
differ from each other more than most outsiders imagine.

With that said.

---

### Waiting

The first thing is the waiting.

He has been proposed by someone who knows him, and the lodge has voted on him in his
absence. He has been told the date. He arrives at a tavern — because in the 1740s that is
where lodges met, in an upstairs room hired for the evening, with the innkeeper's boy
guarding the stair — and he is put in a small room by himself, and the door is shut.

He can hear them. That is part of it. Muffled voices, the scrape of chairs, laughter at
something, then not laughter. Someone knocks on something. The sound of a group of men
doing something orderly on the other side of a door, while you sit alone in your
shirtsleeves, is doing work on you before anybody has said a word.

He is not in his own clothes, or not entirely. He has been asked to remove metal — coins,
buckles, anything of value — and to loosen or remove certain garments. His left breast is
bare. One knee is bare. One foot is unshod, or partly so. Accounts differ on the details
and the details differ by lodge, but the principle is constant across all of them: he is
*not properly dressed*, and he knows it, and he is about to be taken somewhere in that
condition.

Then he is blindfolded. A "hoodwink," the sources call it.

And then a cord is put around his neck: the cable-tow.

---

### The door

Consider what has been arranged, purely as a matter of engineering.

He cannot see. He is partly undressed in a room full of clothed men. He has a rope around
his neck, held by someone else. He has been stripped of money and valuables, which means
he has nothing to offer and nothing to defend. He does not know what is about to happen.
And he has come here voluntarily, at the invitation of a friend, which means he cannot
easily interpret his own fear as sensible.

Every one of those conditions is a documented element of the eighteenth-century
ceremony, and every one of them is a well-understood instrument for producing heightened
attention. Whoever assembled this — and we will spend Part III trying to work out who —
either understood that very well or arrived at it by a long process of practical
refinement. I incline to the second, but it makes little difference to the effect.

Somebody knocks on the door on his behalf. The door opens. A voice asks who comes here.

He does not answer; his guide answers for him. He is described to the room in the third
person while standing in it — a poor candidate in a state of darkness, who comes of his
own free will, properly prepared, humbly soliciting.

And then, in most of the printed versions, something sharp is presented to his naked
breast. A compass point, or a sword. He cannot see it. He can feel it.

The question he is asked, at that moment, is whether he comes of his own free will.

He says yes. Of course he says yes. But he says it with a point against his chest, in the
dark, and the *yes* he gives is not the same yes he gave his friend over dinner three
weeks earlier.

---

### The journey

He is led around the room. Not once — three times, or more, depending on the degree and
the working, always in the same direction, always with the same pauses. At each cardinal
point something happens: a question, a knock, a challenge.

This is the part that looks most arbitrary to a modern reader and is in fact the most
consequential.

He cannot see. Therefore the only map he is acquiring is a map of movement — turns,
distances, the direction of voices, the sound of his own bare foot on boards. He is
learning the room with his body before he ever learns it with his eyes. When the blindfold
finally comes off, the space will already be familiar in a way he cannot account for, and
the sensation of recognizing a place he has never seen is itself part of what the ceremony
produces.

He is also, without being told so, being taught that the room has an order. There is an
east and a west. There are stations. There are people at them with authority. He learns
the shape of the lodge's hierarchy by walking it, in the dark, before anyone explains it.

---

### The obligation

He is brought to the center and made to kneel.

The posture is specified — one knee bare and bent, one foot in a particular position, the
hands placed on an open book with a square and a pair of compasses laid upon it. He
cannot see any of this. It is arranged for him.

And then he repeats, phrase by phrase after the Master, an oath.

He swears to conceal and never reveal. He swears it under penalties that are described in
graphic anatomical detail, and which, as noted in the last chapter, nobody has ever
carried out and which belong to a recognizable family of early-modern oath formulae. He
promises to aid a brother. He promises not to wrong one.

Then he kisses the book.

I want to note something about this moment that the exposures cannot capture and that
ritual theory can. He has just made a serious promise, aloud, in a room full of witnesses,
while kneeling, blindfolded, in a state of ritual undress, with his hands on a Bible.
Whatever he thinks about the content of the promise, the *act* of making it under those
conditions is not easily reversible. He has committed himself with his voice and his body.
People do not readily walk that back.

Arnold van Gennep, writing in 1909 about rites of passage in dozens of cultures, called
this phase *liminality* — from *limen*, a threshold. The candidate is no longer what he
was and not yet what he will be. Victor Turner, half a century later, observed that
people in this condition are characteristically stripped of status, made equal to one
another, and unusually open to whatever the ritual is about to give them.[^1]

I offer those two names as a lens, not as a proof, and I want to be careful here. Van
Gennep and Turner were describing other material, and neither had eighteenth-century
London in mind. They give us a vocabulary for what this design does. They do not license
us to say what any particular man felt. I do not know what the man kneeling on those
boards felt. Nobody does, because he did not write it down — and the very few initiates
who did write about it afterward tended to write in the formulae the ritual had just
taught them, which is a problem of its own.

---

### Light

And then, on a word from the Master, the blindfold comes off.

Everything about the preceding forty minutes has been arranged so that this moment lands.
He has been in the dark for the better part of an hour. The room is lit by candles.
There are men standing around him, in aprons, in an arrangement he half recognizes
because he has walked it. In front of him is the book, and on the book a square and a
pair of compasses.

And he is told — the phrasing varies, but the substance is constant — that these are the
lights by which he is to work: one to guide his faith, one to measure his actions, one to
keep his desires within bounds.

That is the secret. That is what an hour of darkness, a rope, a blade at the chest and a
bloodcurdling oath have been protecting.

It is, viewed one way, absurdly slight. A book and two draftsman's tools and a piece of
moral advice that any preacher would give for free.

Viewed another way, it is the entire mechanism of this book in a single image. The
information is nothing. The information was never the point. What the ceremony has done
is take an ordinary moral commonplace and make it *unforgettable*, by arranging for it to
arrive at the end of an hour engineered to produce maximum receptivity, in a room the
candidate has learned with his body, from men to whom he has just sworn an oath, at the
instant his sight is restored.

He will remember that square and those compasses for the rest of his life. He will see
them on buildings and on gravestones and on watch-fobs and they will mean something to
him that they do not mean to the person beside him. Fifty years later he will be able to
describe the moment the blindfold came off.

That is not information transfer. That is something else, and the rest of this book is an
attempt to work out what to call it.

---

### What is not there

Before leaving the room, it is worth listing what a reader arriving from the conspiracy
literature might expect to find and does not.

There is no invocation of any spirit, angel or demon. There is no magic circle. There are
no barbarous names of power, no planetary hours, no talismans, no seals. There is no
Kabbalah. There is nothing Egyptian. There is no reference to the Knights Templar. There
is nothing that a Christian clergyman of the period would have found doctrinally alarming,
and a good many Christian clergymen of the period were members.

The furniture is a Bible, a square, and a pair of compasses. The vocabulary is the
building trade and the Old Testament. The moral content would not embarrass a
schoolmaster.

And yet the *structure* — darkness, threshold, ordeal, oath, revelation, new status — is
the structure that will be picked up, a hundred and fifty years later, by men who will
fill it with invocation, planetary hours, Hebrew letters, Egyptian god-names and every
grimoire they can lay hands on.

They did not find that content in the lodge. They brought it. The lodge gave them
somewhere to put it.

---

### A caution, stated once

I have described this ceremony at length because the reader cannot follow the rest of the
argument without knowing what a Masonic initiation actually consisted of. But I want to
repeat the warning I began with, in stronger terms.

This is a reconstruction from eighteenth-century printed sources. It is not a description
of any modern working. Present-day rituals differ substantially between England,
Scotland, Ireland, France, the American jurisdictions, and everywhere else — and the
differences are not trivial. Some elements described above have been softened, some
removed, some were never used in some countries at all. A Mason reading this chapter may
recognize a great deal and may also find things that have never formed part of his
experience. Both reactions would be correct.

The point of the exercise is not to expose anything. There is nothing here that has not
been in print for two hundred and sixty years. The point is to establish, from the
documentary record, what kind of machine we are dealing with — because in about twelve
chapters' time we are going to watch people start rebuilding it, and we need to know what
they were working from.

Which leaves one more piece of equipment to install. We know what the ceremony was. We
know what was secret and what was not. What we do not yet have is a way of testing the
enormous claims that would later be made about where all this came from.

That is the next chapter, and it is the most useful thing in the book.

---

# CHAPTER FIVE
## How to Tell a Tradition from a Story

In 1614 a Swiss scholar named Isaac Casaubon, working in London on a commission from
King James, published a demolition.

He had been reading the *Corpus Hermeticum*: a body of Greek philosophical dialogues
attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, an Egyptian sage whom Renaissance Europe believed had
lived in the time of Moses, or possibly before him. For a hundred and forty years these
texts had been read as the oldest theology in the world, a pagan revelation that
anticipated Christianity, evidence that God had spoken to Egypt as well as to Israel.
Marsilio Ficino had interrupted his translation of Plato to render them into Latin first,
because his patron judged them more urgent. They had reshaped European thought about
nature, the soul, magic and the antiquity of wisdom.

Casaubon read them in Greek and noticed that they were not old.

His argument was philological and it was devastating. The vocabulary was wrong: these
dialogues used Greek words that did not exist in the archaic period. The style was wrong:
it was the style of late antique philosophical prose, not of anything from the second
millennium before Christ. The content was wrong: the texts quoted or paraphrased Plato,
which a contemporary of Moses could not have done, and showed the marks of Jewish and
Christian ideas. And the transmission was wrong: there was no manuscript tradition
carrying these texts back into deep Egyptian antiquity, only Greek copies of the Roman
imperial period.

His conclusion — which no competent scholar has ever overturned, and which modern
editors state as a matter of course — was that the *Corpus Hermeticum* was written in
Greek, in Roman Egypt, somewhere between the first and third centuries of the Christian
era.[^1]

Not ancient. Contemporary with the Gospels.

---

Casaubon's demonstration is the founding case study of this book, and not because of what
it proved. It is the founding case because of what happened next, which was very nearly
nothing.

The Hermetic texts went on being read as ancient wisdom. They went on being cited as
Egyptian. Two hundred and fifty years later, in 1871, Albert Pike would be building
elaborate arguments on the antiquity of the Hermetic tradition. Two hundred and seventy
years later the founders of the Golden Dawn would name their order after it. Today you
can buy a book, published this year, that describes the *Corpus Hermeticum* as
pre-Mosaic.

A tradition can survive the destruction of its own credentials, indefinitely, if enough
people need it. That is a fact about human institutions and it is not a criticism of
anybody in particular. But it means that a historian who wants to know whether a claimed
transmission actually occurred cannot rely on how widely it is believed, how old the
belief is, how sincere the believers are, or how much sense it makes. The belief and the
transmission are separate objects, and they must be investigated separately.

This chapter is about how.

---

### The six questions

Here is the apparatus. It is not original to me — it is standard historical method,
assembled for this particular job — and it consists of six questions, applied in order.
Whenever this book confronts a claim that something old was carried into something newer,
these are the questions it asks.

**1. Provenance. Where is the physical object?**

Not "where is the tradition said to have come from" — where is the manuscript, the
letter, the minute book, the printed edition? Who has held it since? What is its
catalog number? A claim supported by a document nobody can point to is not supported.

This question alone disposes of a startling proportion of the literature. The Golden
Dawn's authority rested on correspondence with a German adept named Anna Sprengel. Where
are the letters? They were produced, and examined, and they are wrong — the German is
poor in ways a German adept's would not be, and the addresses do not check out.[^2] The
Rite of Strict Observance rested on the authority of Unknown Superiors. Where is the
warrant? Baron von Hund spent thirty years being asked and never produced one.

**2. Chronology. Does the timeline permit it?**

Astonishingly often, it does not. This is the cheapest test available and it is skipped
constantly. If a claim requires an organization to have influenced something that
happened before the organization existed, the claim is finished, and no amount of
symbolic resonance will repair it.

**3. Transmission. Can you name the route?**

A person, a text, a translation, a meeting, a purchase, a theft — something. This is the
question this book asks most often, because it is the one the literature most reliably
cannot answer. *Availability is not transmission.* That Agrippa's *De occulta philosophia*
was in print and purchasable in seventeenth-century England tells us an English Mason
*could* have read it. It does not tell us that one did, and it certainly does not tell us
that what he read entered the ritual.

The corrective method here is one I will use repeatedly, and I take it from the
historian Alison Butler, who applied it to the Golden Dawn. Instead of asking what ideas
were in the air, she reconstructed what books were physically on the shelves — the actual
catalogd contents of the founders' libraries.[^3] That is transmission evidence. Ideas
in the air are not.

**4. Intertextuality. Does the later source use the earlier one?**

Resemblance is weak. Quotation is strong. Paraphrase is strong. Shared idiosyncratic
error is strongest of all — if two texts make the same odd mistake, one of them copied
the other.

This is the test that settled the question of Albert Pike's sources. For a century it was
possible to argue about how much of *Morals and Dogma* was Pike's own thought. Then
Arturo de Hoyos went through it line by line against the books Pike had been reading, and
demonstrated that large tracts of it are unattributed transcription, substantially from
Éliphas Lévi.[^4] That is not an opinion about influence. That is a collation.

**5. Institutional continuity. Did the organization actually persist?**

Organizations that exist do things. They collect dues, keep lists, rent rooms, quarrel,
split, and leave paper. A claim of unbroken institutional descent across a gap of
centuries, with no dues, no lists, no rooms and no quarrels, is a claim that an
organization existed in a manner unlike every other organization in recorded history.

The Templars were suppressed between 1307 and 1312 and their last Grand Master was burned
in 1314. Freemasonry's first documented grand lodge dates from the 1720s. That is a gap of
four centuries. Something can be asserted across such a gap; nothing can be demonstrated
across it without evidence from inside it.

**6. Anachronism. Does the claim contain something that could not yet exist?**

This is the sharpest instrument in the set, because it can kill a claim outright with a
single observation.

Some examples that will recur. A Masonic symbol whose meaning depends on the English
letter G cannot have originated in a culture that did not use the Roman alphabet. A
Kabbalistic system that assigns the tarot trumps to the paths of the Tree of Life cannot
predate the tarot, which is a fifteenth-century Italian card game. An order claiming
Rosicrucian descent from before 1614 is claiming descent from a document that had not been
written. A ritual that uses the Authorised Version's English cannot be older than 1611.

Anachronism detection requires knowing when things were invented, which is tedious and
unglamorous and is most of what historical training consists of.

---

### The rule

From those six questions follows one rule, which I am going to state in its own paragraph
because it is the single most important sentence in this book after the thesis itself.

**A parallel is not a descent.**

If a Masonic symbol resembles an Egyptian one, that is a fact about the two symbols. To
convert it into a fact about history, you need a route — questions three and four above.
Without a route, what you have is a resemblance, and resemblances are cheap. There are
only so many shapes. Human beings across all cultures have used light for knowledge,
darkness for ignorance, height for authority, the circle for completeness, the square for
stability, and death-and-return for transformation. These are not proprietary. They recur
because human bodies and human societies recur.

The dying-and-rising figure is a useful demonstration. Freemasonry's third degree centers
on the murder and raising of a master builder. Osiris is killed and restored. Adonis dies
and returns. Christ is crucified and rises. Alchemical texts describe a putrefaction
followed by a rebirth. The pattern is everywhere.

Does that mean Freemasonry took its central legend from Egypt?

Ask the six questions. Provenance: where is the document connecting them? None. Chronology:
possible in principle, since Egypt is older. Transmission: name the route — nobody can.
Intertextuality: does the Masonic legend quote or paraphrase any Egyptian source? No; its
materials are biblical and drawn from the craft's own medieval legends. Institutional
continuity: none whatsoever. Anachronism: the legend first appears in print in 1730, and
the Egyptology that would have made a conscious borrowing possible did not exist in Europe
until after Napoleon's expedition of 1798.

The parallel is real. The descent is unsupported. Both of those statements are true at
once, and holding both is the discipline this book is asking the reader to acquire.

---

### Why traditions get invented

One more thing before we begin the history proper, because the six questions tell you
*whether* something was invented and not *why*, and the why is genuinely interesting.

In 1983 the historians Eric Hobsbawm and Terence Ranger published a book arguing that a
great many traditions that present themselves as immemorial are in fact recent, often
strikingly recent, and that they tend to be manufactured in periods of rapid social
change, by people who need continuity with a past that is slipping.[^5] Scottish clan
tartans, most royal ceremonial, a good deal of what looks like folk custom: eighteenth
and nineteenth century, frequently with identifiable authors.

The eighteenth century, which is the century in which almost everything in this book was
built, was exactly such a period. Old structures of religious authority had broken; new
ones had not consolidated. A man who wanted to belong to something with roots had to find
something with roots. And if he could not find one, the period was remarkably willing to
supply the deficiency.

This is not, mostly, fraud. That distinction matters and I want to make it carefully.
When Andrew Michael Ramsay told an audience of French Masons in 1737 that their order
descended from crusader knights, he was not necessarily lying; he may well have believed
a version of it, and he was certainly performing a recognized eighteenth-century genre in
which an institution's dignity was established by giving it an antique genealogy. When
William Wynn Westcott produced letters from a German adept who did not exist, that was
something closer to fraud — but even there, the fraud was in the service of a conviction
he appears genuinely to have held, that the material he was working with was real and
merely needed a pedigree in order to be taken seriously.

The historian Anthony Grafton, in a short and brilliant book on forgery in Western
scholarship, observed that forgers and critics have always developed together: each
advance in the art of fabrication provokes an advance in the art of detection, and the
tools scholars use to catch fakes were largely built by people trying to catch fakes.[^6]
Casaubon's philology is one of those tools. So is everything in this chapter.

---

We now have what we need.

We know what happened at Warrington in 1646, and how little it tells us. We know what the
word *magic* has meant, and how violently it has changed. We know what was secret in a
lodge and why publishing it changed nothing. We know what an eighteenth-century initiation
consisted of. And we have six questions and one rule.

So we can go back to the beginning — the real beginning, which is not a lodge and not a
temple and not a pyramid, but a set of Greek dialogues written in Roman Egypt at about
the time the Gospel of John was being composed, which a Florentine priest would translate
into Latin in 1471 under the impression that he was recovering the oldest wisdom in the
world.

Europe would believe him for a hundred and forty-three years.

Some people still do.

---
