# PART III — THE MASONIC MACHINE

# CHAPTER THIRTEEN
## Schaw's Statutes

On the twenty-eighth of December 1598, the Master of Works to the Crown of Scotland
signed a document regulating the masons of his kingdom, and thereby — without the least
intention of doing so — began Freemasonry.

William Schaw was a courtier. He had charge of the royal building works: palaces,
repairs, the machinery of a small monarchy's architectural ambitions. He had traveled on
the continent, he was a Catholic in a Protestant country, and he seems to have been the
kind of administrator who thinks institutions should be tidy. The statutes he issued in
1598 and again, more remarkably, in 1599 are administrative documents. They are about
apprenticeship terms, the number of apprentices a master may take, the quality of work,
the settling of disputes, the obligation of members to attend, and the fines for not doing
so.[^1]

They are also the founding charter of the modern lodge, and the reason is buried in the
detail.

---

### What the statutes actually do

Four things, and each of them is decisive.

**They make the lodge permanent.** In the medieval building trade a lodge was a shed. It
was the hut beside the cathedral where the masons kept their tools, ate, sometimes slept,
and conducted the business of the site. When the cathedral was finished, the lodge came
down. It was temporary by definition, because a building project is temporary.

Schaw's statutes assume something different: lodges attached to *places* rather than to
projects. Edinburgh's lodge. Kilwinning's lodge. Stirling's lodge. Institutions with
continuous existence independent of whether anything was being built.

**They make it write things down.** The statutes require lodges to keep records — to
enrol apprentices and fellows in a book, with names and dates and witnesses. This is the
single most consequential clause in the history of Freemasonry, and it is entirely
bureaucratic in motivation. Because of it, we have minutes. The Lodge of Edinburgh
(Mary's Chapel) has records running from 1599; the lodge at Aitchison's Haven from 1598.
They are the oldest continuous minutes of any such body in the world, and they exist
because a royal official wanted better paperwork.[^2]

**They make it a jurisdiction.** The statutes give lodges authority over their members —
to judge disputes, impose fines, and regulate conduct. A lodge becomes a small court as
well as a workshop.

**And they make it a body with secrets to test.** The 1599 statutes require that fellows
of craft be examined in "the art of memory and the science thereof," and that the warden
test them on it.[^3]

That last clause is the one everybody argues about, and it is worth stopping on.

---

### The art of memory

In the classical world, an orator with a long speech and no notes used a technique: he
imagined a building he knew well, placed vivid images representing his topics at specific
points along a route through it, and then walked the route mentally while speaking. The
images recalled the topics; the architecture kept them in order. The technique is
described in the *Rhetorica ad Herennium*, in Cicero and in Quintilian, and it was taught
throughout the Middle Ages and the Renaissance.

By the sixteenth century it had grown considerably more ambitious. Giordano Bruno, among
others, proposed memory systems in which the imagined building was the cosmos itself, and
the images were not arbitrary but drawn from astrological and magical correspondence — so
that mastering the memory system would be mastering the universe's structure. Frances
Yates traced this development in *The Art of Memory*, one of the great works of
twentieth-century intellectual history.[^4]

Now: Schaw requires Scottish masons to be tested in the art of memory in 1599. Yates had
established that the art of memory in that period had occult dimensions. David Stevenson,
in *The Origins of Freemasonry*, put the two together and proposed that the Scottish lodge
had absorbed Renaissance esoteric ideas — that this is the moment when something more than
stonecutting enters the craft.[^5]

Stevenson's book is the finest thing written on early Masonic history and I rely on it
throughout. But I do not think this particular argument is proved, and I want to say why,
carefully, because it is the strongest candidate anyone has produced for an esoteric
element in Freemasonry before the eighteenth century.

Run the questions.

The clause says the fellows are to be tested in the art of memory. It does not say which
art of memory. There were two things that phrase could mean in 1599: the ordinary,
utilitarian mnemonic technique taught in every grammar school, which an illiterate or
semi-literate craftsman would need in order to hold a body of trade knowledge without
writing it down; and the elaborate Brunian cosmic version.

Which would you expect a Scottish master mason to be tested on?

The statutes' own context suggests the first. The surrounding clauses are about
apprenticeship terms and fines. The lodge's business was a trade. And the thing a mason
needed to memorize — the catechism, the questions and answers by which he could be
recognized — is exactly the kind of material the ordinary mnemonic art was for.

The transmission question is also unanswered. Schaw had traveled; he was educated; the
Scottish court had continental connections. It is *possible* that he knew the Brunian
tradition. Nobody has produced evidence that he did.

So the honest verdict is: **plausible, suggestive, and unproven.** I record it that way,
and it goes on the list in Chapter 52.

What I would add is that the case does not need to be proved for Schaw to matter. The art
of memory clause is interesting; the record-keeping clause is world-changing.

---

### The gentlemen arrive

Because here is what the records, once they exist, let us see.

In June 1600, the minutes of the Lodge of Edinburgh record the presence of John Boswell,
Laird of Auchinleck. He signs with a mark, as the masons do. He is not a mason. He is a
landowner.[^6]

That is the earliest documented attendance of a non-operative at a lodge meeting anywhere
in the world, and it happens within two years of Schaw's statutes, in a lodge that exists
to keep records because Schaw told it to.

Over the following century the pattern spreads. Scottish lodges admit lairds, merchants,
professional men — people with no intention of cutting stone. Sometimes a few, sometimes
enough to change the character of the body. By the later seventeenth century, some lodges
have more gentlemen than masons.

Why?

The honest answer is that we do not fully know, and the candidates are several. There is
patronage: a trade body benefits from having influential men associated with it, and
influential men benefit from the connection to a trade they may employ. There is
sociability: a lodge was a reason to meet, drink and dine in a society with few other
institutions for doing so across class lines. There is curiosity about a group with an
oath and a secret. There is local politics. And there is, possibly, the appeal of the
craft's own legendary history — the Old Charges, with their claim that masonry descends
from Euclid and the building of Solomon's Temple, which would have been read aloud at
admissions and which gave a small trade body a genealogy grander than any guild in
Scotland.

What there is no evidence for, in any Scottish lodge record of the seventeenth century, is
esoteric practice. No alchemy in the minutes. No Kabbalah. No astrology. No Hermetic
vocabulary. The minutes are about money, discipline, apprentices, and dinner.

---

### The first machine

Step back and look at what exists in Scotland by, say, 1650.

A network of permanent local bodies. Each with a written record. Each with authority over
its members. Each with an admission ceremony involving an oath. Each holding a body of
memorized material — a catechism of questions and answers, a word, a grip — which
distinguishes members from non-members. Each with a legendary history reaching to
antiquity. And each, increasingly, containing men who have nothing to do with the trade
the whole thing is nominally about.

That is Freemasonry. Not the symbolism, not the philosophy, not the three degrees — those
come later and elsewhere. But the *machine*: the structural apparatus that everything else
will be hung on.

And it was built, so far as any evidence shows, for entirely ordinary reasons, by a royal
administrator who wanted his kingdom's masons to keep proper books.

This is the pattern this book keeps encountering, and it is worth naming now that we have
a clean example. **The most consequential things in this history were generally built by
people who were not trying to build them.** Ficino translated the Hermetica because his
patron was dying. Andreae wrote a jest. Schaw wrote a regulation. In each case the object
produced went on to do something enormous that its maker neither intended nor lived to
see.

There is a version of history that finds this disappointing, because it prefers designers
to accidents. I find it the opposite. An institution that was designed can be explained by
its design. An institution that emerged has to be explained by what people actually did
with it, which is harder and more interesting, and which is the only kind of explanation
that ever holds up.

---

### The English question

One complication before we cross the border.

If Scotland has the lodges, the records and the ceremony from 1598, and England has almost
no lodge records before the late seventeenth century, does that mean English Freemasonry
is Scottish in origin?

Stevenson's answer is essentially yes, and the institutional evidence supports him. But
England has something Scotland does not: the Old Charges. The manuscript constitutions of
the English masons — the Regius poem of about 1390, the Cooke manuscript of about 1410, and
more than a hundred later copies — are a body of legendary and regulatory text with no
Scottish equivalent of comparable age. And it is the Old Charges, not the Scottish statutes,
that supply the mythology: Euclid, the two pillars, the Temple, the descent of geometry.

So the honest reconstruction is a convergence rather than a single origin. Scotland
supplies the institution; England supplies the legend; and the fusion happens somewhere in
the seventeenth century, in ways the surviving evidence lets us see only in outline.

Which brings us back to a room in Warrington in 1646, and to the English side of the story
— where the records are worse, the evidence is thinner, and the men who joined are, for
that very reason, more interesting.

---

# CHAPTER FOURTEEN
## Ashmole's Warrington

The English evidence is bad, and it is worth understanding *how* it is bad, because the
shape of the gap has been filled, repeatedly, with whatever the filler wanted to find.

Scotland has lodge minutes from 1598. England has essentially nothing comparable until the
1720s. There are a handful of references — a diary entry here, a company record there, a
naturalist's paragraph — and then a silence of decades, and then, quite suddenly, a fully
organized Grand Lodge with a printed constitution.

What England does have, in abundance, is manuscripts of a different kind.

---

### The Old Charges

More than a hundred copies survive of a text that circulated among English masons from the
late fourteenth century onward. Its oldest version is a poem of some 800 lines, written in
Middle English around 1390, now British Library Royal MS 17 A.I and known as the Regius
manuscript; the next oldest is the Cooke manuscript of about 1410. The later copies, in
prose, run through the fifteenth, sixteenth and seventeenth centuries and into the
eighteenth.[^1]

They have a consistent shape. An invocation. Then a legendary history of the craft. Then a
set of charges — regulations of conduct — which the candidate was to hear read and to
swear to observe.

The history is the part that matters here, and it is worth summarising accurately, because
it is routinely misrepresented in both directions.

It begins before the Flood. The children of Lamech discover the sciences, and, knowing that
the world will be destroyed by either fire or water, inscribe them on two pillars — one of
marble that will not burn, one of brick that will not sink — so that the knowledge will
survive. After the Flood the pillars are found. Geometry passes to Egypt, where Abraham
teaches it; Euclid systematizes it and instructs the sons of Egyptian nobility in the
mason's craft. The craft passes to Israel, where Solomon employs eighty thousand masons on
the Temple. Thence to France, and thence, with St Alban and later with King Athelstan, to
England.

So: yes, the Old Charges contain Egypt. Yes, they contain the Temple. Yes, they contain
antediluvian pillars preserving knowledge from destruction.

And no, none of it is Hermetic, esoteric or occult. Every element is traceable to the
ordinary learned furniture of medieval England: the two-pillars story comes from Josephus,
mediated through medieval universal chronicles; the Euclid-in-Egypt material comes from
the standard histories of the liberal arts; the Temple comes from the Bible.[^2] These are
the sources a literate cleric of 1400 would have had. The craft legend is a trade's
self-flattery assembled from available learning, and it is exactly what a guild would
produce.

But it does something important. It tells a working mason that his trade is not a trade.
It is the practical form of geometry, which is one of the seven liberal sciences, which
were preserved from the Flood, and which built the house of God. A man who has heard that
read aloud at his admission has been told that he belongs to something ancient and
significant.

That is not an esoteric doctrine. It is, however, exactly the kind of thing that attracts
a gentleman with antiquarian interests.

---

### The three witnesses

For England before 1700 we have essentially three kinds of testimony.

**The diaries.** Ashmole's, discussed in Chapter 1: made a Free Mason at Warrington on 16
October 1646 with Colonel Henry Mainwaring, in company with seven men who were not masons,
in a town with no significant building trade; and then, thirty-five years later, attending
a lodge at Masons' Hall in London in March 1682. Ashmole is our best English witness and he
tells us nothing about content.

**The company records.** The London Company of Masons — the livery company, a trade
organization of a different kind — has references from the 1620s onward to something called
the Accepcion or Acception: a body within or alongside the Company into which men could be
"accepted," including men who were not members of the Company at all. Payments are
recorded. It cost more to be accepted if you were not a member.[^3] This is thin evidence,
but it is evidence of a non-operative practice in London a generation before Warrington.

**The naturalists.** In 1686 Robert Plot published *The Natural History of
Staffordshire*, and in the course of surveying the county's customs devoted several
paragraphs to what he called the Society of Free-Masons. He reports that the custom is
spread more or less all over the nation; that persons of the most eminent quality do not
disdain to be of the fellowship; that admission involves a meeting of at least five or six
of the ancients of the Order, a collation, gloves given to the candidate and to his wife,
and the communication of certain secret signs by which members recognize one another
anywhere. He also reports that they have a written history of the craft — he clearly had
seen a copy of the Old Charges — and he thought the history was nonsense, saying so at
some length.[^4]

Plot is a hostile and slightly amused witness, which makes him valuable. If there had been
anything scandalous, magical or heterodox on offer, he would have said so; he was
perfectly willing to say the craft's history was fabulous.

John Aubrey, in a manuscript note of the same year, records that the fraternity of
Free-Masons was to have a convention at St Paul's, and mentions Sir Christopher Wren among
those to be adopted.[^5]

That is the English seventeenth century. Two diary entries, some company accounts, a
skeptical naturalist and an antiquary's note.

---

### What can be said

From that thin material, three statements can be made with confidence, and they are worth
separating from everything that has been built on top of them.

**One: non-operative membership was real, and earlier in England than is often assumed.**
The London Acception from the 1620s, Warrington in 1646, Plot's "persons of the most
eminent quality" in 1686. Men who were not masons were joining masons' lodges across most
of the century.

**Two: the content, so far as any witness describes it, was signs, words, a history and an
oath.** Plot describes recognition signs. Ashmole describes nothing. The Scottish
catechisms, which are the only contemporary ritual texts we have from anywhere in Britain,
describe a word, a grip and a catechism. Nobody in the seventeenth century, friendly or
hostile, describes anything a modern reader would call occult.

**Three: nobody wrote down why they joined.**

That third point is the one I want to press, because the gap it leaves is where most of the
mythology lives.

We have, in Elias Ashmole, a man who was an alchemist, an astrologer, a collector of John
Dee's manuscripts, a copier of Rosicrucian application letters — and a Freemason. It is
almost irresistible to conclude that the last item belongs with the others.

But consider what else Ashmole was. He was an antiquary who collected everything: coins,
curiosities, charters, heraldry, the founding documents of the Order of the Garter, on
which he wrote an enormous book. He was a joiner of things. He was a man who documented
customs. A small, old, secretive society with a written legendary history reaching back to
Euclid and Solomon is, for a person of that temperament, an object of interest entirely
independent of whether it contains any wisdom.

Both readings are available. Neither is documented. And the correct historical statement is
that we do not know, which is unsatisfying and true.

---

### The shape of the silence

One last observation, because it will matter enormously in the next chapter.

Between Plot in 1686 and the printed constitutions of the 1720s, English Freemasonry is
almost invisible. There are scattered references. There is no institutional record. And
then, in a period of about six years, the following things happen: a Grand Lodge is either
formed or consolidated in London; a minister named James Anderson is commissioned to
compile a constitution; the constitution is printed in 1723 with a mythological history,
a set of charges, and a religious formula of startling breadth; the first exposures appear
in the newspapers; and the institution begins expanding at a rate that will carry it
across Europe within twenty years.

Something happened in London between about 1716 and 1723.

We know less about what it was than almost anyone assumes, and the reason is that our
principal source for it is a man writing fifteen years afterward with an institutional
interest in the answer.

---

# CHAPTER FIFTEEN
## 1717, or 1721

Every organization needs a birthday, and Freemasonry's is printed on the front of its own
buildings.

The story is told in James Anderson's *New Book of Constitutions*, published in 1738. Four
old London lodges — meeting at the Goose and Gridiron alehouse in St Paul's Churchyard, the
Crown near Drury Lane, the Apple-Tree Tavern in Covent Garden, and the Rummer and Grapes
in Westminster — came together at the Apple-Tree, constituted themselves a Grand Lodge,
and on St John the Baptist's Day, 24 June 1717, at the Goose and Gridiron, elected Anthony
Sayer as their Grand Master.[^1]

From that meeting, the account runs, descends every regular Grand Lodge on earth. In 2017
the United Grand Lodge of England celebrated its tercentenary on that basis, with a great
deal of ceremony and a number of commemorative objects.

There is a difficulty. The difficulty is that Anderson's 1738 account is the only evidence
that the 1717 meeting ever happened.

---

### The evidentiary situation

Set out plainly, it is this.

Grand Lodge's own minutes begin on 24 June 1723. Nothing survives from before that date.

Anderson's first *Constitutions*, printed in 1723 — that is, within six years of the
claimed foundation, by a man writing for the body itself — does not mention the 1717
meeting at all. It gives the charges, the history, the regulations. It does not say the
Grand Lodge was founded in 1717.[^2]

The 1717 narrative appears for the first time in the second edition of 1738: fifteen years
after the first, twenty-one years after the event, written by the same author, who by then
had a considerable interest in establishing the institution's continuity and his own place
in it.

There is no contemporary newspaper report of the 1717 meeting. No lodge minute. No letter.
No diary entry. Nothing.

Apply the first of our six questions — *where is the physical object?* — and the answer is:
there is one book, printed twenty-one years afterward, by an interested party.

That does not make the story false. Institutions frequently record their own beginnings
late and imperfectly, and Anderson was writing within the lifetime of participants who
could have contradicted him. But it does mean that 1717 is a *claim*, not a fact, and it
should have been treated as one long before it was.

---

### The diarist

In 2016 and 2017 two historians, Andrew Prescott and Susan Mitchell Sommers, published work
proposing a different date, and their principal external evidence is a diary.[^3]

William Stukeley was an antiquary of real distinction — one of the founders of the Society
of Antiquaries, the man whose fieldwork at Stonehenge and Avebury produced records of
features that have since been destroyed, and a compulsive keeper of notes. On 6 January
1721 he wrote:

> I was made a Free Mason at the Salutation Tav. Tavistock street, with Mr Collins, Capt.
> Rowe who made the famous diving engine.

And he added an observation. He was, he said, the first person made a freemason in London
for many years, and they had had difficulty finding enough members to perform the
ceremony.[^4]

Sit with that for a moment.

The Salutation Tavern in Tavistock Street is a two- or three-minute walk from St Paul's
Churchyard. If a Grand Lodge comprising four lodges had been meeting in that immediate
neighborhood continuously since June 1717 — holding annual assemblies, electing Grand
Masters, constituting new lodges — then in January 1721 it should not have been difficult
to assemble enough Masons to initiate a candidate a few streets away. And a well-connected
antiquary who moved in exactly the circles from which the Grand Lodge's membership was
drawn should not have believed that he was the first man made a Mason in London for years.

Stukeley was not arguing a case. He was writing a private note about his own evening. That
is precisely what makes it valuable: a disinterested contemporary observation that is very
hard to reconcile with the received account.

---

### The alternative

What Prescott and Sommers propose in its place is that the effective foundation of the
Grand Lodge was the installation of John Montagu, second Duke of Montagu, as Grand Master
at Stationers' Hall on 24 June 1721.

The case for that date is circumstantial but coherent. It is the first Grand Lodge event
for which there is decent contemporary evidence. It marks the moment a nobleman took the
chair, which is when a tavern association becomes an institution with social weight and
national ambitions. It is immediately followed by everything that looks like organizational
activity: the commissioning of Anderson's *Constitutions*, the start of the minute book,
rapid expansion, and the first newspaper attention.

And on this reading, the 1717 story is not a fabrication so much as a *backdating* — the
projection of an origin a few years earlier than the reality, perhaps by conflating an
informal gathering of London Masons with the founding of a Grand Lodge, in a book written
two decades later by a man whose job was to supply the institution with a history.

---

### How to hold this

I want to be careful about how firmly to state the conclusion, because there is a
temptation to overclaim in both directions.

Some accounts of the Prescott and Sommers argument say it "conclusively proves" the 1721
date. That is stronger than the evidence supports. An argument from one diarist's
impression, however well placed, is not proof; Stukeley could have been mistaken about how
long it had been since a London initiation, or could have been describing a lodge
unconnected with the Grand Lodge's circle, or could have been in an unfashionable corner
of a world larger than he knew.

But the other direction is worse. The 1717 date rests on a single retrospective source
with an institutional motive, unsupported by any contemporary document, and contradicted by
the best contemporary observation we have. A historian who treated any *other* claim on
that evidence as established would be doing it wrong.

So the verdict this book adopts: **the 1721 case is stronger, the 1717 date should be
described as Anderson's claim rather than as a fact, and the question is genuinely open.**

It goes on the list in Chapter 52.

---

### Why this chapter matters more than a date

Here is the reason I have given a disputed anniversary two thousand words.

This is the one case in the entire book where we can watch a foundation myth being made,
at close range, with the documents in front of us — and where the institution making it is
not an occult order but the most respectable, best-documented and most bureaucratically
scrupulous body in this history.

Grand Lodge was not a conspiracy. It kept minutes. It printed its constitutions. Its
members were physicians and lawyers and Fellows of the Royal Society. And within
twenty-one years of its own beginning, it had acquired an origin story that its own
earlier publication does not support, which it then repeated for three centuries, which is
carved into buildings and printed on certificates and was celebrated worldwide in 2017.

Nobody had to lie for that to happen. It requires only an institution that wants a clean
beginning, an author willing to supply one, a gap of two decades, and the ordinary human
preference for a date over a muddle.

Now hold that observation up against the rest of this book.

If the *documented, minute-keeping, Enlightenment* end of this history acquired a false or
at least unverifiable origin date within twenty-one years — what should we expect of
organizations that kept no minutes, published nothing, swore their members to silence, and
had every incentive to claim antiquity?

We should expect exactly what we find. The Templar descent of the Rite of Strict
Observance. The Unknown Superiors nobody could produce. The German Rosicrucian adept who
never existed. The Egyptian mysteries. The unbroken chain from Solomon.

Anderson is the control case. He shows us how little pressure it takes, and how honest the
people involved can be, for a history to drift.

---

### A note on what did not happen

One more thing, because the 1717/1721 question is sometimes recruited into a larger and
sillier argument.

Nothing in this dispute suggests that Freemasonry is younger than commonly thought, or that
it was invented in the 1720s out of nothing. The Scottish lodges are documented from 1598.
The Old Charges run back to about 1390. Ashmole was initiated in 1646. Plot was describing
an established English custom in 1686. The Craft is unambiguously older than any Grand
Lodge.

What is at issue is the founding date of a *governing body* — an administrative
superstructure imposed on an existing practice. That superstructure mattered enormously,
because it standardized ritual, printed constitutions, chartered lodges abroad, and turned
a scattered custom into an international institution. But it did not create Freemasonry. It
organized it.

And in doing so, it produced the document that would define what Freemasonry was permitted
to mean for the next three hundred years — a printed book of 1723, containing a mythical
history, a set of regulations, and one sentence about religion that would prove to be the
most consequential sentence in Masonic history.

---

# CHAPTER SIXTEEN
## The Religion in Which All Men Agree

In 1723 a Presbyterian minister from Aberdeen, working in London, published a book that
contained the following sentence about what a Freemason was obliged to believe:

> A Mason is oblig'd by his Tenure, to obey the moral Law; and if he rightly understands
> the Art, he will never be a stupid Atheist, nor an irreligious Libertine. But though in
> ancient Times Masons were charg'd in every Country to be of the Religion of that Country
> or Nation, whatever it was, yet 'tis now thought more expedient only to oblige them to
> that Religion in which all Men agree, leaving their particular Opinions to themselves.[^1]

It is the most important sentence in the history of Freemasonry, and probably the most
consequential sentence written by any Englishman in the 1720s that is not about property or
succession.

---

### What it does

Read it again and notice the manoeuvre.

It does not say that Masons may believe anything. It requires the moral law, and it rules
out atheism and libertinism. It is not a charter of free thought.

What it does is *relocate the requirement*. The old rule was: be of the religion of your
country. The new rule is: be of the religion in which all men agree.

And since the religion in which all men agree is, necessarily, whatever remains when you
subtract everything people disagree about, the sentence has the effect of removing
confessional doctrine from the qualifications for membership. Trinity, sacraments, papal
authority, predestination, the real presence, church government — the entire catalog of
things Europeans had been killing each other over for two centuries — are placed outside
the lodge door and described as "particular Opinions."

Anderson does not say what the religion in which all men agree consists of. He leaves it
undefined. Later Masonic usage would settle on belief in a Supreme Being, formulated as the
Great Architect of the Universe, and on immortality; the English Constitutions today
require belief in a Supreme Being as a condition of membership, and some continental
jurisdictions dropped the requirement in the nineteenth century, which is the origin of
the great regularity schism. But in 1723 it is deliberately vacant.

Vacancy, again. The same structural feature we found in the ritual.

---

### What it was for

Anderson was not a philosopher of toleration. He was writing for a specific group of men
with a specific problem, and the historian who has done most to identify them is Ric
Berman.

Berman's reconstruction of the circle around the early Grand Lodge — the Horn Tavern lodge
in Westminster above all — shows a tightly connected network: Fellows of the Royal Society,
Newtonians, Huguenot refugees, Whigs, physicians, natural philosophers and men of the
professions. John Theophilus Desaguliers is the emblematic figure: a Huguenot whose family
fled France after the revocation of the Edict of Nantes, an experimental philosopher who
demonstrated Newton's work publicly, a Fellow of the Royal Society, and Grand Master in
1719.[^2]

Berman's argument is that this group did not inherit Freemasonry so much as reconfigure it
— that the organization was radically reshaped by a small, largely self-appointed core
with a coherent social and political program.

Margaret Jacob, approaching from a different angle, places the same development inside the
emergence of European civil society: lodges as places where men of different ranks met on
declared equality, elected their officers, kept accounts, debated procedure, and practiced
a form of associational life that had few other outlets in the period. Her term for this
is the making of a new kind of public.[^3]

Both readings point the same way. The religious formula was not a mystical gesture. It was
the enabling condition for a social experiment: if you want a room in which a Huguenot
refugee, an Anglican clergyman, a Presbyterian Scot, a freethinking Whig and — within a
decade — a Jewish merchant can sit together without the conversation exploding, you must
have a rule that removes the subject.

---

### The cost

I want to state the achievement fairly and then state what it cost, because this book has
no interest in flattering its subject.

The achievement is real and should not be patronised. In 1723, within living memory of a
civil war fought partly over church government, in a country with penal laws against
Catholics and disabilities against Dissenters and Jews, a public organization declared that
it would not ask. Jewish members appear in London lodges within a few years of the
*Constitutions*.[^4] Continental lodges would admit across confessional lines in places
where that was socially extraordinary. The lodge was, for a great many eighteenth-century
men, the only room in their lives where the question did not come up.

The cost is the subject of most of Part VI.

A body that will not specify what it believes cannot object to what people decide it
believes. Anderson's formula purchased breadth by surrendering definition — and a symbolic
system with no authorised interpretation is a system that can be interpreted by anybody.

For a century that hardly mattered. Then, from the 1740s, the high degrees began supplying
content, and nobody had the standing to say no, because the institution had given up the
vocabulary of doctrine. By the nineteenth century, an American Mason could open a book
written by the head of his own Rite and find Kabbalah, Zoroastrianism, Hermeticism and
comparative mythology presented as the inner meaning of his degrees — and there was no
mechanism by which Freemasonry could say *that is not what this means*, because
Freemasonry had spent a hundred and fifty years declining to say what anything meant.

The vacancy that made the Craft tolerant made it colonisable. Those are the same property,
described twice.

---

### The rest of the book

The 1723 *Constitutions* is not only that sentence, and the rest repays attention.

It opens with a history of the craft that occupies nearly half the volume: the Old Charges'
legend, expanded, polished and extended, running from Adam — who is credited with geometry
written on his heart — through the Flood, Egypt, Solomon's Temple, the classical world, and
so to Britain and the present.[^5] It is antiquarian in manner and entirely uncritical. It
is also the mechanism by which the medieval craft legend, a trade's private self-flattery,
became a printed and widely distributed account available to every reader in Europe.

That matters more than its accuracy. From 1723 onward, anybody in Europe who wanted to know
what Freemasonry claimed about its own origins could buy the answer. The claim was: we
descend from the builders of the Temple, and our art is geometry, and our lineage runs to
the beginning of the world.

When Frenchmen began constructing high degrees in the 1740s, this was the raw material they
were working from. When nineteenth-century occultists looked for ancient wisdom in Masonry,
this was the text that told them it was there. Anderson's history is the source of the very
belief this book spends its length testing — and Anderson himself almost certainly regarded
it as antiquarian entertainment rather than as doctrine.

The volume also contains the Charges of a Free-Mason, the General Regulations, and — a
detail worth noticing — a set of Masonic songs with music, to be sung at the annual feast.
The founding document of the world's great secret society ends with drinking songs.

---

### Who Anderson was

James Anderson is a slightly absurd figure and I have some affection for him.

He was a Scot, an ordained Presbyterian minister of a London congregation, a compiler by
temperament. He lost money in the South Sea Bubble. He published, besides the
*Constitutions*, an enormous work of royal genealogy, which tells you what kind of mind he
had: the kind that likes descent, lineage and the tracing of things back.

He was commissioned to digest the old Gothic Constitutions into a new and better method,
and he did. Whether he also invented the 1717 story, or repeated what he had been told, or
conflated things he half remembered, we cannot say. What is clear is that the man who gave
Freemasonry its written history was a genealogist by inclination, working for an
institution that wanted a pedigree, at a moment when nobody could check.

That is not a conspiracy. It is a job description, and the results of it are still being
argued about three hundred years later.

---

We now have the institution: a Grand Lodge of uncertain birthday, a printed constitution, a
religious formula of unprecedented breadth, a legendary history in wide circulation, and a
membership of physicians, noblemen and natural philosophers.

What we have not yet examined is the thing they actually did when the door was shut. Not
the secrets — we covered those, and they were thin — but the *performance*: a room arranged
in a particular way, men standing in particular places, a script, a drama, and a candidate
in the middle of it.

That is where the machine does its work, and it is where we go next.

---

# CHAPTER SEVENTEEN
## The Lodge as Theater

Consider the room.

An upstairs chamber in a tavern, hired for the evening. Perhaps thirty feet by twenty. A
table, chairs, candles, a fire if the season demands it. When the lodge is not sitting, it
is a room where people eat.

And then, for two hours, it is not.

The transformation is accomplished by almost nothing. Chairs are placed: one in the east,
raised if possible; one in the west; one in the south. The door is guarded. Something is
laid out on the floor — in the early eighteenth century, a design drawn in chalk and
charcoal on the boards, which the newest member erased with a mop and bucket at the end of
the evening, and which would later be replaced by a painted cloth and then by a framed
board hung on the wall.[^1] A book is opened on the table with two instruments laid on it.

That is the whole apparatus. A tavern room, some chairs, a drawing on the floor, and men
standing where they are told.

It works because of where they stand.

---

### Blocking

Theater people call the arrangement of bodies in a space *blocking*, and it is the first
thing a director does, because an audience reads position before it hears a word.

The Masonic lodge is blocked with great precision and the meaning of the positions is never
explained to the candidate. He learns it by occupying the space.

There is an east, and the principal officer sits in it, and light comes from there. The
association between east, sunrise, authority and knowledge does not need to be taught to
anyone raised in a European culture; it is already installed by every church the man has
ever entered. There is a west, with a second officer, and a south with a third. The
candidate enters from a direction that is not east. He is conducted around the room in a
fixed direction, stopping at stations. He is brought to the center. He kneels at the
center. When he rises he is placed somewhere he was not before.

None of this is stated as doctrine. All of it is experienced as fact.

Catherine Bell, writing about ritual in general, made the observation that does the most
work here: ritual, she argued, should be thought of not as a category of activity but as a
*strategy* — a way of acting that distinguishes itself from ordinary acting, and that
produces authority precisely by being formalised, repetitive and set apart. What ritual
does, on her account, is not communicate a message but structure a situation, and the
structuring is the point.[^2]

A tavern room in which men stand in cardinal positions, speak in fixed formulae and move in
prescribed directions is not a tavern room. Nobody has to say so.

---

### Script

The eighteenth-century lodge ran on memorized call and response.

The catechisms in the exposures give the form. *Are you a Mason? I am so taken and
accepted among brothers and fellows. How shall I know you to be a Mason? By signs and
tokens and perfect points of my entrance.* And onward, question by question, through the
form of the lodge, its supports, its covering, its furniture, its jewels, its situation.[^3]

Three features of this are worth naming.

**It is rhythmic and formulaic**, which is how oral material survives. The questions do not
vary. The answers do not vary. A man who learned them in 1730 could be examined in 1770 and
still have them. Before general literacy, this was simply how bodies of knowledge were
carried, and the fact that Masonic ritual was transmitted by memory rather than by book —
in most jurisdictions it is still learned by heart — is a direct inheritance of that world.

**It is dialogic.** Nobody lectures. The knowledge exists between two people and has to be
produced by both. You cannot possess the catechism privately; it only exists when it is
performed. That is a striking property for a body of secret knowledge to have, and it means
the knowledge is inseparable from the relationship.

**And it is a test.** The catechism is how a stranger is examined. Its social function is
identification, but its ritual function is something else: it turns membership into
something continuously demonstrable. You are a Mason to the extent that you can answer.

---

### Drama

By 1730 there is something in the lodge that is not a catechism at all.

The third degree, as reported in *Masonry Dissected*, contains a story with characters and
a plot, and — critically — the candidate does not hear the story. He *plays* it. He is the
master builder. He is accosted, struck, killed. He is laid out. He is buried. He is
searched for, found, and raised.

This is a different technology from question and answer, and it is the innovation that
makes Freemasonry what it became.

The theater historian's term for what the candidate undergoes is not quite available,
because ordinary theater has an audience watching actors. Here the audience *are* the
actors, and the protagonist has not read the script, does not know what happens next,
cannot see for part of it, and is the only person in the room who does not know how it
ends.

The effect is not that he learns a legend. It is that a legend happens to him.

And note what is required for this to work: a room arranged in advance, men who know their
parts, an order of events, properties, costume of a sort, and a candidate in a prepared
state. That is a production. The lodge of 1730 is a company of amateur players performing
the same piece repeatedly, each time for a single spectator who is also the lead.

---

### Kit

The material culture deserves a paragraph, because it is where the theater becomes visible
to us at this distance.

**The apron.** A working mason's protective garment, made of leather, entirely practical in
origin — and then made smaller, made of white lambskin, decorated, and turned into the
principal badge of membership. By the later eighteenth century, aprons were being painted
with elaborate symbolic scenes and are now museum objects. The trajectory from protective
clothing to ceremonial regalia is the whole history of Freemasonry in a single garment.

**The tracing board.** The design drawn in chalk on the floor, later painted on cloth or
board: a diagram containing the symbols of the degree, arranged as a composition. It is a
teaching aid, a stage set and an icon at once, and it is where the Craft's symbolism
becomes *visual* rather than verbal.

**The working tools.** Square, level, plumb-rule, gauge, gavel, trowel, compasses. Real
tools, or models of them, handled and explained.

**The book, with the square and compasses upon it.**

What strikes me about this inventory is its restraint. There is nothing exotic in it.
Nothing is imported. Every object is either a builder's tool, a builder's garment, or a
Bible. In a century that produced a great deal of elaborate ceremonial in other contexts,
the Craft's stage kit is the contents of a workshop.

The exoticism arrives later, with the high degrees, and it arrives as *addition* — swords,
crowns, coffins, Hebrew letters, crosses, eagles. When you see those, you are looking at
the eighteenth century's contribution, not at the Craft's inheritance.

---

### Why theater rather than teaching

A question worth asking directly: why go to this trouble? If the aim is to convey that a
man should be upright, why not say so?

Three answers, all supported by what we can observe.

**Because it is remembered.** A proposition heard is forgotten; an event undergone is not.
This is not a mystical claim, and it does not require any theory of ritual beyond ordinary
observation of how memory works. The literature on memory is consistent that events
combining strong emotion, novelty and bodily involvement are retained far better than
information received passively.[^4] The Masonic initiation is engineered — deliberately or
by long refinement — to combine all three.

**Because it produces commitment.** A man who has been through something and sworn an oath
in the middle of it is invested in a way that a man who has read a pamphlet is not. This is
sociologically banal and it is why fraternities, regiments, medical residencies and
religious orders all do a version of it.

**And because it does not fix a meaning.** This is the subtle one and it is the point of
this chapter.

If the lodge taught doctrine, its members would have to agree with the doctrine. Some
would not. The institution would fracture along the same lines as the churches it had so
carefully declined to imitate.

Instead the lodge presents an *experience*, and leaves the interpretation open. The
candidate is raised from a symbolic grave. What that signifies — the immortality of the
soul, the moral resurrection of the reformed man, the death of the old self, the
persistence of a craft beyond its masters — is not specified. Every man supplies his own,
and every man is right, because nobody has been given the authority to be wrong.

Which is the same vacancy we found in Anderson's religious formula, and in the secret
itself, and it is now possible to state the pattern as a general property of this
institution:

**Freemasonry withholds meaning at every level, and derives its durability from doing so.**

That property is why it survived the Enlightenment, the Revolution, the Anti-Masonic
panics, two world wars and the twentieth century. It is also, precisely, why it could be
filled with theurgy in 1761 and with Hermetic Qabalah in 1888 — and why, when those things
were poured in, no one inside the institution had the standing to object.

We have the room, the blocking, the script and the drama. What we have not asked is why the
whole thing is dressed as *building*. Of all the trades in Europe, why did this one produce
an initiatory system — and why did the science it claims as its own turn out to be the one
science that had, for two thousand years, been considered a route to God?

---

# CHAPTER EIGHTEEN
## The Geometry of Initiation

Above the door of Plato's Academy, according to a tradition that is probably later than
Plato, was an inscription: *let no one ignorant of geometry enter here*.

Whether or not it was ever carved, the sentiment is authentic to the tradition. In the
*Republic*, Plato argues that geometry draws the soul toward truth, because it compels the
mind to contemplate objects that cannot be seen — the perfect circle, the line without
width — and thereby turns it away from the world of appearances. Geometry is not useful
knowledge. It is a training in looking at the right kind of thing.

Two thousand years later, in a tavern room in London, a candidate is told that Masonry is
founded on geometry, that geometry is the noblest of the sciences, and that by it the
mason learns to trace the works of the Almighty.

The line between those two rooms is not direct. But the reason both exist is the same, and
it is worth understanding, because it explains why — of all the crafts in Europe — this one
produced an initiatory philosophy and the tanners and coopers did not.

---

### Why geometry and not something else

Three reasons, and they compound.

**First: geometry was the one liberal art with a trade attached.**

The medieval curriculum divided knowledge into the trivium — grammar, rhetoric, logic — and
the quadrivium — arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy. These were the seven liberal
sciences: the knowledge proper to a free man, as against the mechanical arts of those who
worked with their hands.

Masonry sat, uniquely, on the boundary. A mason worked with his hands and was therefore a
mechanic. But what he did was *apply geometry*. He set out a right angle, struck an arc,
calculated a load, raised a vault. The Old Charges make the identification explicit and
repeatedly: masonry *is* geometry, the science is the craft, and the craft is the science.

No other trade could say this. A weaver has no liberal art. A blacksmith has no liberal
art. A mason has one, and the Old Charges make sure he knows it.

That single fact does more to explain the emergence of speculative Freemasonry than any
theory of ancient transmission. Here is a trade whose own founding documents tell its
members that they practice a branch of philosophy. Give that trade a lodge system, a
written record and an oath, and then let educated gentlemen hear the Charges read aloud at
an admission — and the attraction is not mysterious at all.

**Second: geometry was the century's prestige science.**

The lodge that produced the 1723 *Constitutions* was thick with Fellows of the Royal
Society. Desaguliers demonstrated Newtonian mechanics for a living. Newton had, in living
memory, shown that the motions of the heavens obeyed mathematics — that the universe was,
literally, a geometrical object. In that world, a fraternity organized around geometry was
not archaic. It was modern.

This is a point that gets lost when Freemasonry is read backward through its
nineteenth-century occult interpreters. The Grand Lodge of the 1720s was not looking
backward to Egypt. It was looking at Newton. Its Grand Master was a man who did experiments
in public. The symbolism of the square and compasses in that decade sits closer to the
Royal Society than to the Hermetic tradition, and the sensible reading of an institution
that describes God as the Great Architect of the Universe is that its members had read the
*Principia* and thought the universe looked designed.

**Third: geometry connected the workshop to God without any theology.**

And here is where it becomes useful in the way this book cares about.

The proposition *the universe is built according to measure* is available to a Catholic, an
Anglican, a Presbyterian, a Huguenot, a Jew and a deist. It is in the Book of Wisdom: God
ordered all things by measure and number and weight. It is in Plato's *Timaeus*. It is in
Newton. It commits you to nothing that divides Christians from each other or from anybody
else.

So the lodge acquired a sacred science that did not require a sacred doctrine — which is
precisely what Anderson's religious formula demanded. Geometry is the content that fits the
vacancy.

---

### The tools become moral

The mechanism by which this becomes a system of ethics is so simple that it is easy to
underrate.

A square tests whether an angle is true. A level tests whether a surface is horizontal. A
plumb-rule tests whether a line is vertical. A gauge divides a length. These are
instruments of *verification*: their entire function is to determine whether something
conforms to a standard that is external, impersonal and not negotiable.

Transfer that to conduct and you have a complete ethical vocabulary with no metaphysics in
it. To act on the square is to act correctly, and correctness is not a matter of opinion,
because a right angle is not a matter of opinion. To be upright is to be plumb. To meet
another man on the level is to acknowledge that the standard applies to both of you
regardless of rank — and in a society organized by rank, in a room containing a duke and an
apothecary, that is not a platitude.

This is the operation the Craft performs on every object it touches, and it is worth naming
because it recurs: **a physical instrument of measurement is converted into a moral
instrument of measurement, with the logic of verification carried across intact.**

It is not allegory in the loose sense of "this stands for that." It is an argument: the
moral order, like the physical one, has a standard against which things can be tested, and
the standard does not care who you are.

---

### The candidate as material

There is a second symbolic logic in the lodge, and it runs in the opposite direction.

The working tools are what the mason uses. But the mason is also, in the Craft's imagery,
what is worked on. The rough ashlar is a stone as it comes from the quarry: irregular,
unusable. The perfect ashlar is the same stone squared, smoothed and ready to be set into a
wall. The candidate is told that he is the first and should become the second.

So the initiate occupies both positions at once. He holds the square, and he is being
squared. He is the craftsman and the material.

This doubling is the most philosophically interesting thing in Craft symbolism, and it is
also the point at which the Masonic system comes closest to the traditions this book has
been tracking — without, so far as any evidence shows, having borrowed anything from them.

Consider. Alchemy's operator works on a material that the tradition tells him is also
himself. Iamblichus's theurgist performs an operation whose object is his own ascent.
Hermetic regeneration is a process the practitioner undergoes and conducts. And the Masonic
candidate is simultaneously mason and stone.

That is a real structural convergence, and I have no route to offer for it. Nobody has.
What I would suggest — and I offer it as a hypothesis, not a finding — is that it is a
convergence rather than a transmission: that any system which sets out to change a person
through their own deliberate action will arrive at the same doubling, because the doubling
is simply what self-transformation *is*. You cannot be improved without being both the
improver and the improved.

If that is right, then the resemblance between Masonry and the esoteric traditions at this
point is not a clue about the past. It is a fact about the logic of self-transformation,
which any sufficiently serious system will discover independently.

That hypothesis is one of the two or three things this book is genuinely arguing, and it
will be tested properly in Part VII.

---

### The building that is not there

One last observation before we go to the Temple.

Speculative Freemasonry is a system of building imagery practiced by people who do not
build anything.

The lodge has a floor, pillars, a covering of clouded canopy or starry heaven, a
foundation, and an orientation. It has tools and materials and a design. The candidate
learns to square a stone that does not exist for a wall that will not be built, using
instruments he will never employ, in a structure that is described in detail and is
nowhere.

I do not think this was felt as a loss. I think it is the point. Once the building is
imaginary, the vocabulary is free — it can be applied to a man's character, to a
congregation, to a nation, to the cosmos, to the soul. The Craft became universal at
exactly the moment it stopped being about construction.

And it had, ready to hand, the greatest imaginary building in the Western tradition: a
structure described in the Bible in obsessive dimensional detail, destroyed twice, never
excavated, and reconstructed on paper by Renaissance architects who had never seen a stone
of it.

---

### NOTES

[^1]: On geometry in the Old Charges and the identification of masonry with the liberal

1938).

[^2]: On the Royal Society and Newtonian circles in the early Grand Lodge, see Ric Berman,

Margaret C. Jacob, *The Radical Enlightenment* (London: Allen & Unwin, 1981).

[^3]: Wisdom of Solomon 11:20, "thou hast ordered all things in measure and number and

[^4]: The moral interpretation of the working tools is set out in the eighteenth-century

# CHAPTER NINETEEN
## Solomon's Temple

No one has ever seen it. No stone of it has been identified. It stood, if the biblical
chronology is even roughly right, for about three hundred and seventy years, and it was
destroyed by the Babylonians in 586 BCE. The building that replaced it was rebuilt again
by Herod and destroyed by the Romans in 70 CE. The site is among the most politically
impossible places on earth to excavate.

What survives is a description — two of them, in 1 Kings 6–7 and 2 Chronicles 3–4, with
further material in Ezekiel's visionary temple and in Josephus — and the descriptions do
not entirely agree.

From that, Western Europe built a thousand years of architecture, a theory of proportion,
a political vocabulary, and the central symbol of the world's largest fraternal
organization.

The Temple is the most productive building in history, and it is not there.

---

### What the text gives you

The biblical account is oddly specific about some things and silent about others. It gives
dimensions: the house sixty cubits long, twenty wide, thirty high. It gives the porch, the
side chambers, the windows, the cedar lining, the gold. It gives the inner sanctuary — the
holy of holies, a cube of twenty cubits, containing the ark and two enormous carved
cherubim.

And it gives two pillars, standing before the porch, cast in bronze, with elaborate
capitals of lily-work and pomegranates and chain-work. They are given names, which is
unusual and has never been satisfactorily explained: the right-hand pillar Jachin, the
left-hand pillar Boaz.[^1]

It also gives a workman. Solomon sends to Tyre for a man skilled in bronze — the text
calls him Hiram, or Huram, a widow's son of the tribe of Naphtali, whose father was a man
of Tyre — and this craftsman makes the pillars and the vessels.[^2]

That is the entire biblical basis for the Masonic legend. A craftsman named Hiram, of
mixed descent, a widow's son, working in bronze. He is not the architect. He is not
murdered. Nothing whatever happens to him. He makes the pillars and the text moves on.

What happens between that verse and the drama performed in a London tavern in 1730 is the
subject of Chapter 22. Here I want to establish something prior: why the *building* came to
carry so much weight.

---

### The perfect building

The reason is theological and it is very old.

If God specified the dimensions — and the text is emphatic that the plan was given, to
Moses for the tabernacle and to David for the Temple — then those dimensions are not
arbitrary. They are revealed proportion. A building made to divine specification is a
disclosure of divine order in the same way that scripture is.

From that premise, an entire tradition follows. If the Temple's proportions are revealed,
then reconstructing them is theology. If they encode a pattern, then the pattern may be
found elsewhere — in the human body, in the cosmos, in music. And if a Christian church
can be built to those proportions, then the church participates in the revelation.

Medieval and Renaissance architecture took all of this seriously and literally. The Temple
is invoked at Saint-Denis and in the cathedral tradition that followed. Renaissance
theorists of proportion worked with it. And in the 1590s and early 1600s, two Spanish
Jesuits, Jerónimo Prado and Juan Bautista Villalpando, produced an enormous illustrated
commentary on Ezekiel containing a full architectural reconstruction of the Temple —
engraved plans, elevations, sections, orders of columns — arguing that it had been built in
a divinely revealed order of which the classical orders were a degenerate memory.[^3]

Villalpando's Temple is a magnificent and entirely imaginary Renaissance palace. It was
also enormously influential: reproduced, copied, argued with, used by architects, and
consulted for two centuries.

So by 1700, educated Europe possessed a detailed visual idea of Solomon's Temple. It did
not come from archaeology. It came from a Jesuit's engravings, made from a prophet's vision
and a set of biblical measurements, in the architectural language of the late sixteenth
century.

When eighteenth-century Freemasons imagined the Temple, this is substantially what they
imagined.

---

### The claim to test

Which brings us to a claim worth taking seriously, because a version of it is made in a
great deal of popular writing: that Freemasonry preserves genuine knowledge of Solomon's
Temple — traditions, measurements, secrets of its construction, handed down through the
craft.

Run the questions.

**Provenance.** Is there any document, of any age, containing Temple information not
derivable from the Bible, Josephus and the Renaissance reconstructions? None has been
produced.

**Chronology.** For a tradition to carry genuine information from the tenth century BCE to
the eighteenth century CE, it must cross the Babylonian destruction, the exile, the Second
Temple period, the Roman destruction, and fifteen centuries of European history in which
the craft of building was repeatedly interrupted. Each of those is a break point.

**Transmission.** Nobody has proposed a route that survives inspection. The Templar version
— that the order excavated the Temple Mount and recovered something — is a modern
speculation with no medieval evidence, and we will deal with it in Chapter 26.

**Intertextuality.** This is where the claim dies. Everything in the Masonic Temple is
traceable to available sources. The dimensions are biblical. The pillars are biblical, with
their names. The craftsman is biblical. The architectural imagination is Villalpando's.
There is no residue — no detail that requires an independent source.

**Institutional continuity.** The masons' craft in medieval Europe is documented, and it is
a European building trade with European techniques and no Levantine connection.

**Anachronism.** The Masonic Temple has features that could not have belonged to the
original: it is furnished with the working tools of the early modern European building
trade, its pillars are described with globes on top — celestial and terrestrial globes,
which are early modern instruments — and its symbolism assumes the Renaissance
architectural orders.

**Verdict: unsupported, comprehensively.** The Masonic Temple is a *literary and
architectural* construction of the early modern period, built from the Bible and from
Renaissance reconstructions, and it contains nothing that requires an ancient channel.

---

### What it is actually for

And having said that, I want to say the other thing, because a book that stopped there
would have missed the point entirely.

The Temple's power in Masonic symbolism has nothing to do with whether the Craft knows
anything about it. It works *because* it is unavailable.

Consider its properties as a symbol.

**It is destroyed.** A building that still stood could be visited, measured, disagreed
about. A destroyed building is perfect, because nothing can contradict it. The Temple is
available only as description, which means it is available only as imagination.

**It was built to a given plan.** So it can stand for any project undertaken according to a
design not of one's own making — which is to say, for a life.

**It was built by a workforce.** The biblical account gives numbers: tens of thousands of
laborers, hewers, bearers of burdens, overseers. It is a *collective* achievement with a
division of labor and a hierarchy of skill — which maps onto a lodge of apprentices,
fellows and masters with no adjustment at all.

**It is sacred without being sectarian.** Jews, Christians and Muslims all recognize it.
Anderson's formula requires exactly this: a religious symbol available to everyone in the
room. There is no other building in the world that qualifies.

**It can be rebuilt inwardly.** Once the physical structure is gone, the injunction to
build the Temple becomes available as metaphor — and Paul had already made the move, in a
letter to Corinth, asking whether his readers did not know that they were the temple of
God. That metaphor was seventeen centuries old when the Craft picked it up. It was not an
esoteric secret. It was in every sermon.

Put those five properties together and you have the ideal symbolic object: universally
recognized, infinitely interpretable, incapable of refutation, and already pre-loaded with
the meaning *you are the building*.

The eighteenth century did not select the Temple because it possessed hidden knowledge of
it. It selected the Temple because the Temple was the best available machine for the job,
and because a craft of builders looking for the noblest building in its own tradition had
only one plausible candidate.

---

### The shape of the lodge

One consequence worth noting before we go on.

Because the Temple is the model, the lodge acquires its shape. The lodge is said to be
situated east to west, as the Temple was. It has pillars. It has a chequered pavement,
after the Temple's floor. It has a sanctuary at the east. The officers' stations correspond
to points in a building that no longer exists.

Which means that every Masonic lodge in the world is, formally, a model of a building known
only from a text — and every member who walks its floor is walking a reconstruction of a
reconstruction of a description.

That is a strange and rather beautiful fact, and it explains why the Craft's imagery proved
so portable. A lodge is not a place. It is a *plan*, and a plan can be laid out in a tavern
in Westminster, a warehouse in Boston, a tent in a colonial garrison, or a room above a
shop in Calcutta. The Temple travels because the Temple was never anywhere.

Now to its furniture — starting with the two instruments that have become, for most of the
world, the only thing anybody can identify about Freemasonry at all.

---

[^2]: 1 Kings 7:13–14; 2 Chronicles 2:13–14. The two accounts differ on the craftsman's

# CHAPTER TWENTY
## The Square and the Compasses

It is on gravestones in Ohio and on lodge doors in Ghana. It is on pocket watches, ties,
rings, car bumpers and the sides of buildings. It is one of the most widely recognized
emblems in the world, and the vast majority of people who recognize it could not say what
it means beyond "Freemasons."

Two draftsman's instruments, interlocked. Sometimes a letter G between them. Sometimes,
in American usage, the compasses are described as opened to a particular angle, and the
relative position of square and compasses varies with the degree.

Where did it come from, what did it mean, and when?

---

### The instruments

Both are ancient and both are unremarkable.

A square — two arms fixed at a right angle — is among the oldest tools in the world. It
exists wherever people build with rectangles, which is to say nearly everywhere. Egyptian
tomb paintings show them. Roman examples survive. Medieval European masons used them.

Compasses — two legs joined at a pivot, for striking arcs and transferring distances — are
equally widespread and equally old.

They belong together because they are the two instruments of practical geometry: one tests
and produces the straight and the right-angled, the other the curved and the measured. With
those two tools and a straight edge you can construct essentially the whole of Euclidean
plane geometry, which is why Euclid's constructions are called compass-and-straightedge
constructions.

So the pairing is not symbolic in origin. It is what is in the toolbox.

---

### Before the Craft

Now the part that requires discipline.

The square and compasses appear in European visual culture long before Freemasonry, and
this fact is routinely deployed as evidence of an ancient esoteric pedigree. Let us look at
what the appearances actually are.

**In craft heraldry and mason's marks.** Masons' guilds and companies used tools as
identifying devices, as every trade did — shears for cloth-workers, a last for cobblers.
The London Company of Masons' arms include compasses. Gravestones of masons across medieval
and early modern Europe are carved with the tools of the trade. This is professional
identification and nothing more.

**In images of God as geometer.** A striking medieval iconographic tradition shows the
Creator leaning from the heavens with a pair of compasses, measuring out the world — the
most famous English example being the frontispiece of the thirteenth-century *Bible
moralisée*, and the most famous later one William Blake's *Ancient of Days* of 1794, which
is a knowing reuse of the tradition.

This image is genuinely important for the intellectual background of Chapter 18, because
it visualises exactly the proposition that the universe was made by measure. But note
carefully: it shows *compasses*, used by *God*, in a *Christian devotional* context. It is
not a square-and-compasses emblem, it is not Masonic, and it is not esoteric. It is
scriptural illustration — Proverbs 8:27, "when he set a compass upon the face of the depth."

**In the wider symbolic repertoire.** Squares and compasses appear in Chinese iconography —
the mythological figures Fuxi and Nüwa are conventionally depicted holding a square and
compasses respectively, in imagery going back to the Han dynasty. This is a real and
striking parallel and it is frequently cited as evidence of a universal ancient tradition.

It is also, on inspection, exactly what the Parallel-Is-Not-Descent rule exists for. Two
civilisations that build with right angles and circles will both produce a square and a
pair of compasses, and both may use them to represent the ordering of the world, because
that is what the tools do. There is no transmission route, no chronology of contact
relevant to the emblem's Masonic use, and no textual connection. The resemblance tells us
something about the universality of construction. It tells us nothing about Freemasonry.

---

### When the Masonic meaning appears

The documented history is more modest and much more interesting.

The tools appear in the Craft's own early material as *working tools among others* — square,
level, plumb, gauge, gavel, compasses — each with a moral interpretation attached. The
square is early and prominent: to act "on the square" is idiomatic English by the
seventeenth century, and the phrase's currency shows how natural the moral transfer was.

The compasses acquire their distinctive Masonic meaning in the eighteenth-century ritual
material: to circumscribe desires and keep passions within due bounds. The image is
precise — a pair of compasses defines a limit, and the moral point is limitation rather
than measurement.

The combination of square and compasses with the open book as the "Three Great Lights"
belongs to the eighteenth century and is attested in the exposures and the ritual
literature of that period.[^1] The emblem as a *logo* — square and compasses interlocked,
standing alone, used as an identifying badge on buildings and objects — becomes standard in
the nineteenth century, with the growth of Masonic material culture, printing and
regalia-manufacturing.

The letter G is later still in its prominence, and is discussed in the next chapter.

**So the dating is: tools, always. Moral interpretation, from at least the early
eighteenth century and probably before. The emblem as a public badge, nineteenth
century.**

---

### The later overlay

And then, from the middle of the nineteenth century, something is added that is not in the
eighteenth-century material at all.

In the occult reading — Lévi, and everyone downstream of Lévi — the square and compasses
become a polarity. The square is matter, the compasses spirit; the square is passive, the
compasses active; the square is the terrestrial, the compasses the celestial; and their
interlocking is the union of opposites, the conjunction, the hexagram of ascending and
descending triangles.

This is a genuinely elegant reading. It is also demonstrably a nineteenth-century addition.
The eighteenth-century sources contain nothing of it. The moral interpretation they give is
the one described above — rectitude and restraint — and it is notably unmetaphysical.

The polarity reading arrives with the occult revival and is carried into American Masonry
by Pike, who took a great deal from Lévi. Within two generations it had become, for many
Masons, simply what the emblem meant.

I want to be careful not to sneer at this. Symbols acquire meanings; that is what symbols
are for, and a meaning discovered in 1856 is not a lesser meaning than one recorded in
1730. The error is not interpretation. The error is *dating* — the assumption that because
a reading is profound, it is old.

---

### A note on the relative position

One small point of Masonic practice, included because it illustrates how differently these
things work from the outside.

In many jurisdictions, the position of the compasses relative to the square on the open
volume of sacred law differs by degree: both points hidden, one point revealed, both points
revealed. It is a simple, elegant index of progression, immediately legible to anyone who
knows the system and invisible to anyone who does not.

This is characteristic of how the Craft actually uses its symbolism. The meaning is not
hidden in the sense of being encoded. It is hidden in the sense of being *unremarked*. A
man who has been through the degrees sees a lodge board and reads information from it; a
visitor sees a decorative arrangement. No cipher is involved. The knowledge is entirely
contextual, and that is a very different thing from a secret.

It also varies by jurisdiction, like almost everything else in this book, and any account
that presents one national practice as universal is wrong before it starts.

---

### What the emblem actually tells us

Step back and ask what the square and compasses, taken as historical evidence, establish.

They establish that Freemasonry's self-image is **a craft, not a cult**. The badge is two
tools. Not a symbol of a deity, not a mystical sign, not a letter of a sacred alphabet: the
contents of a workman's bag.

They establish that the Craft's ethics are **verificationist**. The tools test. The moral
system they carry is one of conformity to an external standard that does not care about
your opinion.

And they establish that the Craft was **content to be legible**. A body seriously concerned
with concealment does not put its emblem on the front of its buildings.

That last point is worth holding onto when we reach the conspiracy literature in Chapter
50. The organization accused of running the world by stealth has, for two centuries,
advertised its premises with a sign, published its constitutions, listed its officers in
directories, and marched in public processions in full regalia at the laying of corner
stones.

There is a great deal that is genuinely hidden in the history this book is telling. It is
almost never hidden by the Freemasons.

---

[^1]: On the Three Great Lights and the eighteenth-century ritual usage, see Samuel

[^2]: Proverbs 8:27 (Authorised Version): "when he set a compass upon the face of the

edn., ed. Arturo de Hoyos (Washington, D.C.: Supreme Council, 33°, S.J., 2011).
