# CHAPTER TWENTY-ONE
## The Pillars, the Ladder, the Star

This chapter is a dating exercise, and it is deliberately unglamorous.

For each of six symbols, three questions: what does the Masonic tradition say it means;
when does that meaning first appear in a source we can hold; and what has been added
since, by whom, and when.

The result is a stratigraphy. Symbols that look like a single ancient system turn out to
have been deposited in layers, over about two hundred years, by identifiable processes. And
the layers do not run in the direction most people expect: the elements that feel most
archaic are frequently the most recent.

---

### The Pillars

**The claim.** Two pillars stand at the entrance of the lodge, as they stood at the porch
of Solomon's Temple. They are named. In most workings they carry globes — one celestial,
one terrestrial.

**The source.** Direct and unambiguous: 1 Kings 7:15–22 and 2 Chronicles 3:15–17. The
pillars, their bronze, their capitals, their lily-work and pomegranates, and their names —
Jachin on the right, Boaz on the left — are all biblical. This is one of the rare
occasions in this book where a Masonic symbol has a completely documented ancestor, and
the ancestor is scripture, available to every member in his own house.

**The dating.** The pillars are in the earliest Masonic material we have. The Scottish
catechisms of the 1690s are concerned with them. They are in the English exposures from
the 1720s. There is no period of Masonic history without them.

**The additions.** Two, both later and both traceable.

The *globes* are early modern. Terrestrial and celestial globes were instruments and status
objects of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries — you can see them in portraits of
natural philosophers. Putting them atop the pillars converts a biblical architectural
feature into an emblem of universal knowledge, and it is an eighteenth-century decision. A
pillar of the tenth century BCE did not have a globe on it, and the anachronism is worth
noticing precisely because nobody minds it: the Craft was never attempting historical
reconstruction.

The *Kabbalistic reading* — the pillars as Severity and Mercy, the two outer columns of the
Sephirotic Tree, with a middle pillar of equilibrium between them — is nineteenth century.
It is Lévi's world, transmitted through Pike and the Golden Dawn. It is not in any
eighteenth-century Masonic source. It is now so widespread that many Masons encounter it
before they encounter 1 Kings.

**Verdict:** biblical descent, documented; Kabbalistic interpretation, Victorian.

---

### Jacob's Ladder

**The claim.** A ladder reaching from earth to heaven, with three principal rungs — Faith,
Hope and Charity — appears on the first-degree tracing board.

**The source.** Genesis 28:12, Jacob's dream at Bethel: a ladder set up on the earth with
its top reaching to heaven, and the angels of God ascending and descending on it. The three
virtues are 1 Corinthians 13:13.

**The dating.** The ladder is not in the earliest catechisms. It appears in the developed
tracing-board imagery of the later eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries, when the
visual symbolism of the degrees was being elaborated and standardized.[^1]

**The additions.** The mapping of the ladder onto a Kabbalistic or planetary ascent —
seven rungs for seven spheres, in the Hermetic manner of Chapter 6 — is an occult reading
of the nineteenth century. There is an obvious *structural* affinity between a ladder of
ascent and the Neoplatonic and Hermetic ladders, and I do not want to pretend otherwise.
But affinity is not derivation: the biblical image was universally available, it required
no esoteric source, and the Masonic version's three rungs are Pauline, not planetary.

**Verdict:** biblical descent; the planetary and Sephirotic mappings are later overlays.

---

### The Blazing Star

**The claim.** A star, usually five-pointed, in the center of the lodge floor or on the
tracing board. Interpretations offered in the Masonic sources include Providence, the
guiding star, the sun, and glory.

**The dating.** Present in eighteenth-century material; the interpretations offered in that
period are broad and vary between workings, which is itself informative — the Craft's own
sources are not consistent about what it means.

**The additions.** The identification of the five-pointed star with the *pentagram* as a
magical figure, and the elaborate discussion of its point-up and point-down orientations as
signifying spirit over matter or the reverse, is Lévi. He published the contrast in the
1850s, and it is one of the single most successful pieces of nineteenth-century occult
image-making: the point-down pentagram as an emblem of evil is, so far as anyone has
traced, his invention and has no earlier pedigree.[^2]

The star was in the lodge for a century before it acquired that freight.

**Verdict:** old within Masonry, ambiguous in its own sources, and given its most famous
meaning by a French author in 1856.

---

### The Letter G

**The claim.** The letter G, suspended in the east of the lodge or set within the square
and compasses, signifying Geometry, and also God.

**The dating.** Eighteenth century. It is in the exposures.

**The observation that settles everything.** The symbol is a letter of the Roman alphabet
whose meaning depends on the initial letter of two English words, *Geometry* and *God*.

That single fact is the most efficient demonstration in this book of the anachronism test
from Chapter 5. The symbol cannot be ancient. It cannot be Egyptian, Hebrew, Phoenician,
Greek or Templar, because in none of those languages does the relevant concept begin with
that letter. It is a symbol that could only have been generated by English-speaking people,
and it therefore dates from the period in which English-speaking people were making Masonic
symbols, which is the eighteenth century.

The point survives translation, too: in French lodges the letter persists, sometimes with
*Gloire*, *Grandeur*, *Génie* supplied — that is, the letter traveled as a letter and
acquired new meanings to justify itself, which is exactly backward from how an ancient
symbol behaves.

**The additions.** The occult readings are numerous and inventive: the Hebrew letter *yod*,
the generative principle, the number three, the tetragrammaton. All are nineteenth century
or later, and all are attempts to give an English initial an ancient parentage.

**Verdict: demonstrably modern, and the cleanest available proof that at least some Masonic
symbolism was manufactured in the eighteenth century.**

---

### Sun and Moon

**The claim.** Sun and moon appear among the lodge's "lesser lights," with the Master; the
sun to rule the day, the moon the night.

**The source.** Genesis 1:16 supplies the formula almost verbatim.

**The dating.** Eighteenth century, in the catechisms.

**The additions.** The alchemical reading — Sol and Luna, the king and queen, the
conjunction of opposites — maps onto the Masonic pair without difficulty, and has been
applied to it constantly since the nineteenth century. As with the pillars, the mapping is
elegant. As with the pillars, the eighteenth-century Masonic sources give no sign of it.
The Craft's own explanation is calendrical and biblical: these are the two lights God set
in the firmament, and the Master rules the lodge as they rule day and night.

**Verdict:** biblical; the alchemical reading is an overlay.

---

### The All-Seeing Eye

Kept for last, because it is the symbol about which the public is most confident and the
evidence is least satisfactory.

**The claim.** An eye, often within a triangle, often radiant, signifying the watchfulness
of God.

**The source.** Christian devotional iconography. The Eye of Providence in a triangle —
the triangle for the Trinity — is a commonplace of European religious art from the
Renaissance onward. It is on churches, on tombs, in Bibles, in emblem books. It was not
invented by, and was never proprietary to, Freemasonry.

**The dating within Masonry.** Later than most people assume. It is not in the earliest
Masonic material; it appears in the second half of the eighteenth century, and it becomes
prominent in the nineteenth with the growth of printed and manufactured Masonic imagery.
Establishing the precise earliest datable appearance in Masonic material culture — as
opposed to commentary — requires work on tracing boards, aprons and certificates that I
have not been able to complete, and I flag it as an open question rather than guess.[^3]

**The additions.** Here the overlay is not occult but conspiratorial, and it is very recent.
The identification of the Eye with the Bavarian Illuminati, and the reading of the reverse
of the Great Seal of the United States as a Masonic-Illuminati emblem, belongs
substantially to the twentieth century and overwhelmingly to the period after about 1960.

The Great Seal's designers — a committee process running from 1776 to 1782, with the final
design by Charles Thomson, Secretary of Congress — used the Eye of Providence because it
was a standard emblem of divine favor in the visual vocabulary of the period. Thomson was
not a Freemason.[^4] The unfinished pyramid comes from a different committee proposal
entirely. The design was not reproduced on the dollar bill until 1935.

**Verdict:** Christian iconographic descent, documented; Masonic adoption in the later
eighteenth century; conspiratorial reading entirely modern.

---

### The stratigraphy

Set the six side by side and the pattern is unmistakable.

| Symbol | Documented source | In Masonry by | Principal later overlay |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pillars | 1 Kings / 2 Chron. | 1690s | Kabbalistic pillars, C19 |
| Jacob's Ladder | Genesis 28; 1 Cor. 13 | later C18 | planetary/Sephirotic ascent, C19 |
| Blazing Star | — (broad) | C18 | Lévi's pentagram, 1856 |
| Letter G | English initials | C18 | Hebrew *yod* etc., C19 |
| Sun and Moon | Genesis 1 | C18 | alchemical Sol/Luna, C19 |
| All-Seeing Eye | Christian art | later C18 | Illuminati reading, C20 |

Every documented source is biblical or ordinary Christian iconography. Every esoteric
reading is nineteenth century. Every conspiratorial reading is twentieth.

Nothing in the Craft's symbolic vocabulary requires an ancient hidden channel, because
everything in it was in a book the members already owned.

That is the finding of this chapter, and it is worth stating without triumph. It does not
make the symbols less interesting. It makes them differently interesting: they are not an
inheritance, they are a *construction*, and we can watch it being built.

Which leaves the one symbol that cannot be handled this way, because its source is not in
the Bible at all. There is a craftsman named Hiram in 1 Kings who makes two pillars and
then disappears from the narrative alive.

Somebody killed him. We do not know who, or when, or why.

---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-TWO
## Hiram

The central legend of Freemasonry has no author, no manuscript, and no datable moment of
composition. It appears, complete, in a hostile pamphlet printed in London in 1730, and
before that there is nothing.

This is the most remarkable fact in Masonic history, and it is remarkable in a way that
cuts against both the parties usually arguing about it. The ancient-wisdom school has to
explain why a legend supposedly carried from antiquity leaves no trace until the reign of
George II. The skeptical school has to explain how a drama of that quality was composed by
persons unknown, adopted across an entire institution within a few years, and left no
draft, no attribution and no record of the decision.

Neither has managed it.

---

### The legend

As given in *Masonry Dissected* and in the developed workings that followed, the story runs
roughly as follows. I summarise rather than quote, and I note again that versions differ
substantially between jurisdictions.

At the building of Solomon's Temple, three grand masters hold the secrets of the master's
degree: Solomon, Hiram King of Tyre, and Hiram Abiff, the chief architect. Fifteen fellows
of the craft, impatient for the master's word before they have earned it, conspire to
obtain it by force; twelve of them repent, three do not. The three accost Hiram Abiff at
the three gates of the Temple as he leaves at high twelve. Each demands the secret; he
refuses; each strikes him, and the third blow kills him.

They bury the body, hastily, and mark the grave. The workmen, finding the master missing,
search. The grave is discovered — in most versions by a sprig of acacia which the murderers
had planted or which had grown there. The body is raised. Because the master's word died
with him, a substitute word is adopted.

And the candidate plays Hiram.

---

### What is and is not biblical

The raw material is scriptural and the plot is not.

Scripture gives a craftsman named Hiram or Huram, sent from Tyre, a widow's son, skilled in
bronze, who makes the pillars and the vessels of the Temple. That is all. He is not called
an architect. He is not murdered. Nothing in the Bible or in Josephus supplies the
conspiracy, the three blows, the burial, the acacia, the search or the raising.

The name *Abiff* is not biblical either in the form the Craft uses; it derives from a
Hebrew phrase in 2 Chronicles rendered variously in different versions — the Geneva Bible's
handling of the passage is generally taken to be the immediate source of the Masonic
form.[^1]

So the legend takes a biblical person, gives him a promotion, and then constructs an
entirely non-biblical drama around him.

---

### The candidates for a source

Four proposals have been made seriously. Let us take them in order.

**One: it descends from the ancient mysteries.** The dying-and-rising figure — Osiris,
Adonis, Attis, Tammuz — is offered as the archetype, with Masonry preserving a mystery-cult
initiation.

This fails on everything except chronology. There is no transmission route, no textual
relation, no institutional continuity, and no evidence that eighteenth-century Englishmen
had access to the content of the ancient mysteries, which were genuinely secret and are
poorly attested even now. The comparison was made *by* eighteenth- and nineteenth-century
writers — it is in the Masonic literature from the 1770s onward, and it is everywhere in
Pike — but it is their interpretation, not their source. They found the parallel, as we
would, by reading about Osiris; they did not inherit it.

**Two: it is alchemical.** Handled in Chapter 11. The parallel is real and the descent is
unsupported.

**Three: it derives from the Templars.** Chronologically impossible in any documented form,
and the Templar material enters Masonry in the 1730s and after — *later* than the Hiramic
legend, not earlier. Chapter 26.

**Four: it was composed in the 1720s, in England, by persons unknown, out of biblical
material, the craft's existing legends, and the dramatic repertoire of the period.**

The fourth is the only one that fits the evidence, and it is worth noticing that it is also
the only one nobody finds satisfying. There is something about "we do not know who wrote
it" that the human mind refuses, and a great deal of Masonic historiography is the record
of that refusal.

---

### Why the 1720s

The case for composition in that decade rests on four things.

The earliest catechisms — Scottish, 1696 to about 1714 — describe *two* degrees and contain
no Hiramic legend. There is a word, and the tradition of a lost or substituted word appears
in embryo, but there is no murder and no raising.

The earliest English exposures — 1723, 1724 — also show two degrees and no legend.

*Masonry Dissected*, 1730, has three degrees and the full legend.

And Grand Lodge's own records show the third degree being worked in London lodges from the
mid-1720s onward, with the practice spreading through that decade.[^2]

A window of roughly six years, in one city, at the exact moment when a new Grand Lodge was
standardizing and publishing and expanding — and when, not coincidentally, London theater
was in one of its most vigorous periods.

---

### What kind of object it is

I want to make an observation about the legend as a piece of construction, because it is
frequently discussed for its supposed sources and rarely for its craftsmanship.

It is extremely well made.

Consider the problem its author faced. The Craft had two degrees, a word, and a great deal
of moral instruction. It needed a third degree — the pressure for one seems to have come
from the ordinary desire for further advancement — and that degree had to do something the
first two did not.

The solution chosen was to give the candidate an *experience of loss*. Not of gain: the
third degree is the only one in which the candidate does not receive the thing he came for.
The master's word is lost. It died with Hiram. What he is given is a substitute, explicitly
described as such.

That is a genuinely unusual design decision, and I think it is the reason the degree works.
A ceremony that promised a secret and delivered one would be a transaction. A ceremony that
promises a secret, kills the only man who has it, and then hands the candidate a
placeholder — while he is lying on the floor playing the corpse — is doing something else
entirely. It is teaching that the thing sought is not available, that the search is the
inheritance, and that what is transmitted is the search.

Whether or not its author intended that, it is what the degree performs. And it is, I
think, the single most interesting idea in Freemasonry.

It also explains a great deal about what happened next. An institution whose central
ceremony declares that the true word is lost has built an open question into its own
foundations. For two hundred years, men would enter that ceremony and come out convinced
that something was missing — because they had been *told* something was missing — and some
of them would go looking for it.

They looked in the high degrees, which promised to restore the word. They looked in the
Royal Arch, whose entire subject is the recovery of the lost name. They looked in Kabbalah,
in Egypt, in the Templars, in Hermeticism, in Theosophy, in the Golden Dawn.

The occult history of Freemasonry is, in a real sense, the history of men taking the third
degree literally.

---

### The acacia

A small closing observation.

The grave is found by a sprig of acacia. The acacia is evergreen, which is the ordinary
symbolism of immortality; it is also, and more interestingly, the shittim wood of the
Tabernacle, the timber God specified for the ark and its poles in Exodus.

But the function in the drama is not symbolic. It is *forensic*. The murderers bury the
body and something growing marks the place, and the searchers find it because of a detail
the murderers did not control.

That is the structure of the entire ceremony in miniature: the thing that was hidden is
found because burial is imperfect, and something always comes up through the ground.

I would not put much weight on that as an interpretation of the legend. I put a great deal
of weight on it as a description of this book's method. Institutions that conceal
themselves leave sprigs. The Scottish lodges kept minutes because an administrator told
them to. Anderson published a constitution. Prichard printed the secrets for sixpence.
Stukeley wrote in his diary. Taxil confessed in a Paris hall. Westcott's German letters
were examined and the German was wrong.

Something always comes up through the ground. That is why this history can be written at
all.

---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE
## What the Exposures Reveal

Between 1723 and 1762, in London and Paris, a small industry produced pamphlets telling the
public what happened inside a Masonic lodge. They were written by men who had been inside,
or claimed to have been, and they were sold cheaply to a public that wanted to know.

They are the most valuable documents in early Masonic history, and they are valuable for a
reason their authors did not intend. Because they were published, they are *dated*. And
because they cover four decades, they let us watch the ritual change.

This chapter is the payoff of that. It is short, and it is the evidentiary spine of Part
III.

---

### The sequence

**1723 — *A Mason's Examination*.** Printed in *The Flying Post*, a London newspaper, in
April. Brief. A catechism of questions and answers. Two degrees.[^1]

**1724 — *The Grand Mystery of Free-Masons Discover'd*.** Fuller. Still two degrees.

**1730 — *Masonry Dissected*, by Samuel Prichard.** The watershed. Three degrees, the full
catechism for each, and the Hiramic legend. Three editions in under a fortnight, and
reprinted for the rest of the century.

**1737–1745 — the French wave.** Several exposures appear in Paris, culminating in the
Abbé Pérau's *Le Secret des Francs-Maçons* (1742) and *L'Ordre des Francs-Maçons Trahi*
(1745). These are important because they show the ritual after its transplantation to
France and before the high degrees have taken hold.

**1760 — *Three Distinct Knocks*.** An English exposure of the "Antients" working.

**1762 — *Jachin and Boaz*.** The most commercially successful of all, reprinted for
decades, translated, and pirated.[^2]

Set them in a row and four things become visible that cannot be seen any other way.

---

### Finding one: the third degree is new

The 1723 and 1724 texts describe two degrees. The Scottish catechisms of 1696–1714 describe
two degrees. The 1730 text describes three, with a legend.

There is no earlier witness to the third degree, in any country, in any form.

This is negative evidence, and negative evidence needs care — but it is unusually strong
here, because of *who was writing*. These were commercial publications competing for a
scandal-hungry market. An author who knew of a dramatic ritual involving a murder and a
resurrection and did not print it would have been leaving money on the table. Prichard
printed it the moment it existed, and his sales prove the point.

So: the central ceremony of Freemasonry is a product of the 1720s. Everything built on the
assumption that it is ancient is built on sand.

---

### Finding two: the symbolism grows

Track individual elements across the sequence and the accretion is plain.

The 1723 catechism is spare: the lodge, its form, its supports, the signs, the words. By
1730 there is more furniture. By 1762 there is a great deal more — lesser lights, jewels,
ornaments, the moral interpretations of the tools, the ladder, and the elaborated
descriptions that would become the eighteenth-century lectures.

Nothing is subtracted. Things are added, decade by decade, and the additions are of a
consistent kind: more symbols, more interpretation, more explanation of what things mean.

This tells us something structural about how Masonic ritual develops. It does not evolve by
mutation of an original core. It **accumulates**, and it accumulates *commentary*. The
Craft's history is a history of people explaining it to each other, and the explanations
becoming part of it.

Which is, incidentally, exactly the mechanism by which nineteenth-century occult
interpretations would enter and become traditional. The system was built to absorb
commentary. It had been doing it since the 1720s.

---

### Finding three: what is not there, for forty years

Now the list that matters most for this book's argument.

Across every English and French exposure from 1723 to 1762, the following do not appear:

Kabbalah. The Sephirot. The Tree of Life. Gematria or any operation on Hebrew letters.
Planetary correspondences. Astrological hours. Angelic or spirit names used operatively.
Magic circles. Talismans. Alchemical terminology — no sulphur, mercury, salt, nigredo,
prima materia, philosophers' stone. Egyptian gods or Egyptian mysteries. Any reference to
the Knights Templar. Any reference to Rosicrucians.

None of it. In forty years of hostile and commercially motivated publication across two
countries.

I want to be exact about what this establishes and what it does not.

It establishes that **the Craft degrees, as worked in England and France between 1723 and
1762, contained no esoteric content of the kind later attributed to them.** That is as well
evidenced as anything in this book.

It does not establish that no individual Mason held esoteric views. Plenty did; Europe was
full of people with such views, and some of them were Masons, exactly as some were
clergymen and some were customs officials.

And it does not cover the high degrees, which are beginning to appear in France precisely
during the later part of this window, and which are a different subject entirely — Part IV.

But for the Craft itself, in its formative and its first expansive period, the negative
finding is firm. The furniture was a Bible, a square, a pair of compasses, some builder's
tools and a legend about a murdered architect.

---

### Finding four: publication did nothing

The last finding is the one this book began with in Chapter 3, and it can now be stated
with the evidence in hand.

Every secret was public from 1730. The most complete exposure was the most successful book.
The French translations put the material across Europe. *Jachin and Boaz* was still being
reprinted in the 1790s.

And in the same period, Freemasonry went from a few London lodges to an international
institution with tens of thousands of members, aristocratic and royal patronage, and a
presence on four continents.

Publication did not damage it. If anything, the exposures functioned as advertising: a man
who read *Jachin and Boaz* out of curiosity now knew what the ceremony was, and some
proportion of such men decided they would rather undergo it than read about it.

Which is the correct conclusion, and it took an industry of pamphleteers forty years to
demonstrate it: **you cannot expose an experience.**

---

### A caution about the sources

These are not neutral documents and should not be read as transcripts.

They were written to sell. Some authors padded. Some worked from memory and got things
wrong. Some included material from lodges that did other things, and some may have invented
where their memory failed. Prichard swore an affidavit of accuracy before a magistrate,
which tells us he anticipated the objection, not that he was right.

The method for using them is comparative. Where three independent exposures agree, the
element is probably real. Where one has something the others lack, it may be local
practice, or it may be invention. Where a French text differs from an English one, it may
reflect genuine divergence in practice between the countries — which by the 1740s was
substantial.

Handled that way, they are excellent evidence. Handled as scripture, they produce the
confident errors that fill the popular literature.

---

### What Part III has established

We can now state the position at the close of the Craft's formative period, entirely from
documents.

Freemasonry as an institution derives from a Scottish lodge system given permanent form by
royal statute in 1598–99, combined with an English manuscript tradition of craft legend
running back to about 1390, and reorganised in London in the early 1720s by a circle of
Newtonian and Whig gentlemen into a Grand Lodge with a printed constitution.

Its founding document declares a religious minimum so broad as to be almost empty, which
purchases unprecedented breadth of membership at the cost of doctrinal definition.

Its ritual is a dramatic technology of remarkable effectiveness, using darkness, ordeal,
oath and staged revelation, culminating in a legend of death and raising whose central
declaration is that the true word is lost.

Its symbolism is drawn, item by item and demonstrably, from the Bible, from ordinary
Christian iconography, and from the tools of the building trade.

And it contains, in forty years of published evidence, no Hermeticism, no Kabbalah, no
alchemy, no ceremonial magic, no Egypt, no Templars and no Rosicrucians.

That is the machine. It is now built, it is spreading across Europe at extraordinary speed,
and it has three properties that will determine everything that happens next: it is
*graded*, so it can be extended; it is *secret*, so extensions are hard to audit; and it is
*empty*, so the extensions can contain anything at all.

Within fifteen years of Prichard's pamphlet, Frenchmen will begin adding degrees. Within
thirty, a Bordeaux merchant will be performing angelic invocations inside a Masonic
structure. Within fifty, a Prussian order will require Masonic membership before teaching
alchemy.

None of that came out of the lodge.

All of it went in.

---

# PART IV — THE GREAT EXPANSION

# CHAPTER TWENTY-FOUR
## The Eighteenth-Century Explosion

In 1730 a Freemason had three degrees available to him.

By 1780 a French or German Mason could, if he had the time, the money and the appetite,
work through Scottish degrees, Elect degrees, Templar degrees, Rose-Croix degrees,
Kadosh degrees, Egyptian degrees, Hermetic degrees, Rectified degrees, philosophical
degrees, chapters, councils, areopagi, consistories and tribunals, ascending through
numbered grades into the twenties, thirties and — in some systems — the nineties.

One estimate puts the number of distinct degrees invented in eighteenth-century France at
well over a thousand. Nobody has ever produced a complete list, and nobody ever will,
because a great many were worked once, in one place, by a dozen men, and left no trace but
a manuscript in a drawer.

This is the most important development in the history of Freemasonry after the
institution's own formation, and it is the least discussed outside specialist literature.
It is also the point at which the subject of this book actually begins — because it is in
these degrees, and not in the Craft, that Western esoteric material enters Masonic
structures.

---

### The mechanism

How does an institution go from three degrees to a thousand in fifty years?

The answer is embarrassingly simple, and it explains the whole phenomenon.

**Nobody owned the copyright.**

Freemasonry had no central authority outside its own jurisdictions, no doctrinal office, no
mechanism for saying *this is not Masonry*. The English Grand Lodge governed its own
lodges. It had no power in France, and by the 1740s it could not have exercised any if it
had. A group of men in Bordeaux or Lyon or Berlin who wished to constitute a new degree
needed nothing but each other's agreement.

And the Craft's own structure invited it. A three-stage system in which the third stage
declares that the true secret is *lost* is a system with an unresolved ending. It is an
invitation, written into the ritual, to ask what was lost and whether it can be found.

The high degrees are, almost without exception, answers to that question. The Royal Arch
recovers the lost word. The Elect degrees avenge the murder. The Templar degrees supply a
continuing order that preserved what was hidden. The Rose-Croix supplies a Christian
fulfillment. Each takes the hole at the center of the third degree and fills it.

---

### The market

That is the structural explanation. There is also a social one, and it is less edifying.

Eighteenth-century France was a society organized by rank and obsessed with it. Precedence,
title, the right to a particular seat or a particular form of address, the distinction
between the noblesse d'épée and the noblesse de robe and the men who were neither — this
was the texture of daily life for the class of people joining lodges.

A system that offers *advancement* to men who had nowhere else to advance is a system with
a market. A merchant who could never be a marquis could become a Knight of the East, a
Prince of Jerusalem, a Sublime Prince of the Royal Secret. He could wear a sash and a
jewel. He could outrank, within the lodge, men who outranked him outside it.

I do not say this cynically, and the mockery it attracted at the time was often
snobbish. But any honest account has to include it, because the degree titles themselves
make it plain. They are not the vocabulary of mysticism. They are the vocabulary of
chivalry and court: Knight, Prince, Sovereign, Grand Elect, Sublime.

There was also, straightforwardly, money. Degrees cost fees. A man who possessed a system
and the authority to confer it had a business. Some of the eighteenth century's more
enterprising degree-mongers traveled Europe selling patents, and at least one — the
Chevalier Ramsay's less scrupulous successors — did very well out of it.

---

### The mythologies

What the degrees actually *contained* varied enormously, and the variety is the point of
Part IV. But the mythological repertoire can be summarised, because it is surprisingly
small.

**The chivalric.** Masonry descends from crusading knights, and the Craft degrees are the
outer court of a knightly order. This is Ramsay's contribution and it is the largest single
source of eighteenth-century high-degree material.

**The Templar.** A specific and more aggressive version of the chivalric: Masonry *is* the
Knights Templar, surviving in concealment since 1314, preserving a secret and awaiting
restoration. This is von Hund's, and it comes with a claim of living authority.

**The vengeance narratives.** The Elect degrees pursue and punish the murderers of Hiram.
These are among the earliest high degrees and their appeal is dramatic rather than
doctrinal.

**The Christian-mystical.** The Rose-Croix degrees reinterpret the Masonic legend in
Christian terms: the lost word is the Word, the Temple is the Church, the raising is the
Resurrection. This is theologically serious and it pulled Masonry back toward a
confessional identity that Anderson had carefully removed.

**The Hermetic and alchemical.** Degrees containing genuine esoteric instruction —
alchemical, Kabbalistic, theurgic. These are the minority, but they are the reason this
book exists.

**The Egyptian.** Cagliostro's rite and its relatives. Late, theatrical, and entirely
eighteenth-century in its Egypt.

---

### What this does to the argument

Here is why Part IV is the hinge of this book.

In Part III we established that the Craft contained no esoteric material between 1723 and
1762, on the evidence of four decades of published exposures. That finding holds, and it is
firm.

But it would be dishonest to stop there and declare that Freemasonry has nothing to do with
Western esotericism, because from the 1740s onward there are Masonic bodies that
unambiguously do. The Élus Coëns performed theurgic operations. The Gold- und Rosenkreuz
taught alchemy. Willermoz's Rectified Rite carried a complete Christian-theosophical
doctrine of the fall and reintegration of souls.

So both statements are true, and the apparent contradiction dissolves once you separate the
Craft from what was built on top of it:

**The Craft supplied a form and no content. The high degrees supplied content — and the
content came from outside.**

That is the carriage thesis in its cleanest form, and the eighteenth century is where it
can be demonstrated, because — and this is the crucial point — **we can usually name the
people who did it.**

This is not a case of tracing influence through the mists. Ramsay gave a speech; we have
the text and the date. Von Hund made a claim; we know what he could not produce. Martinez
de Pasqually founded an order in 1761; we have its documents and its doctrine. The Gold-
und Rosenkreuz reorganised itself in 1777; McIntosh has been through its papers.

These are not survivals from antiquity. They are compositions, by named authors, in known
years, for reasons we can often reconstruct.

---

### A word about quality

One more thing before we start naming names.

There is a tendency, in both Masonic and skeptical writing, to treat the high degrees as an
eighteenth-century absurdity — a proliferation of costumes and titles that sensible people
eventually pruned back. The English response was essentially that: the union of 1813
declared that pure Ancient Masonry consists of three degrees and no more, including the
Royal Arch, and English Freemasonry has been comparatively austere ever since.

That judgment is understandable and it is also a loss. Some of the high-degree material is
junk — repetitive, derivative, invented on Tuesday to be worked on Thursday. Some of it is
not. The Rectified Scottish Rite's doctrine is a coherent and serious piece of Christian
esoteric theology. The Rose-Croix ceremony is, by common consent even among people who
dislike its theology, one of the most powerful rituals ever composed. The Royal Arch is
strange and deep and nobody knows where it came from.

Dismissing the whole phenomenon as costume drama makes the history unintelligible, because
it cannot explain why serious people — including men of real learning and real piety — gave
decades of their lives to it.

They gave those decades because the third degree had told them something was lost, and
because they believed, with varying degrees of evidence and none of it good, that they knew
where to look.

The man who first told them where to look gave a speech in Paris in the winter of 1736, and
he was a Scottish Jacobite tutor with a Catholic conversion, a doctorate from Oxford, and a
gift for saying the thing that a room wanted to hear.

---

chapter, pages, and the basis for any figure quoted.]**

Jacob, *Living the Enlightenment: Freemasonry and Politics in Eighteenth-Century Europe*
(New York: Oxford University Press, 1991); and Pierre-Yves Beaurepaire, *L'Europe des
francs-maçons, XVIIIe–XXIe siècles* (Paris: Belin, 2002).

[^3]: The Act of Union of 1813, declaring pure Ancient Masonry to consist of three degrees
and no more, including the Holy Royal Arch. **[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — exact
wording.]**

---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-FIVE
## Ramsay's Oration

Andrew Michael Ramsay was a Scot from Ayr who had gone to the continent, converted to
Catholicism under the direction of Fénelon, tutored the sons of the exiled Stuart claimant
at Rome, received a doctorate at Oxford, been elected a Fellow of the Royal Society, and
acquired the style of Chevalier. He was a man who had made himself, and who had made
himself out of borrowed materials, and he was good at it.

In 1736 he composed a discourse to be delivered at a Masonic gathering in Paris. Whether he
ever delivered it, and in what form, is disputed; what is not disputed is that it
circulated, in manuscript and then in print, and that it did more to shape continental
Freemasonry than any document except Anderson's *Constitutions*.[^1]

It is about six pages long. It changed everything.

---

### What he said

The oration is a piece of institutional flattery of a very high order, and its argument
proceeds in stages.

Freemasonry, Ramsay says, is not what the vulgar suppose. It is an order dedicated to
virtue, to learning, to the union of nations and the softening of manners. Its members are
bound by a fraternity that transcends country and creed. Its ambition is a universal
republic of the mind — and here Ramsay gestures at a vast project of collaborative
learning, a universal dictionary of the liberal arts, to which the fraternity across Europe
might contribute.

Then the history.

Our ancestors, he says, were the crusaders. In the Holy Land, princes and nobles associated
themselves in a fraternity, took vows, adopted ancient signs and symbols from the
mysteries, and undertook to restore the Temple. They joined themselves with the Knights of
St John of Jerusalem. From that association the order descends, and it was carried into
Europe, and particularly into Scotland, where it was preserved.

And — the sentence that mattered most in that room — the order flourished in Scotland
because a Scottish king, James Lord Steward, was Grand Master, and from Scotland it spread
to France and elsewhere.[^2]

---

### What he was doing

Read that with the six questions in hand and it collapses instantly. There is no evidence
for any of it. Not a document, not a chronicle, not a route. The crusading fraternity Ramsay
describes is unattested; the Scottish preservation is unattested; the Grand Mastership is
unattested.

But asking whether Ramsay's history is true is the less interesting question. The
interesting question is what the speech was *for*, and the answer is visible in its
audience.

Ramsay was addressing French Masons in 1736. Freemasonry had arrived in France within the
previous decade, largely through British — and particularly Jacobite — channels. It was
under suspicion. It had been condemned in some quarters as a foreign import, a Protestant
import, and a political one. In 1737 the French authorities would move against it; in 1738
the Pope would condemn it outright.

A French nobleman considering membership in 1736 faced two objections: that this was an
English trade club unworthy of a gentleman, and that it was religiously suspect.

Ramsay answers both, in a single move. The order is not a trade club — it is *chivalric*.
Its ancestors were not stonecutters but crusading knights, which is to say, the ancestors of
the men in the room. And it is not Protestant — it is crusading Catholic in origin,
associated with the Knights of St John, dedicated to the recovery of the Holy Land.

He also, and not incidentally, made it Scottish. Which was congenial both to his own
identity and to the Jacobite networks in which he had spent his life.

Every element of the history is calibrated to its audience. That is what tells us it was
composed rather than received.

---

### Was he lying?

I do not think the question has a clean answer, and I want to resist making him a villain.

Ramsay was operating inside the intellectual culture described in Chapter 6: the *prisca
theologia*, in which traditions are presumed to descend from a common ancient source, and
in which the job of a learned man is to trace the descent. Within that culture, an argument
from resemblance and plausibility is a legitimate form of scholarship, not a fabrication.
The Old Charges had been doing the same thing since 1390 — tracing masonry to Euclid and
Solomon on no evidence at all — and nobody thought them fraudulent.

Ramsay was also doing something the eighteenth century did constantly: giving an
institution the genealogy it needed in order to be respectable. Hobsbawm and Ranger's
invented traditions are full of exactly this.

What can be said with confidence is that he had no evidence, that the chronology does not
work, that no contemporary produced any corroboration, and that the story was new in 1736.

---

### The consequences

The oration's effects are out of all proportion to its length, and they run in three
directions.

**It made the high degrees possible.** If Masonry descends from crusading knights, then the
three Craft degrees are plainly incomplete — where is the knighthood? Within a few years of
the oration, French Masonry was producing degrees to fill the gap it had identified.
Chivalric grades, Scottish grades, degrees of knighthood, Kadosh degrees, Templar degrees.
Ramsay did not create any of them. He created the space they filled.

**It made Masonry Scottish.** The term *Écossais* — Scottish — became, in French usage, the
label for high-degree Masonry in general, and it has almost nothing to do with Scotland.
The Scottish Rite, which would become the largest appendant body in the world and which
would produce Albert Pike, carries its name because of a paragraph in a speech by a man
from Ayr who was inventing a genealogy for a French audience.

**And it opened the door to antiquity.** Ramsay's crusaders had, he said, adopted ancient
signs and symbols from the mysteries. That clause — offered in passing, almost as
ornament — authorised two centuries of speculation. If the crusaders took it from the
mysteries, then Masonry contains the mysteries, and the search for what the mysteries
contained becomes a Masonic activity.

Blavatsky's Masonry, Pike's Masonry, the Golden Dawn's Masonry and the conspiracy
literature's Masonry are all, ultimately, downstream of that clause.

---

### The pattern to notice

I want to draw out the structure here, because it recurs so exactly that it is worth
naming.

**An institution has a gap.** Freemasonry in France had prestige problems.

**Someone supplies a history that fills it.** Ramsay, 1736.

**The history is plausible, congenial and unevidenced.**

**Nobody checks, because nobody can and nobody wants to.**

**Within a generation the history is the tradition**, and people begin building practices
on it.

**Within a century, the practices are cited as evidence for the history.** The existence of
Templar degrees becomes an argument that Masonry is Templar in origin — which is precisely
backward, since the degrees were created because of the claim.

That last step is the one to watch for, because it appears everywhere in this subject. It
is circular sourcing in institutional form: a claim generates a practice, and the practice
is then offered as evidence for the claim.

The Rite of Strict Observance is the purest example ever produced, and it ran for nearly
thirty years before its own members put it to a vote.

---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-SIX
## The Manufacture of Antiquity

On 18 March 1314, on an island in the Seine, Jacques de Molay, the last Grand Master of the
Knights Templar, was burned alive. The order had been arrested in France seven years
earlier, tried on charges of heresy, blasphemy, idolatry and obscenity extracted largely
under torture, and suppressed by papal bull in 1312. Its properties were transferred, its
members dispersed, imprisoned or absorbed, and its name became, for four centuries, a
byword for a spectacular institutional death.

Four hundred and forty years later, a Saxon baron began telling German Freemasons that
their order was the Templars, that the succession had never been broken, and that he had
his authority from superiors whose names he was not permitted to reveal.

The story of the Rite of Strict Observance is the best documented case in this book of a
tradition being manufactured, defended, and finally dismantled by its own adherents. It
deserves a full chapter, because everything that is usually asserted about hidden
transmission can be watched happening here in the open.

---

### What the Templars actually were

Briefly, because the popular literature has made it necessary.

The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon were founded around 1119 to
protect pilgrims in the crusader states. They took their name from their quarters on the
Temple Mount in Jerusalem — the al-Aqsa compound, which the crusaders identified with
Solomon's Temple. They became a military order, then an enormously wealthy one, operating
what amounted to an international banking network.

They were not architects. They were not custodians of esoteric knowledge. They did not
excavate anything, so far as any medieval source records. Their charges of heresy were
extracted under torture by a French king who owed them money, and even the Church's own
inquiry — the Chinon parchment, rediscovered and published in the twenty-first century —
indicates that Clement V absolved the leadership of heresy while suppressing the order for
reasons of political expediency.[^1]

Their suppression was total and well documented. There is no medieval evidence of a
surviving Templar organization.

Between 1314 and the 1730s, nothing in Freemasonry refers to them. The Old Charges do not
mention them. The Scottish records do not mention them. The catechisms do not mention them.
The English exposures of 1723 to 1762 do not mention them.

The Templar entry into Masonry happens in the eighteenth century, and it happens after
Ramsay's chivalric opening of 1736.

---

### Von Hund

Karl Gotthelf, Baron von Hund und Altengrotkau, was a Saxon nobleman, a Catholic in a
Protestant region, a man of substantial estates and, by every account including hostile
ones, sincere.

His account of his own initiation ran as follows. In Paris in the early 1740s he was
received into a higher Masonic order, in the presence of persons of great rank — he named,
at various times, Lord Kilmarnock and others associated with the Jacobite cause — and was
given to understand that this order was the continuation of the Knights Templar, preserved
in secret since the suppression. He was invested with authority over a German province. He
was told that Unknown Superiors directed the order, and that he would be contacted.

He was never contacted.

For the rest of his life — he died in 1776 — von Hund waited. He built the Rite of Strict
Observance around the claim: a system of degrees in which every member took a knightly
name, was assigned to a province of the old Templar geography, and swore, as the rite's
name declares, *strict observance* to superiors he could not identify.

And the rite spread extraordinarily. Through the 1760s and 1770s it became the dominant
form of high-degree Masonry in the German lands and beyond, attracting princes, dukes and,
eventually, most of the continent's serious Masonic energy.

---

### The problem

Von Hund's problem was structural and it could not be solved.

The whole rite rested on an authority he could not produce. He had no charter. He could name
no living superior. The men who had allegedly received him in Paris were dead or
unidentifiable, and when pressed he could not give a coherent account. He appears to have
believed his own story — his behavior over thirty years is very hard to explain otherwise,
and he spent his own fortune on the rite — but belief is not evidence, and by the 1770s his
followers were asking increasingly hard questions.

Other claimants appeared, as they always do in such situations, offering rival authorities.
A man named Johnson claimed to be an emissary of the Unknown Superiors and was exposed as
an impostor. Rival systems multiplied. Alchemists arrived offering the secret the order was
supposed to possess. The whole German Masonic world descended into a confusion of competing
warrants.

---

### Wilhelmsbad

So in 1782 they held a convention to settle it.

The Convent of Wilhelmsbad met near Hanau, over several weeks, and it is one of the most
extraordinary meetings in the history of Western esotericism: an assembly of European
Masonic leaders, gathered to determine, by inquiry and vote, whether their order descended
from the Knights Templar.

They had before them the Strict Observance's claims, the absence of documents, the failure
of the Unknown Superiors ever to appear, and thirty years of accumulated doubt. They also
had a rival proposal from Lyon, of which more in the next chapter.

The convention's conclusion was that the Templar descent could not be sustained. The
Strict Observance was effectively wound up; the Templar claim was set aside; and the
convention gave its endorsement to a reformed system in which knighthood was retained as a
*symbolic* rather than a historical inheritance — the Chevaliers Bienfaisants de la Cité
Sainte, the Beneficent Knights of the Holy City, within Jean-Baptiste Willermoz's Rectified
Scottish Rite.[^2]

Let me state what happened there as plainly as I can.

**A body of eighteenth-century European aristocrats, who had every reason to want an
ancient chivalric pedigree, who had built an international organization on one, and who had
staked their honor on it, examined the evidence and concluded that it was not there.**

Then they said so, and reorganised accordingly.

That is a remarkable act of intellectual honesty, and it almost never gets mentioned. The
popular literature on Masonic Templarism is enormous, and virtually none of it tells the
reader that the claim's own believers voted it down in 1782.

---

### Why the claim survived anyway

And yet it did survive. It is arguably stronger now than it was then.

Templar degrees persisted in Masonic systems and persist today — the Knights Templar of the
York Rite in America and the Great Priory in England, both perfectly respectable bodies
which are entirely clear that their Templar reference is commemorative rather than
genealogical. The claim of literal descent migrated out of Masonry into the wider culture,
where it has flourished: the Templars in Scotland, the Templars at Rosslyn, the Templars
and the Grail, the Templars and the bloodline, the Templars and everything else.

Why does a claim survive its own refutation?

Chapter 6 gave part of the answer: a tradition can outlive the destruction of its
credentials if enough people need it. But there is a second mechanism visible here, and it
is worth naming.

**Once a claim generates institutions, the institutions become evidence for the claim.**

By 1782 there were Templar Masonic bodies all over Europe. They had members, regalia,
ceremonies, archives and continuity. A person encountering them in 1850, or in 1950, would
reasonably ask why Masonic Templar orders exist — and the available answer, the one that
requires no research, is that they exist because Masonry is Templar in origin.

The actual answer is the reverse. They exist because a Saxon baron said so in the 1750s,
and enough people believed him for long enough to build things.

This is the circularity identified at the end of the last chapter, operating at full scale
and across centuries. It is the single most common mechanism of false historical belief in
this entire subject, and once you have seen it here you will recognize it in the
Rosicrucian orders, in the Golden Dawn's German warrant, and in a dozen twentieth-century
organizations claiming Egyptian, Essene or Atlantean descent.

---

### The Rose-Croix, briefly

One other family of degrees belongs in this chapter, and it needs a different verdict.

The Rose-Croix degrees — which appear in France from the 1750s and become the eighteenth
degree of the Scottish Rite — take the Masonic legend and read it Christologically. The
lost word is the Word made flesh. The Temple is rebuilt in the Resurrection. The ceremony
is constructed around the passion and the restoration, and in its developed form it is, by
wide agreement, among the most affecting rituals ever written.

Does it derive from Rosicrucianism?

The name says so. The evidence does not. Chapter 12 established that there was no
Rosicrucian organization in the seventeenth century to descend from, and the Rose-Croix
degrees show no textual dependence on the manifestos. What they show is a *Christian
recovery*: an attempt by French Catholic Masons to take back the confessional content that
Anderson's formula had removed, using a name that carried mystical prestige.

That is not fraud and it is not descent. It is renaming, and it is extremely common.

**Verdict on this chapter's two claims: the Templar descent is unsupported, was manufactured
in the 1750s, and was repudiated by its own adherents in 1782. The Rose-Croix is a genuine
eighteenth-century Christian-mystical development wearing a seventeenth-century name it has
no title to.**

Which leaves the case that is not like either of them. Because while von Hund was waiting
for superiors who never came, a customs official in Bordeaux with an obscure background and
a claimed Iberian descent had founded an order that was doing something no Masonic body had
done before.

It was not claiming ancient authority. It was performing operations.

---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-SEVEN
## Theurgy in the Lodge

This is the chapter the book has been building toward since Chapter 1, and its finding can
be stated in a sentence before the evidence.

**The first unambiguous, documented entry of operative magical practice into a Masonic
structure occurs in 1761, in France, and we know who did it.**

Not in Egypt. Not at Solomon's Temple. Not among the Templars. Not in a Scottish lodge in
1598, or a Warrington tavern in 1646, or the Goose and Gridiron in 1717. In 1761, at
Bordeaux, by a man named Martinez de Pasqually, about whom almost nothing is certainly
known.

---

### The founder

Martinez de Pasqually — the spelling varies; so does nearly everything else about him —
appears in the French Masonic record in the 1750s carrying a patent of doubtful provenance
which he claimed had been granted to his father by Charles Edward Stuart. He was probably
born around 1727, possibly in Grenoble; his family background may have been Iberian and may
have been Jewish or of converso descent, which has been argued both ways and settled by
nobody. His French was imperfect. His writing is difficult.

In 1761 he founded, or completed the founding of, an order with an extravagant name: the
*Ordre des Chevaliers Maçons Élus Coëns de l'Univers* — the Order of Knight-Masons, Elect
Priests of the Universe. *Coën* is a rendering of the Hebrew *kohen*, priest.[^1]

Its structure was Masonic. It had degrees; it recruited from lodges; it used Masonic forms
and language. Its content was not Masonic at all.

---

### The doctrine

Martinez's teaching survives principally in a long, unfinished and famously obscure work,
the *Traité de la réintégration des êtres*, and in his correspondence with his students.
The system runs roughly as follows.

Before the material world, spiritual beings emanated from God. Some fell, through pride,
and their fall necessitated the creation of matter as a prison and a remedy. Adam was
created as a being of glory, charged with the reintegration of the fallen; he fell in turn,
and the material body is the consequence. Human history since is the process of
*réintégration* — the return of emanated beings to their source.

And the process can be assisted. Not merely by piety, but by *operation*.

The Élus Coëns performed ceremonial workings, at prescribed times determined
astrologically, in prepared spaces marked with figures and characters, with invocations of
divine and angelic names, aimed at obtaining what the order called *la Chose* — the Thing —
a visible sign of spiritual contact, often described as a luminous appearance or a *passe*.

This is theurgy, in precisely the Iamblichan sense set out in Chapter 7: ritual operation
directed at contact with intelligences above the human, understood by its practitioners as
religious rather than magical, and undertaken for the purpose of the operator's ascent.

Martinez's students recorded whether operations succeeded. Some did, in their judgment;
many did not. The correspondence about this is one of the most curious bodies of material
in eighteenth-century religious history: serious, literate men, writing to each other about
whether a working had produced its sign, in the tones of colleagues comparing experimental
results.

---

### Why this matters so much

Because it is the exception that establishes the rule, and because it does so with dates.

Everything in Part II showed esoteric material available in print and *not* entering the
lodge. Everything in Part III showed forty years of published Masonic ritual containing
nothing esoteric. Chapter 26 showed a claim of ancient descent manufactured and then
repudiated.

Here, at last, is the real thing: genuine operative magic, inside a genuinely Masonic
organizational structure, with doctrine, ritual texts, practitioners and results.

And its date is 1761.

Which means that when someone asserts that Freemasonry has always contained a magical
tradition, the correct response is not denial. It is: *yes, from 1761, in one French order,
founded by a man we can name, whose doctrine is his own and whose sources are traceable to
Christian theosophy, Kabbalah and the Renaissance magical tradition — and which mainstream
Freemasonry neither authorised nor adopted.*

That is a far more interesting answer than either yes or no.

---

### Saint-Martin

Martinez died in 1774, in Saint-Domingue, where he had gone to settle an inheritance,
leaving his order without a head and his treatise unfinished. Two of his students carried
the work forward in opposite directions, and the divergence is instructive.

Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin had been Martinez's secretary. He performed the operations
and, over time, came to believe that they were unnecessary — that the external ceremonial
was a scaffolding, and that the real work was interior. He turned away from theurgy toward
what he called the *voie intérieure*, the inward way, and toward Jakob Böhme, whom he
learned German in order to read and then translated into French.

He published under the name *le Philosophe Inconnu*, the Unknown Philosopher, and his
writing — mystical, Christian, anti-clerical, politically ambiguous — was widely read. The
current called Martinism descends from him, and it has been reconstituted more than once,
most influentially by Papus at the end of the nineteenth century, which is where it
re-enters this book.

Saint-Martin's trajectory is worth marking because it is a permanent option in this
history: the practitioner who goes through the ceremonial and comes out the other side
believing that the ceremonial was the least of it. A. E. Waite would make the same journey
a hundred and thirty years later, in a different order, and arrive at a similar place.

---

### Willermoz

The other student went the opposite way, and had the greater institutional effect.

Jean-Baptiste Willermoz was a Lyon silk merchant, an organizer of formidable ability, and a
man who spent his long life inside Masonic structures. He had been a Coën. He had also
joined the Strict Observance. And he concluded — correctly — that von Hund's rite had a
magnificent chivalric form and no doctrine worth the name.

So he supplied one.

Willermoz took the Strict Observance's structure, removed the Templar-descent claim that
was by then indefensible, and filled the resulting system with a version of Martinez's
doctrine of the fall and reintegration, refined and made more orthodox, and inflected by
Saint-Martin's interiority. The result, worked out at the Convent of Lyon in 1778 and
carried at Wilhelmsbad in 1782, is the *Régime Écossais Rectifié* — the Rectified Scottish
Rite — whose members are the Beneficent Knights of the Holy City.[^2]

It survives. It is worked today in France, Switzerland and elsewhere. It is, so far as I
can judge, the most theologically coherent system ever built inside a Masonic frame.

And look at what its history demonstrates.

At Wilhelmsbad, the European Masonic leadership rejected a false claim of ancient descent
and adopted in its place a system whose doctrine had been composed *within the previous
twenty years* by two Frenchmen and a Lyon merchant.

They were not fooled, and they did not pretend. They chose content over pedigree.

That is the single most encouraging episode in this book, and it is worth setting against
everything in Part VI, where the opposite choice is made repeatedly.

---

### What the case establishes

Four things, and I want them explicit.

**One: magic entered Freemasonry from outside, late, and by an identifiable route.** Not
carried down within it. Poured in, by named men, in the 1760s and 1770s.

**Two: the Masonic form was the container, not the source.** Martinez used Masonic degree
structure because it was the available technology for organizing graded initiatory
instruction in eighteenth-century France. His doctrine owes nothing to Masonic ritual, and
Masonic ritual acquired nothing from him.

**Three: this was a minority phenomenon.** The Élus Coëns were never large. The Rectified
Rite has never been large. The overwhelming majority of eighteenth-century Freemasons
worked three degrees, dined, gave to charity and went home. Any account that presents
theurgic Masonry as typical is wrong by several orders of magnitude.

**Four: mainstream Freemasonry did not adopt it and in most jurisdictions did not know
about it.** An English Mason of 1780 had no idea that anything of the kind existed.

Those four statements are, together, the carriage thesis stated for the eighteenth century.
The Craft supplied a vessel. Someone else supplied the cargo. The vessel was not changed by
what it carried, and the cargo did not originate on board.

The same thing was happening simultaneously in Prussia, on a larger scale, with a different
cargo — and there the order in question made the relationship explicit, by requiring that
every candidate be a Freemason before he could be taught anything at all.

---

# CHAPTER TWENTY-EIGHT
## The Gold and Rosy Cross

In 1786 the King of Prussia died and was succeeded by his nephew, and within a short time
two men who had been the new king's instructors in a secret alchemical order were among the
most powerful figures in the Prussian state.

Frederick William II had been initiated into the Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross — the
*Gold- und Rosenkreuz* — in 1781. His initiators, Johann Christoph Wöllner and Johann
Rudolf von Bischoffwerder, became respectively his minister responsible for religious
affairs and one of his closest advisers. Wöllner's Religious Edict of 1788 imposed doctrinal
orthodoxy on the Prussian clergy and censorship on theological publishing, in a state that
under Frederick the Great had been a byword for enlightened toleration. Kant himself was
eventually silenced on religious subjects.[^1]

This is the closest thing in the eighteenth century to the scenario the conspiracy
literature has always alleged: an esoteric order, recruiting through Freemasonry, whose
members reached the summit of a European state and used the position.

It is worth examining exactly, because the reality differs from the legend in ways that are
more interesting than the legend — and because it is, on the evidence, the most consequential
junction between an occult tradition and Masonic structure in the entire century.

---

### The order

The Gold- und Rosenkreuz emerged in Central Europe around the 1750s and was reorganised on
a new basis in 1777. Christopher McIntosh's study of it remains the standard account, and
what follows depends on his work throughout.[^2]

Its content was alchemical and Rosicrucian. It taught a system of nine grades —
Juniores, Theoreticus, Practicus, Philosophus, Minor, Major, Adeptus Exemptus, Magister,
Magus — through which a member ascended, receiving at each stage instruction, symbolic
material and, at the higher levels, alchemical processes.

Its mythology was the Rosicrucian one of Chapter 12: an ancient brotherhood, hidden
superiors, a wisdom preserved from antiquity. Its actual materials were seventeenth- and
eighteenth-century alchemical and theosophical literature, some of it excellent, some of it
the ordinary dross of the genre.

And its entry requirement was Freemasonry.

---

### The requirement

That clause deserves its own section, because it is the evidence.

To be admitted to the Gold and Rosy Cross, a man had to be a Master Mason. Not as a
formality — as a structural condition. The order treated the Craft degrees as the
preliminary stage of its own system: Masonry was the outer court, the Rosicrucian grades the
inner.

Consider what this means for the direction of influence.

If Rosicrucianism had descended into Freemasonry, as the popular story has it, we would
expect to find Rosicrucian content inside Masonic ritual, carried there by transmission.
We do not: Part III established its absence across forty years of published evidence.

What we find instead is an alchemical order, founded in the 1750s and reorganised in 1777,
which *uses Freemasonry as its recruiting ground and its structural model*, and which
requires Masonic membership because Masonry supplies the graded initiatory apparatus on
which its own system is built.

The Craft is the platform. The Rosicrucian material is the application running on it.

That is the carriage thesis with a documented institutional requirement attached, and the
Gold- und Rosenkreuz is the cleanest single proof of it in the eighteenth century.

---

### What they actually did

The order's members practiced alchemy, and I want to be careful about what that means,
because Chapter 11 established that the word covers a range.

Some of it was laboratory work. Bischoffwerder appears to have conducted actual operations.
The order circulated processes, recipes and instructions of the kind that had been passing
through alchemical networks for two centuries.

Some of it was theater. The order's higher grades involved elaborate ceremonial, and there
are accounts — including from the Prussian court — of demonstrations that were, to put it
carefully, assisted. Bischoffwerder is said to have staged spirit manifestations for the
future king, and whether or not the specific stories are reliable, the general phenomenon
was common enough in the period to need no special explanation.

And some of it was genuinely intellectual: the transmission and study of a substantial
literature.

The order also produced one of the great artefacts of eighteenth-century esotericism, the
*Geheime Figuren der Rosenkreuzer* — the Secret Symbols of the Rosicrucians — published at
Altona in the 1780s, a folio of colored alchemical and theosophical plates that has been
reproduced continuously ever since and is one of the most visually influential esoteric
works ever printed.[^3]

---

### The politics

Now the difficult part, because this is where the legend and the evidence diverge.

The Wöllner ministry was reactionary. It rolled back Frederician toleration, imposed
orthodoxy, censored, and made Prussia briefly a less free country than it had been. Its
architects were members of an occult order who had reached power through their relationship
with a prince they had initiated.

That is all true, and any honest account must say so. It is the one case in this book where
something close to the conspiracy scenario actually happened.

But notice the shape of it, because the shape is the opposite of the legend.

The Gold- und Rosenkreuz was not a radical or revolutionary body. It was *counter*-
Enlightenment: religiously orthodox, politically conservative, monarchist, and explicitly
hostile to the rationalism of the age. McIntosh's argument is that Rosicrucianism of this
period constituted a kind of third force, sometimes allying with the Enlightenment and
sometimes against it, and in Prussia in the 1780s it came down firmly against.

Meanwhile, and in the same decade and the same language, the Bavarian Illuminati — the
order that the conspiracy literature accuses of destroying throne and altar — were doing the
opposite: infiltrating lodges in order to promote rationalism, anticlericalism and
enlightened reform.

So in the 1780s there were two occult-political networks operating through German
Freemasonry, and they were on opposite sides. One reached actual state power and used it to
impose religious orthodoxy. The other was suppressed in 1784–87 and its papers published,
and it became the permanent villain of Western conspiracy theory.

The one that won is almost never mentioned.

I find that a useful corrective, and not only about the eighteenth century. The conspiracy
imagination reliably selects the group that lost, was suppressed, and left a documentary
record — because a suppressed group with published papers is easier to build a story on
than a successful one that simply became the government.

---

### The collapse

The Gold- und Rosenkreuz did not last. By about 1787 the order had largely suspended its
activity, for reasons that included internal exhaustion, the death or withdrawal of key
figures, and the general collapse of confidence in high-degree systems that followed
Wilhelmsbad.

Its afterlife, however, is long.

Its nine grades — Zelator, Theoricus, Practicus, Philosophus and upward — reappear, with
modifications, in the Societas Rosicruciana in Anglia in 1866, and from there in the
Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn in 1888. The Golden Dawn's grade structure is
recognizably this order's, transmitted through nineteenth-century English Masonic
Rosicrucianism.[^4]

Its plates were reprinted and became part of the standard visual vocabulary of modern
occultism.

And its basic arrangement — Masonry as the outer court, an esoteric order as the inner —
became the template. The SRIA required Masonic membership. So, in its way, did the O.T.O.
The idea that Freemasonry is the antechamber to something more is not an ancient Masonic
doctrine. It is a marketing structure invented in Central Europe in the middle of the
eighteenth century, and it has never gone out of use.

---

### The verdict

**Documented.** An alchemical and Rosicrucian order, active from the 1750s, reorganised
1777, requiring Masonic membership, teaching a graded esoteric system, reaching state power
in Prussia in the later 1780s, and transmitting its grade structure into the nineteenth-
century occult revival.

**Not documented.** Any descent from a seventeenth-century Rosicrucian organization, because
there was none.

**The direction of influence: outward from Masonry, not inward.**

We have now examined four eighteenth-century cases — Ramsay's chivalric genealogy, von
Hund's Templars, Martinez's theurgy, and the Prussian Rosicrucians — and in every one the
Masonic contribution is the same. Not doctrine. Not antiquity. Not secret knowledge.

A structure. A graded, secret, initiatory, portable structure, available to anyone who
wanted to organize men around a teaching.

There is one more group to whom it became available in this century, and they were not
supposed to have it at all.

---
