# CHAPTER TWENTY-NINE
## Women at the Door

In 1744 — fourteen years after Prichard printed the Hiramic legend, seventeen years before
Martinez founded the Élus Coëns — women were being initiated in France into ceremonies
built on Masonic ritual.

They went on being initiated for the next two hundred and seventy years. The rite they
worked has its own texts, its own degrees, its own officers and its own symbolism, and it
was practiced across France and beyond, in lodges attached to men's lodges, attended by
duchesses and by the wives and daughters of the bourgeoisie, with published rituals and
documented membership.

It is called the Rite of Adoption, and until very recently it was almost entirely absent
from the history of Freemasonry.

The scholar who recovered it is Jan Snoek, whose study reconstructs the rite's development
from some hundred and thirty manuscripts and printed sources, and whose work makes it no
longer possible to write about eighteenth-century Freemasonry as an exclusively male
phenomenon.[^1]

---

### The exclusion and its basis

Anderson's *Constitutions* of 1723 states that the persons admitted members of a lodge must
be good and true men, free-born, of mature and discreet age, no bondmen, no women, no
immoral or scandalous men.[^2]

That is the rule, and it has governed mainstream Freemasonry ever since. The usual
justification offered is the operative inheritance: the craft of masonry was a male trade,
and the speculative body preserved its qualifications.

The justification does not survive much pressure. By 1723 the qualifications had already
been detached from the trade in every other respect — the members were not masons, the
lodges were not building sites, and "free-born" was retained while "able to cut stone" was
not. The rule excluding women was a social rule, retained because eighteenth-century English
associational life was male, and dressed in a craft rationale.

France, notably, did not accept the position. French Masonic sociability was built around
salons and mixed company in a way the London tavern lodge was not, and within twenty years
of the *Constitutions* the French had invented a solution.

---

### What Adoption was

The Rite of Adoption was worked by a lodge of women *adopted* by, and meeting under the
authority of, a men's lodge. Its ceremonies were conducted by officers of both sexes: the
men's lodge supplied a presiding officer, the women's their own.

Its degrees were its own. The usual four are Apprentice, Companion, Mistress and Perfect
Mistress, with further degrees in some systems.

And its symbolism was *not* a copy of the Craft's. This is Snoek's most striking finding
and the one that most changes the picture. The Adoption rituals draw heavily on the Garden
of Eden and its imagery — the tree, the apple, the serpent, the ladder — and on the Tower of
Babel, and on the Ark. Where the men's Craft is built on Solomon's Temple and the death of
a builder, the women's rite is built on Genesis and on a narrative of fall and restoration.

That is a genuinely different symbolic system, developed in parallel, with its own logic.
It is not a watered-down men's ritual, and describing it as one — which is what the older
literature did when it noticed the rite at all — is simply a failure to read the texts.

The rite attracted serious participation. Its patronesses included women at the summit of
French society; the Duchess of Bourbon presided over a lodge in Paris in the 1770s. It
survived the Revolution, was revived under the Empire, and continued through the nineteenth
century.

---

### Why it was forgotten

Three reasons, and they are worth naming because the mechanism is general.

**It was not "regular."** Adoption lodges were not Craft lodges, and as the nineteenth
century hardened the definitions of regularity, they fell outside them. Bodies outside the
definition do not appear in the histories written by bodies inside it.

**Its records were less well kept, and less well preserved.** Institutional archives
preserve what institutions value.

**And the men who wrote Masonic history were not looking for it.** This is the largest
factor and the least excusable. A century of Masonic historiography treated the Craft as
the subject and everything else as an aberration — and when the aberration involved women,
it was recorded, if at all, as a curiosity.

Snoek's recovery of the rite is a straightforward demonstration of what happens when
someone looks: a hundred and thirty sources, a coherent ritual tradition, two and a half
centuries of practice, hiding in plain sight in archives that had always been open.

---

### What it proves

For this book's argument, the Adoption rite does something no other case does.

Every previous chapter in Part IV has shown Masonic *form* being filled with content from
outside — chivalric, Templar, theurgic, alchemical. Each time the content came from the
Western esoteric tradition, and each time a reader might reasonably suspect that the
affinity was more than structural: that something in Masonic ritual invited esoteric
filling.

The Adoption rite removes that suspicion, because here the same form is filled with content
that is not esoteric at all. The material is Genesis. The purpose is moral and social. The
result is a functioning initiatory system for women in a society that offered them almost
no institutional life of their own.

The form took it without difficulty. It would have taken anything.

That is the strongest possible statement of the carriage thesis, and it comes from the
part of Masonic history that the esoteric literature has never bothered with.

---

### The nineteenth century and after

The story does not end in 1744, and its later stages matter for Part VI.

Adoption Masonry continued in France through the nineteenth century. In 1882 Maria
Deraismes, a writer and campaigner for women's rights, was initiated in a French lodge —
irregularly, from the standpoint of the obediences — and in 1893, with Georges Martin, she
founded *Le Droit Humain*, an order admitting men and women on equal terms in the same
lodges. That is Co-Masonry, and it spread internationally.

In England it arrived in 1902, and its principal promoter was Annie Besant — by then the
president-in-waiting of the Theosophical Society — who made Co-Masonry a vehicle for
theosophical ideas and carried it across the British Empire and to America.

Which produces a fact worth stating plainly, because it cuts against the usual account:
**the most successful transmission of Masonic ritual into the twentieth-century occult
world ran through women's and mixed Masonry, not through the regular male Craft.**

Besant's Co-Masonry, not the United Grand Lodge of England, is the route by which Masonic
initiation reached the Theosophical movement. And Aleister Crowley's earliest Masonic
initiation of any kind was, on his own account, in an irregular or co-Masonic context.

The bodies excluded from Masonic history as irregular turn out to be the ones that did the
most to carry Masonic ritual into the world beyond the Craft.

---

### A note on the present

Women's Freemasonry exists now and is substantial: in England the Order of Women
Freemasons and the Honourable Fraternity of Ancient Freemasons, in France several
obediences, in America a range of bodies including the Eastern Star, which is a different
kind of organization again. The United Grand Lodge of England has since 2018 recognized
these bodies as regular in their practice while not in amity with them, and has issued
guidance on gender reassignment that would have been unimaginable a generation ago.

None of that is this book's subject. I mention it because the eighteenth-century material
is often presented as though the question were settled and historical, and it is neither.

What the historical record shows is narrower and more useful: that the exclusion of women
from Freemasonry was a social decision of 1723, not a structural property of Masonic
ritual; that the ritual worked perfectly well for women from 1744; and that a great deal of
what happened next in the esoteric world happened because of bodies that mainstream
Freemasonry had declined to recognize.

Part IV is complete. The eighteenth century took a three-degree English moral ceremony and
turned it into the universal structure for initiatory association in the Western world —
capable of holding chivalry, theurgy, alchemy, Christian mysticism, feminism and, before
long, a great deal else.

It also carried it across an ocean, into a society that would use it for purposes nobody in
London had anticipated, and where a free Black man in Boston would obtain a warrant from
the Grand Lodge of England and begin a tradition that is still arguing with its parent
institution two hundred and forty years later.

---

# PART V — THE ATLANTIC CRAFT

# CHAPTER THIRTY
## Colonial Brethren

In 1734 a Philadelphia printer reissued a London book. He had no particular reason to
expect it to sell — it was a set of regulations for a fraternal society, with a long
antiquarian history and some drinking songs at the back — but he was a member of the
society, he had recently become master of his lodge, and he was a man who understood that
printing a thing makes it real.

Benjamin Franklin's edition of Anderson's *Constitutions* was the first Masonic book
printed in America.[^1]

It is a small event with a large meaning. Freemasonry did not arrive in the colonies as an
exotic import to be marveled at. It arrived as a going concern, with a printed rulebook, a
recognized procedure, and a local printer willing to underwrite it — and within forty years
it would be one of the few institutions in British North America that existed
simultaneously in every colony, with a common language and mutual recognition across a
thousand miles.

---

### The arrival

The dates are reasonably clear. Provincial Grand Masters were appointed for the American
colonies from 1730 onward. Lodges are documented in Philadelphia and Boston in the early
1730s — St John's Lodge in Boston from 1733 under a deputation from England — and in the
following decades across the seaboard, in Virginia, the Carolinas, New York, Rhode Island
and the rest.[^2]

Two features of the colonial situation shaped what Freemasonry became there, and both
matter for the rest of this book.

**The first is scarcity of institutions.** A colonial town of two thousand people had a
church, perhaps a court, and very little else. There were no universities to speak of, few
clubs, no professional associations in the modern sense. A body that met regularly, kept
accounts, elected officers, dined, and admitted men by ballot was supplying something for
which there was no competition.

**The second is distance.** A merchant traveling from Charleston to Boston, or from
Philadelphia to London, arrived among strangers and needed a way to establish that he was
respectable, solvent and worth extending credit to. Masonry supplied exactly that: a
recognition system, a claim on hospitality, and a network of men who had sworn to assist
each other.

Steven Bullock's study of the American Craft in this period documents the consequences in
detail, and the picture that emerges is not primarily one of philosophy or symbolism. It is
one of credit, sociability, status and connection.[^3]

---

### Who joined, and why it mattered

In the colonial period, American Freemasonry was an elite institution. Its members were
merchants, officials, planters, professional men, officers — the colonial gentry and the
people who aspired to join them. Membership was a marker of standing and was understood as
one.

That is worth stating because it cuts against two later mythologies at once. It is not the
democratic brotherhood of Revolutionary legend; that comes later. And it is not a
conspiratorial cabal; a body whose membership is a public marker of social standing, whose
processions are attended by the whole town, and whose constitutions are printed by the
local newspaper man is not operating in the dark.

What it was, precisely, was **a technology for making strangers into associates**, in a
society composed largely of strangers. That function — social rather than mystical — is the
principal reason for its colonial success.

---

### The furniture of colonial Masonry

What did they actually do? Largely what English lodges did, with local variation.

They met in taverns. They worked three degrees. They dined, at length and with a great deal
to drink — the Masonic songs at the end of Franklin's *Constitutions* were not decorative.
They processed publicly on St John's Day, in aprons, to a church service, and the sermon
was frequently printed. They buried their members with Masonic honors. They laid corner
stones.

That last practice deserves a note, because it became characteristically American. A Masonic
corner-stone ceremony is a public ritual in which the fraternity's private symbolism —
square, level, plumb, corn, wine and oil — is performed in the open, at a civic occasion, to
consecrate a public building. It is the single most visible thing American Freemasonry has
ever done, and it establishes something important about its self-understanding: this was an
organization that understood itself as *civic*, with a public role, using its ceremonial to
bless the construction of a shared society.

A body engaged in a secret conspiracy does not do its rituals on the courthouse steps with
the governor watching.

---

### The absence

And now the question this book has to ask of every Masonic environment: what esoteric
content was present?

The answer for colonial America is: essentially none, and the evidence is good.

The ritual worked was the English Craft ritual of the period, which Part III established
contained no Hermetic, Kabbalistic, alchemical or magical material. The high degrees, which
did contain such material, arrive in America late — the Scottish Rite's Supreme Council is
not founded at Charleston until 1801, and the systems that fed it were mostly imported in
the later eighteenth century through the West Indies and French connections.

The colonial Masonic sermons that survive — and a good number were printed — are ordinary
Protestant moral discourses. They talk about brotherly love, charity, order, the duties of
society and the virtues of the Craft. They do not talk about Egypt, or ancient mysteries, or
hidden wisdom.[^4]

The American colonial Mason was, in the overwhelming majority of cases, a Protestant
gentleman who had joined a dining club with an impressive ceremony and useful connections.

This matters enormously for Chapter 50, where we deal with the claim that the United States
was founded as a Masonic project on occult principles. Whatever else may be said about it,
the Freemasonry actually available in colonial America contained no occult principles to
found anything on.

---

### Franklin

A word about Franklin himself, because he is the most cited American Mason after Washington
and the most misrepresented.

He joined St John's Lodge in Philadelphia in 1731, became its master, printed the
*Constitutions* in 1734, and served as Provincial Grand Master. In France in the 1770s and
1780s, as American commissioner, he became a member and eventually Venerable Master of the
Lodge of the Nine Sisters in Paris — a lodge of intellectuals, artists and philosophes,
which had admitted Voltaire shortly before his death and which Franklin presided over for a
period.

That Parisian connection is where the interesting material is, and it is not occult. The
Nine Sisters was a lodge of the Enlightenment: encyclopedists, scientists, writers. Its
politics were reformist. Franklin's Masonry in Paris was a diplomatic and intellectual
asset, and he used it as one — the lodge was a room in which an American agent could meet
influential Frenchmen without an audience.

Franklin was also, in the same city and the same decade, a member of the royal commission
appointed in 1784 to investigate Franz Mesmer's animal magnetism. The commission's report —
which devised blind testing, found that the effects were produced by imagination rather than
by any magnetic fluid, and is a landmark in the history of experimental method — is one of
the more elegant ironies in this book.[^5]

The most famous American Freemason of the eighteenth century spent part of his time in
Paris debunking the fashionable occult science of the day, using controls.

---

### What the colonies added

By 1775 Freemasonry in America had acquired two features it did not have in England, and
both would matter.

**It was more socially mobile.** English Masonry was aristocratic at the top and
respectable below. American Masonry, in a society with no aristocracy, was a mechanism by
which men of moderate origin could associate with men of standing — and increasingly, as
lodges multiplied, a mechanism by which men of moderate origin associated with each other.

**And it was becoming political, without intending to.** Lodges were where men met. In the
1760s and 1770s, what men met to talk about in Boston and Philadelphia was increasingly the
relationship with Britain. Nothing in Masonic ritual addresses that subject, and Anderson's
*Constitutions* explicitly forbids discussing politics in lodge. But an institution that
gathers the politically active in a private room, under obligations of mutual loyalty, at a
moment of national crisis, is going to be *relevant* whether or not it is intentional.

What happened next has been written about more, and more badly, than almost anything else in
this history.

---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-ONE
## Revolutionary Brotherhood

The list is genuinely impressive, which is part of the problem.

George Washington was made a Mason at Fredericksburg in 1752, at the age of twenty. Benjamin
Franklin, Provincial Grand Master. Paul Revere, Grand Master of Massachusetts. Joseph
Warren, killed at Bunker Hill, Provincial Grand Master. John Hancock. Israel Putnam. Henry
Knox. Nine signers of the Declaration of Independence by the most careful counts, and more
by the enthusiastic ones. A third or more of Washington's general officers.

On 30 April 1789, Washington took the presidential oath on a Bible borrowed from St John's
Lodge No. 1 of New York, administered by Robert Livingston, Grand Master of New York. In
1793 he laid the corner stone of the Capitol wearing a Masonic apron, with a silver trowel
and the ceremonial of the Craft, before a public procession.[^1]

None of this is disputed. All of it is documented. And almost everything built on it is
wrong, in both directions.

---

### The two errors

**The conspiracy error** says: the United States was founded by Freemasons as a Masonic
project, its capital laid out on occult principles, its seal encoded with esoteric
symbolism, its Constitution a Masonic document.

**The apologetic error** says: Masonic membership was a social formality with no
consequences, and the Craft had nothing to do with the Revolution.

Both fail the same test, which is proportion. The question is not whether Freemasons made
the Revolution or whether Masonry was irrelevant. The question is what an institution of
this kind actually does in a society undergoing that kind of crisis — and that question has
a decent, documented, unexciting answer.

---

### What Masonry actually supplied

Bullock's account, which is the standard one, identifies several contributions, and none of
them is doctrinal.

**A pre-existing intercolonial network.** In 1774 there was almost nothing connecting
politically active men in Boston to politically active men in Charleston. There were no
national newspapers, no political parties, no federal institutions. There *were* Masonic
lodges, mutually recognizing, with a common ceremonial and an obligation of assistance.
When men from thirteen colonies had to learn to trust each other very quickly, some of them
already had a framework for doing so.

**A rehearsal of procedure.** Lodges elected officers by ballot. They kept minutes. They had
constitutions and by-laws, points of order, committees, and rules for admitting and
expelling members. For a great many colonial Americans, the lodge was the first place they
ever participated in a constituted deliberative body that governed itself by written rules.
That is not a small training.

**A vocabulary.** Brotherhood. The level. Virtue. The temple. Harmony. Order. The language
of Revolutionary and early national civic rhetoric is saturated with it, and the saturation
is not coincidental — many of the men producing that rhetoric had heard it every month for
years.

**And a model of association across rank.** The lodge's declaration that men meet on the
level was, in a hierarchical society, a genuine anomaly, and one that Americans took more
seriously than the English did.

Those four things are real, documented, and sufficient. They do not require any esoteric
content, and there was none available to require.

---

### What Masonry did not supply

It did not supply a program. Masonic ritual contains no politics. Anderson's
*Constitutions* explicitly instruct members to be peaceable subjects, never to be concerned
in plots against the state, and to submit to the civil powers. The lodge's own rules forbade
political discussion within it.

And the membership was divided. This is the fact that disposes of the conspiracy reading
most efficiently, and it is almost never mentioned.

**There were Loyalist Freemasons.** Many of them. Masonic lodges existed in the British
army; military lodges traveled with regiments and were among the most important vehicles
of Masonic expansion in the eighteenth century. Officers on both sides of the war were
Masons. Some American lodges split; some ceased working; some carried on with members who
would end the war in Nova Scotia.

An institution that supplies both sides of a war with members is not directing it.

---

### The Boston case

The most interesting American Masonic politics is local and it concerns two rival grand
lodges, which tells us more than the national story does.

Boston had, from the 1750s, both a Grand Lodge deriving from England's "Moderns" and a body
deriving from the "Antients" — the rival English grand lodge formed in 1751, whose appeal
was to Irish immigrants and to men outside the established elite. The Antients were, socially,
the less grand of the two, and in Boston that mapped onto the political division with
reasonable fidelity: the Antients' St Andrew's Lodge was the home of Joseph Warren and Paul
Revere, and its members were prominent in the patriot movement.

It was at the Green Dragon Tavern, which St Andrew's Lodge owned a share of, that the
Boston Tea Party was organized — and the lodge's minutes for the evening of 16 December
1773 record that the lodge closed early on account of the small number of members present.
This has been cited for two centuries as evidence of Masonic direction of the Tea Party.
What it is evidence of is that the men who threw the tea into the harbor were also the men
who attended that lodge, which is a different claim, and a true one.[^2]

The distinction is exactly the distinction this book keeps insisting on. Overlapping
membership is not institutional action. A room where the patriots met is not a patriot
organization, any more than a tavern is.

---

### Washington

Washington's own Masonry has been the subject of more invention than any other aspect of
this history, so it is worth stating what the record shows.

He was initiated in 1752 and passed and raised in 1753 at Fredericksburg Lodge. He attended
irregularly. During the war he took part in Masonic observances with military lodges, and
there is good evidence he valued the fraternity's role in the army. He was named charter
master of Alexandria Lodge No. 22 in 1788 and accepted, though he did not preside actively.
He wrote courteous letters to Masonic bodies. He laid the Capitol corner stone in 1793.

He also, in 1798, wrote a letter to a Reverend G. W. Snyder, who had sent him a copy of
Robison's *Proofs of a Conspiracy*, in which he said that he did not believe the lodges of
Freemasons in this country had, as societies, endeavored to propagate the doctrines of the
Illuminati or the principles of Jacobinism, and that he had not been in a lodge more than
once or twice in the last thirty years.[^3]

That letter is routinely quarried by both sides — conspiracists cite the first half, Masons
the second. Read whole, it is the statement of a man who thought the fraternity was
harmless, who was not personally very active in it, and who was faintly irritated at being
asked.

---

### The corner stone

I want to end on the image, because it is the right one for this chapter.

In September 1793 the President of the United States walked in procession to the site of
the Capitol, wearing a Masonic apron, carrying a silver trowel, accompanied by lodges of
Maryland and Virginia in full regalia, before a crowd, with artillery, and laid a stone with
corn, wine and oil.

Everything about that scene is public. The newspapers reported it. The apron survives. The
trowel survives.

And what it means is not that a secret society was founding a nation in code. It means that
in 1793 the Masonic corner-stone ceremony was simply *how Americans consecrated a public
building* — the available civic ritual for a republic that had abolished established
religion and needed something to do at ceremonies. The Craft supplied a non-sectarian
liturgy for a state that could not use a sectarian one.

That is the American story in a sentence, and it is the carriage thesis again in a
different register. Freemasonry gave the new republic a *form* — of association, of
procedure, of public ceremony — and the republic filled it with its own content.

Which is precisely what a group of free Black men in Boston had already understood, nine
years earlier, when they obtained a warrant from London and began to do the same thing for
themselves.

---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-TWO
## African Lodge No. 459

On 29 September 1784, the Grand Lodge of England issued a warrant constituting African
Lodge No. 459 in Boston, Massachusetts. Its Master was a free Black man named Prince Hall.

The warrant took three years to arrive. When it did, the lodge it authorised was as regular
as any in America — chartered by the same body, under the same constitutions, working the
same degrees. And American Freemasonry spent the next two hundred years declining to
recognize it.

The reasons given changed over time. The reason did not.

---

### Prince Hall

What is known about him is less than one would wish and more than is usually reported.

He was born around 1735 or 1738; the place is uncertain, and the confident accounts of a
Barbadian birth rest on nineteenth-century sources of doubtful reliability. He was enslaved
in the household of a Boston leatherworker named William Hall, and was manumitted in 1770.
He worked as a leatherdresser and caterer. He was a property owner and a voter. He was a
Methodist. He signed petitions.

Those petitions are the best evidence we have of his mind, and they are remarkable. In
1777 he was among the signers of a petition to the Massachusetts legislature arguing that
the natural right to freedom belonged to every person and that enslavement was incompatible
with the principles the colonies were then asserting against Britain. In 1787 he petitioned
for equal access to schools for Black children in Boston, and when refused, helped to
establish a school. In 1788 he petitioned successfully against the kidnapping of free Black
Bostonians into slavery.[^1]

A man petitioning a legislature in 1777 on the incompatibility of slavery with natural
right is not incidentally political. He was building institutions in a city that offered
his community none, and Freemasonry was one of them.

---

### The initiation

In March 1775, Prince Hall and fourteen other free Black men were initiated into
Freemasonry in Boston — not by an American lodge, but by a military lodge attached to a
British regiment then garrisoning the town, on the eve of the Revolution.[^2]

The circumstance is worth pausing on. Irish military lodges traveling with British
regiments were among the most prolific initiators in the eighteenth-century Masonic world,
and they operated with a latitude that settled lodges did not. When the regiment departed,
the fifteen men were left with a permission to meet as a lodge but without a charter to
work degrees.

They met that way for nine years. In 1784 they applied to London. In 1787 the warrant
reached Boston. African Lodge No. 459 was constituted, and in 1791 Prince Hall was
designated Provincial Grand Master.

Everything in that sequence is procedurally regular. The warrant came from the same Grand
Lodge that had chartered lodges across the colonies.

---

### The refusal

American Masonic bodies did not recognize it, then or for a very long time afterward.

The arguments deployed over two centuries were various: that the warrant lapsed when
English Grand Lodge authority ended in America; that African Lodge had become irregular by
failing to affiliate; that territorial jurisdiction was exclusive and a state Grand Lodge
could not permit a second body in its territory. These are real points of Masonic law and
they were argued with great technical seriousness.

They were also applied to no other case with anything like the same rigor. The territorial
argument, in particular, was applied in states where multiple grand bodies had coexisted
without difficulty, and was maintained in jurisdictions that recognized foreign grand
lodges thousands of miles away while refusing the one in their own city.

The stated reasons were jurisdictional. The operative reason was race, and the historical
scholarship is unanimous on this.[^3]

Recognition came slowly and unevenly. Some northern jurisdictions recognized Prince Hall
Grand Lodges from the late twentieth century; a number of southern ones did not do so until
the twenty-first; at the time of writing, a small number still have not.

---

### What it means for this book

I have placed this chapter at the structural center of the book's argument, and not for the
reason a reader might expect. It is here because it is *evidence*.

Part IV showed Masonic form being filled with content from outside: chivalric, Templar,
theurgic, alchemical. Chapter 29 showed it filled with Genesis and adapted for women. The
pattern was clear but a skeptic could still object that all of those cases involve
initiatory or religious content, and that there might be something in Masonic ritual that
specifically invites it.

African Lodge removes the objection completely.

Here the same form — identical degrees, identical warrant, identical ritual — is taken up
by a community for whom its meaning is *neither esoteric nor moral but political*: an
institution of self-governance in a society that permitted them none. A lodge is a body
that elects its own officers, keeps its own funds, disciplines its own members, buries its
own dead with honor, and corresponds as an equal with bodies abroad. For free Black
Bostonians in 1787, none of those capacities was available anywhere else.

Chernoh Sesay's work on the social origins of Black Freemasonry makes the case in detail:
the lodge was a mechanism of community formation, leadership, mutual aid and public
standing, emerging precisely in the passage from slavery to freedom.[^4] Stephen Kantrowitz
has traced how the institution carried forward into the nineteenth century as a vehicle of
Black political life.

The ritual did not change. The meaning did, completely, because the users were different.

**That is the carriage thesis proved in a register where no magic is involved at all.** The
Masonic form is a container. What it means is set by who picks it up.

---

### The universalism problem

There is a harder point here and I do not want to slide past it.

Anderson's *Constitutions* required that a candidate be free-born. In eighteenth-century
England that clause was a relic of guild qualification, distinguishing free men from
bondmen in a society that had few of the latter. Transplanted to a slave society, it became
something else, and it was used as something else: a formerly enslaved man was not
free-*born*, and the clause was deployed against Prince Hall Masonry accordingly.

A rule written for one society became an instrument of exclusion in another. Nobody in
London intended that. It happened anyway, and it happened because the universalist language
of the Craft — men meeting on the level, the brotherhood of all mankind — was always being
worked by people with a narrower idea of who counted.

The same tension appears everywhere in this part of the book. Colonial lodges in
slaveholding societies were full of slaveholders. Lodges that declared the equality of all
men under the Great Architect excluded women by rule and Black men by practice. Masonic
imperial networks, as Jessica Harland-Jacobs has shown, carried British power around the
globe while proclaiming universal brotherhood, and their local application of that
brotherhood was calibrated precisely to colonial racial hierarchy.[^5]

I do not think this makes the universalist language hypocritical, exactly. I think it makes
it *unfinished* — a claim made in advance of anybody's willingness to honor it, which
people then held the institution to. Prince Hall's petitions are an example of exactly that
move: taking the Revolution's language of natural right and insisting on its application.
His lodge did the same thing with the Craft's.

An institution that says all men meet on the level, and then refuses, has handed its
critics the standard by which it will be judged. That is not nothing. It is, in fact, a
substantial part of why Masonic language proved so useful to the people it excluded.

---

### The long consequence

Prince Hall Freemasonry became one of the most important institutions in African American
life. Through the nineteenth century and into the twentieth, Prince Hall lodges were
centers of leadership, mutual aid and respectability politics, with a membership that
included a large share of Black professional and civic leadership. W. E. B. Du Bois was a
member. Thurgood Marshall was a member.

And it accumulated its own historiography, its own disputes, and its own claim to
legitimacy — argued, for two centuries, on precisely the documentary grounds this book has
been using throughout. *Where is the warrant? Who issued it? On what date? Under what
authority?*

The answer, in this case, is satisfying in a way that few answers in this book are. The
warrant existed. It is dated. It was issued by the Grand Lodge of England on 29 September
1784.

The documents were never the problem.

---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-THREE
## The Morgan Affair

In the summer of 1826, a stonemason and bricklayer in Batavia, New York, told people he was
going to publish the secrets of Freemasonry.

William Morgan was about fifty, a drinker, in debt, and of unreliable reputation. He had
claimed Masonic membership; whether he had been regularly initiated is disputed. He had been
refused admission to the new Royal Arch chapter at Batavia, and he was angry about it. He
made an arrangement with a local printer named David Miller, himself a disaffected Mason,
to publish an exposure.

What happened next destroyed American Freemasonry for a generation, produced the first
third party in United States history, and remains the most consequential single episode in
the Craft's history anywhere in the world.

---

### The events

The sequence, so far as it can be established from the trials and contemporary records, ran
as follows.

Word of the forthcoming book spread among local Masons through the summer of 1826. There
were attempts to stop it: pressure on Miller, an attempt to burn the printing office, and
harassment.

On 11 September, Morgan was arrested on a petty charge — a debt, and an allegation of
stealing a shirt and tie — and taken to Canandaigua. He was released, immediately re-arrested
on the debt, and jailed. On the evening of 12 September, someone paid the debt, and he was
released from jail at night. Outside, he was seized, forced into a carriage, and driven
west.

The carriage route can be traced with reasonable confidence: through a series of villages,
changing horses, to Fort Niagara, an abandoned military post on the American side of the
Niagara River. Morgan was held in the magazine there.

After that, nothing is certain. He was never seen again.[^1]

---

### What happened to him

We do not know, and I want to be careful, because both certainties on offer are unearned.

The Masonic-apologetic position — that Morgan was paid off and sent to Canada, or emigrated,
or was later seen abroad — rests on rumors and sightings of the kind that follow every
disappearance, none corroborated.

The anti-Masonic position — that he was rowed into the river and drowned — rests on a
confession made many years later, of doubtful provenance, and on the general probabilities.

A body recovered from the lake at Oak Orchard in 1827 was identified as Morgan at an
inquest, then re-examined and identified as a Canadian named Timothy Monro, whose widow
described his clothing accurately. The anti-Masonic leader Thurlow Weed is reported to have
remarked that the body was "a good enough Morgan until after the election," a line that has
followed American politics ever since, and which tells you a great deal about the atmosphere
of the moment.[^2]

What is certain: Morgan was abducted, by Freemasons, in an organized operation involving
many people across several counties, and he disappeared. Several men were tried. The
convictions obtained were for conspiracy to abduct, and the sentences were light — the
longest was a matter of years, most were months.

Those light sentences did more damage than the abduction.

---

### The reaction

Because what the public concluded — and it was not an unreasonable conclusion — was that
the courts would not convict Freemasons.

Consider the local situation. In western New York in 1826, a substantial proportion of
sheriffs, judges, jurors, prosecutors and newspaper editors were Masons. A crime had been
committed by Masons against a man who threatened the fraternity. Witnesses were
uncooperative. Investigations stalled. Grand juries returned little. Some officials
obstructed openly.

And the obligation itself was quoted back at them. A Mason had sworn to keep a brother's
secrets. What did that oath require of a juror?

That question — whether a private obligation could override a public duty — is the real
subject of the Morgan affair, and it is the reason the reaction was so enormous. It was not
primarily about a missing bricklayer. It was about whether a republic could tolerate
institutions whose members owed each other prior loyalties.

---

### Anti-Masonry

What followed was one of the strangest and most consequential mass movements in American
history.

Anti-Masonic conventions met. Anti-Masonic newspapers proliferated — dozens of them.
Churches, particularly in the evangelical revival that was sweeping the same region at the
same time, demanded that members renounce the lodge. Ministers preached against it.
Thousands of Masons publicly seceded, and their renunciations were printed.

And in 1828 it became a political party — the first significant third party in United States
history, and the first to hold a national nominating convention, in Baltimore in 1831, a
procedural innovation that both major parties promptly copied. It ran a presidential
candidate in 1832, carried Vermont, and elected governors and congressmen. Among the men
who began their careers in it were Thurlow Weed, William Seward, and Thaddeus Stevens.[^3]

Historians have long debated what Antimasonry was really about, and the debate is worth
noting. Kathleen Smith Kutolowski's work on the social base of the movement found that it
was not a movement of the dispossessed against an elite in any simple sense — the
distinctions between Masons and Antimasons in western New York were subtler than the
rhetoric suggested.[^4] It correlates strongly with evangelical revivalism, with
temperance, and with a broad anxiety about secret and privileged associations in a
democratic society.

Its effect on Freemasonry was devastating. In New York State, lodge numbers collapsed —
from hundreds to a fraction. Grand lodges in several states suspended operations. The total
American membership fell by something like two-thirds over the following decade. Some
lodges surrendered their charters; others simply stopped meeting and hoped.

---

### What it demonstrates

Three things, and each connects to an argument running through this book.

**One: secrecy has costs, and they come due in a crisis.** Chapter 3 described how
concealment manufactures authority and mythology. The Morgan affair is the bill. An
institution that had cultivated an air of privileged knowledge and mutual obligation found,
when something went wrong, that the public had no way to evaluate what had happened and
every reason to assume the worst. The lodge's opacity, which had been an asset for a
century, became the thing that convicted it.

**Two: the conspiracy was real, and it was local and small.** This is the part that neither
side likes. The abduction of William Morgan *was* a Masonic conspiracy: organized,
multi-county, involving officials, and followed by obstruction. It was also completely
unlike the thing the Antimasons believed they had uncovered. It was not directed from
anywhere. There was no national plan, no international body, no secret ruler. It was a
group of provincial men in western New York who panicked about a book and did something
criminal, and then their friends covered for them.

That is what Masonic conspiracies actually look like when they occur, and the gap between
that and the imagined one is instructive. Real institutional wrongdoing tends to be local,
improvised, and stupid. The version the conspiracy literature requires — coordinated,
patient, global, invisible — is a different kind of object, and the Morgan affair is
evidence against it rather than for it, because it shows what happens when Masons actually
do conspire: they get caught, tried, and written about for two centuries.

**Three: the movement against it made American politics.** The national nominating
convention, the modern third-party template, and a cohort of politicians who would go on to
found the Republican Party and drive emancipation — all of it came out of the reaction to a
disappearance in Batavia.

---

### The recovery

Freemasonry in America did not die. It came back from the 1840s, and by the later nineteenth
century it was larger than it had ever been — the age of the great Masonic temples, of the
Scottish Rite's expansion, of the Shrine, of fraternal orders generally, in what historians
of the period call the golden age of fraternity.

But it came back changed. The post-Morgan Craft was more careful, more publicly charitable,
more anxious to be seen as respectable, and considerably more invested in explaining
itself. The enormous American Masonic literature of the nineteenth century — Mackey's
encyclopedias, the ritual monitors, the lecture systems, and eventually Pike's *Morals and
Dogma* — is partly a product of an institution that had learned what happens when the
public cannot tell what you are.

Which is to say: the Craft responded to an accusation of hidden knowledge by producing an
enormous quantity of interpretation.

And the man who produced the most influential interpretation of all was, at the time of the
Morgan affair, a seventeen-year-old schoolteacher in Massachusetts who had not yet gone
west.

---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-FOUR
## Empire and the Portable Lodge

A regiment of the British army in the eighteenth century might carry, among its
impedimenta, a warrant.

Irish Grand Lodge in particular issued charters to lodges attached to regiments rather than
to places — traveling lodges, which moved with the battalion and could be opened wherever
it was quartered. There were hundreds of them. They operated in Ireland, in England, in the
Americas, in the Caribbean, in India, in the Mediterranean, in Africa, and wherever else the
British army went, which by the nineteenth century was very nearly everywhere.

They were among the most effective vehicles of Masonic expansion in history, and the reason
is structural. A lodge that is attached to a place needs a building and a settled
membership. A lodge that is attached to a warrant needs neither. It needs a room, a few
objects, and enough members to open.

It was one such lodge that initiated Prince Hall in Boston in 1775.

---

### The infrastructure of empire

Jessica Harland-Jacobs's study of Freemasonry and British imperialism is the essential work
here, and its argument is worth stating carefully because it is easily caricatured.[^1]

Her claim is not that Freemasonry ran the empire. It is that Freemasonry functioned as
imperial *infrastructure* — one of the connective tissues by which a scattered, mobile,
administratively thin empire held itself together.

Consider the problem an imperial system faces. It must move men constantly — soldiers,
officials, merchants, engineers, planters — between places where they know nobody. Each
arrival must be integrated into a local society of expatriates. Each must be assessed for
trustworthiness. Each needs sociability, credit, and, when things go badly, assistance and
a decent burial.

Freemasonry supplied all of it, with a recognition system that worked across the entire
network. A Scottish engineer arriving in Calcutta, a Cornish officer posted to Jamaica, an
Irish clerk sent to Cape Town — each could present himself at a local lodge and be received.

By the mid-nineteenth century the Craft was established across the empire on a scale that
is difficult to overstate: lodges in every substantial colonial settlement, district grand
lodges, imperial dignitaries as grand masters, and a constant traffic of certificates,
warrants and correspondence flowing back to London, Edinburgh and Dublin.

---

### Universalism and its limits

And here the tension of the last two chapters reappears, sharpened.

Masonic rhetoric was emphatically universal. The Craft declared itself the center of union
among men who would otherwise have remained at a perpetual distance. It admitted men
without regard to nation or creed. In India, lodges admitted Indians — Parsis first and
most readily, then Hindus and Muslims — and Anderson's religious formula was stretched to
accommodate them, with candidates obligated on their own sacred books.

That is a real and significant thing, and it happened earlier and more extensively than the
surrounding colonial society would have predicted.

It was also, everywhere, contested and limited. Admission of non-Europeans was resisted in
many lodges, granted grudgingly in others, and in some colonial contexts effectively
prohibited by social practice regardless of what the constitutions said. In southern Africa
and in the Caribbean, color lines ran through the Craft as they ran through everything
else. And the entire imperial Masonic network operated as an instrument of an imperial
project, whatever its members believed about brotherhood.

The honest formulation is that Freemasonry in the empire was *simultaneously* a genuine site
of cross-cultural association unusual for its time, and a component of a structure of
domination. Both statements are true. Treating either as the whole truth produces bad
history — the first produces the self-congratulatory Masonic version, the second produces a
flattening that cannot explain why Indian, Ottoman and African men joined in substantial
numbers and found it useful.

Thierry Zarcone's work on Freemasonry in the Ottoman and Islamic world documents the latter
case in detail: lodges as sites of reformist politics, of contact between Ottoman elites and
European ideas, and eventually of nationalist organizing — which is to say, Masonic
structures used against the imperial interests that had carried them there.[^2]

The form again. It travels, and it does what its users want.

---

### Jews and the Craft

A second case, differently instructive.

Jewish men were admitted to English lodges within a few years of the 1723 *Constitutions*,
at a time when Jews in Britain labored under substantial civil disabilities and would not
be admitted to Parliament for another century and a quarter. Anderson's formula — the
religion in which all men agree — made it possible, and lodges took the opportunity.

Jacob Katz's study of Jews and Freemasons in Europe traces the consequences, and they are
not simple.[^3] In Britain and the Netherlands, admission was comparatively easy. In the
German lands it was fought over for a century, with some lodges admitting, others refusing,
and others admitting Jews only as visitors. In France the position shifted with the
Revolution. Everywhere, Jewish membership was both a genuine opening and a recurring
occasion for conflict.

And it produced a long-running libel. From the late nineteenth century, antisemitic
polemic in France, Germany and Russia fused the two — *judéo-maçonnerie*, the Jewish-Masonic
conspiracy — into a single imagined enemy. That fusion was a principal theme of the
*Protocols of the Elders of Zion* and of the propaganda that followed, and it had lethal
consequences. Freemasonry was banned and its members persecuted under fascist regimes
across Europe; Masonic bodies were suppressed in Italy, Germany, Spain and occupied
territories, and Masons were among the groups deported and killed.

That deserves to be stated plainly in a book about Masonic secrecy, because it is the
clearest possible demonstration of what opacity costs. An organization that will not
explain itself supplies its enemies with a blank surface. In the twentieth century, people
wrote things on that surface and then killed for them.

---

### Class

One more dimension, more mundane and easily lost.

Freemasonry in the eighteenth century was a gentleman's institution. In the nineteenth it
became something broader, and in Britain it became substantially a *lower-middle-class* one
— shopkeepers, clerks, foremen, tradesmen — alongside its grander lodges.

This matters because it is bound up with the enormous nineteenth-century expansion of
friendly societies and fraternal orders generally: the Oddfellows, the Foresters, the Buffs
and dozens more, most of which borrowed Masonic ritual form wholesale. Daniel Weinbren and
others have documented the relationship between Masonic ceremonial and the mutual-aid
movement, and it is a large and under-appreciated part of the Craft's influence.[^4]

Before the welfare state, an ordinary family's protection against sickness, unemployment
and burial costs was a subscription to a society that paid benefits. Those societies needed
trust, discipline, and a mechanism for binding members to obligations — and they reached,
almost without exception, for the Masonic template: degrees, passwords, regalia, officers,
a ceremonial of admission.

So the largest single application of Masonic ritual technology in the nineteenth century
was not esoteric at all. It was insurance.

I find that a useful thing to know when reading the occult literature of the same decades.
While Éliphas Lévi was reconstructing ceremonial magic in Paris and Westcott was assembling
the Golden Dawn in London, several million Europeans were being initiated into
Masonic-derived ceremonies in order to secure sick pay.

---

### Part V's finding

The Atlantic and imperial chapters establish something the esoteric chapters cannot,
precisely because no esotericism is involved.

The Masonic form proved portable to a degree that no other institutional form of its period
matched. It crossed oceans, empires, languages, religions, classes and the color line. It
was used by revolutionaries and loyalists, slaveholders and abolitionists, imperial
administrators and anti-imperial nationalists, aristocrats and clerks, men and women.

In every case it did roughly the same *structural* work — bounded association, graded
admission, mutual obligation, self-government, public ceremony — and in every case its
*meaning* was supplied by the people using it.

Which means that when we turn, in the next part, to a group of Victorian Englishmen who
took the Masonic form and filled it with Kabbalah, Egyptian god-names and ceremonial magic,
we should not be surprised, and we should not treat it as a revelation about Freemasonry.

They were doing what everybody else had been doing for a hundred and fifty years.

They were just doing it with stranger cargo, and they had a French author to supply it.

---

### NOTES

# PART VI — THE OCCULT REVIVAL

# CHAPTER THIRTY-FIVE
## Éliphas Lévi

In 1847 a Frenchman of thirty-seven was released from prison for the second time. He had
been convicted, on both occasions, of writing books.

The first, *La Bible de la liberté*, had appeared in 1841 and been seized within days: a
work of revolutionary socialism in prophetic register, declaring that the poor were the true
inheritors and that the existing order was an offense against God. The second, in 1847,
belonged to the same current. Between them he had written on the emancipation of women, on
the Christian communism of Lamennais and Buchez, and in a milieu that included Flora
Tristan, the utopian socialists and the Catholic left.

His name was Alphonse-Louis Constant. He had trained for the priesthood at Saint-Sulpice
and been ordained deacon, and had left before taking final vows, unable — the accounts
vary — to accept celibacy or to accept the Church.

Seven years after his release, in 1854, he published the first volume of a work under a
different name. He had rendered Alphonse-Louis Constant into its Hebrew equivalents: Éliphas
Lévi Zahed. The book was called *Dogme et rituel de la haute magie*.

It is the single most influential book on magic written in the last five hundred years, and
almost everything after it in this book descends from it.[^1]

---

### The revision

The received account of Lévi, repeated in occult literature for a century and a half, is
that he recovered a tradition: that he read the old books — Agrippa, the grimoires, the
Kabbalah — and restored to Europe a magical science that had been lost.

The work of Julian Strube has substantially dismantled this, and it is the most important
recent revision affecting the whole field.[^2]

Strube's method is simply to read Constant's earlier writings and trace the continuities.
What emerges is that the magical synthesis of 1854 did not arrive from nowhere after a
period of antiquarian study. It grew directly out of the socialist and Catholic-heterodox
writing of the 1840s, and it carries that inheritance in its bones.

The 1840s Constant had argued that a primitive revelation underlay all religions, that the
Church had corrupted it, that a coming synthesis would reconcile religion and reason, and
that the reconciliation would inaugurate a transformed social order. The 1850s Lévi argues
the same things — with *magic* as the name for the primitive revelation, and the magus as
the figure who holds the synthesis.

The revolutionary content did not disappear. It was transposed.

This matters for a reason that goes beyond biography. It means that *occultism*, as a
movement, is not the resurfacing of an ancient current. It is a nineteenth-century
formation with nineteenth-century concerns — the crisis of religious authority, the
prestige of science, the failure of 1848, the search for a synthesis that would satisfy both
faith and reason.

Wouter Hanegraaff's larger argument points the same way: the category of Western esotericism
is itself a construction, and the tradition it names looks continuous mainly because its
participants kept insisting it was.[^3]

---

### What the book says

*Dogme et rituel* is in two volumes and twenty-two chapters each, one for every letter of
the Hebrew alphabet and — Lévi's own innovation — one for every trump of the tarot.

Its central doctrine can be stated in three propositions.

**One: there is a universal agent.** Lévi calls it the astral light: a subtle medium
pervading everything, the vehicle of life, light and imagination, plastic to the will. Every
magical operation works through it. It is, quite clearly, a synthesis of Mesmer's animal
magnetism with the Renaissance *spiritus* of Chapter 8 and with the physics of an age
excited about imponderable fluids — electricity, magnetism, the luminiferous ether.

**Two: the instrument is the will.** This is Lévi's decisive move and it is what makes him
modern. In the older ceremonial magic, the operator compels or petitions a spirit. In Lévi,
the operator's trained will acts upon the astral light directly. Ceremonies, names, circles
and instruments are not addressed to anyone; they are devices for concentrating the
operator's own imagination and resolve.

**Three: the key is the Kabbalah, and the key to the Kabbalah is the tarot.**

That third proposition deserves its own paragraph, because it is an invention.

---

### The tarot

The tarot is a fifteenth-century Italian card game. It acquired a divinatory and esoteric
reputation in eighteenth-century France, principally through Court de Gébelin, who in the
1780s declared it a survival of Egyptian wisdom, and through the cartomancer Etteilla.
There is no evidence for the Egyptian claim and the documentary history of the cards as a
game is well established.

Lévi's contribution was to connect the tarot to the Kabbalah. Twenty-two trumps,
twenty-two letters of the Hebrew alphabet: therefore, he proposed, a correspondence.
Therefore the trumps encode the letters, the letters encode the paths of the Tree of Life,
and the whole system is a single key.

No Jewish source contains this. No pre-modern source of any kind contains it. It is Lévi's,
published in the 1850s.

And it became, within forty years, the organizing structure of the entire Anglo-American
magical tradition. The Golden Dawn built its grade system on it. Crowley elaborated it in
*Liber 777* and in the *Book of Thoth*. Every tarot deck on sale today with Hebrew letters
or Sephirotic attributions is downstream of a Frenchman's analogy from a numerical
coincidence.

I want to be clear that I do not say this to belittle it. It is an extraordinarily
productive idea, and systems do not have to be ancient to work. But the sequence must be
stated correctly, because the reverse is asserted constantly: the tarot-Kabbalah system is
not ancient wisdom that Lévi recovered. It is a nineteenth-century construction that later
practitioners projected backward.

---

### The Baphomet

And then there is the picture.

In the second volume, Lévi published a drawing of his own: a seated winged figure with the
head of a goat, a torch between its horns, one arm raised and one lowered with the words
*solve* and *coagula* inscribed on them, a caduceus at the belly, breasts, and a goat's
lower body. He called it the Sabbatic Goat, the Baphomet of Mendes.

His explanation is emphatically *not* satanic. The figure is a composite emblem of the
universal equilibrium — the union of opposites, male and female, above and below, dissolution
and coagulation. It is a pantacle, a summary of his system in one image.

That drawing is now the visual definition of the Devil in Western popular culture. It is on
album covers and in films. It is what people picture when they hear the word satanic.

It was drawn in 1856 by a former socialist pamphleteer to illustrate a doctrine of balance.

We will follow its career in Chapter 38, because it is also the image that will be used,
within forty years, to accuse an American Freemason of devil-worship on the strength of a
forged letter.

---

### The man

A word about Lévi himself, because the temperament matters.

He was poor for most of his life and made his living by writing and by teaching students.
He married a woman much younger than himself who left him. He was, by the accounts of those
who knew him, gentle, learned, talkative and somewhat vain. He performed at least one
recorded ceremonial operation — an evocation of Apollonius of Tyana, conducted in London in
1854, which he described afterward in careful and rather deflating terms: he saw
something, he was exhausted, he was not certain what had happened, and he advised against
repeating it.[^4]

That report is worth holding up against everything the twentieth century made of him. It is
the account of a cautious man describing an ambiguous experience and refusing to overstate
it.

He remained, throughout, a Catholic — heterodox, argued-with, and sincere. He believed he
was restoring the Church's own lost science, not opposing it.

---

### The transmission

How did a French author reach everyone?

By being read, and the routes are documented.

**Into England.** *Dogme et rituel* circulated among English occultists from the 1850s; Lévi
visited London in 1854 and again in 1861, meeting Kenneth Mackenzie, Edward Bulwer-Lytton
and others in the circles that would produce the SRIA. A. E. Waite translated him into
English, beginning in the 1880s, and the translations carried him to a mass audience.

**Into the Golden Dawn.** Mathers, Westcott and their colleagues read him. The astral light,
the tarot-Kabbalah correspondence, the pentagram and its orientations, the doctrine of the
will — all of it is in the Order's material.

**Into Theosophy.** Blavatsky quotes and argues with him extensively in *Isis Unveiled*.

**And into American Freemasonry.** Albert Pike read him in French, and used him — very
extensively and almost entirely without attribution — in *Morals and Dogma*, which was
published in 1871 and given to Scottish Rite initiates for the following century.

That last route is the one this book has been traveling toward since Chapter 1.

Because it means that when an American Mason opened the standard commentary on his own
degrees and found Kabbalah, the astral light, the pentagram, comparative mythology and the
symbolism of the pillars as Severity and Mercy — he was not reading an ancient Masonic
tradition.

He was reading a Parisian who had been in prison for socialism fifteen years before the
book was written.

---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-SIX
## The Occult Renaissance

The nineteenth century was, by a wide margin, the most religiously inventive period in
European history since the Reformation, and the inventions came from an unexpected
direction.

Not from the churches, which were losing ground. Not from the universities, which were
professionalising. From a diffuse, commercial, largely middle-class culture of seances,
lectures, periodicals, correspondence courses and small orders — an ecosystem that
historians now call the occult revival, and that produced, within about fifty years, nearly
every institution and idea that constitutes modern Western esotericism.

This chapter maps the ecosystem, because the next seven chapters take place inside it.

---

### Mesmer's afterlife

Begin with a Viennese physician who had been discredited by a French royal commission in
1784, with Benjamin Franklin on it.

Franz Anton Mesmer had proposed a universal magnetic fluid, blockages in which caused
illness, and which a practitioner could redirect. The commission — using what were in
effect blind trials — concluded that the effects were real but were produced by
imagination, not by any fluid.

That finding should have ended it. Instead, mesmerism flourished for another century, and it
did so because the commission had inadvertently identified something more interesting than
what it disproved. If imagination could produce these effects, then imagination was a
physical force, and the investigation of it was a science.

Nineteenth-century mesmerism produced trance states, somnambulism, clairvoyance,
anaesthesia, and a very large body of reported phenomena. It is one of the ancestors of
hypnosis and of dynamic psychiatry. It also supplied the occult revival with three things
it could not have done without: a *medium* through which invisible influence travels, a
*technique* for inducing altered states, and a *vocabulary* that sounded scientific.

Lévi's astral light is mesmeric fluid with a cosmology attached.

---

### Spiritualism

On 31 March 1848, in a farmhouse at Hydesville, New York, two sisters named Fox reported
that rapping noises in the house were answering their questions.

Within five years spiritualism was a mass movement on two continents. Within a decade it
had millions of adherents, its own press, its own lecture circuit and its own celebrity
mediums. It reached the highest levels of society — there were seances at the White House —
and it retained a large following into the twentieth century, boosted by the bereavement of
the First World War.

Spiritualism is not the same thing as occultism, and the distinction was fought over at the
time. Spiritualism was broadly democratic, Christian in tone, and focused on contact with
the dead. Occultism was hierarchical, initiatory, and interested in the operator's
development rather than in messages from Aunt Mary. Blavatsky, who began in spiritualist
circles, spent much of her later career distinguishing herself from it, sometimes rudely.

But spiritualism did something indispensable: it created the audience. It produced, in two
generations, a large public accustomed to the idea that invisible intelligences could be
contacted, that special people could do it, and that this was a matter for investigation
rather than dogma. Every occult order of the later nineteenth century recruited from that
public.

---

### The publishing machine

The revival ran on print, and this is the least glamorous and most important fact about it.

Cheap steam printing, rising literacy, a postal system, and a market for periodicals
produced an infrastructure that had not existed before. There were occult publishers —
George Redway, who printed Mathers's *Kabbalah Unveiled*; John M. Watkins, still trading a
century later — occult booksellers, occult magazines, and a second-hand trade in old
esoteric books that made Agrippa, the grimoires and the alchemical corpus available to
anyone with a few shillings and an address.

This is the mechanism by which Part II reaches Part VI. The Renaissance magical library did
not survive in a secret order. It survived in *the antiquarian book trade*, and it was
reassembled in the nineteenth century by collectors.

Alison Butler's reconstruction of the two most important English collections makes the
point precisely. Frederick Hockley, a working occultist of the mid-Victorian period, spent
decades assembling and copying magical manuscripts. William Wynn Westcott built a Hermetic
library of two hundred and eighty-six items, weighted heavily toward seventeenth- and
eighteenth-century cabalistic, Rosicrucian, Hermetic and Neoplatonic sources.[^1]

When we ask, in Chapter 41, where the Golden Dawn's material came from, this is the answer.
It came off a shelf, and we know what was on the shelf.

---

### The French scene

Paris after Lévi produced an institutional occultism with a distinctly French character:
literary, quarrelsome, and connected to both Catholic traditionalism and the political left.

Its central organizer was Gérard Encausse, a physician who wrote as **Papus**. He was
enormously productive — his *Traité méthodique de science occulte* (1891) is a synthesis on
the Lévi model — and enormously energetic as a founder of things. Chief among them was the
revived **Martinist Order**, which took its name and claimed its descent from Louis-Claude
de Saint-Martin, whom we met in Chapter 27.

That claim is worth a sentence of scrutiny. Saint-Martin had no order; he had readers, and
a deliberate suspicion of ceremonial organization. The nineteenth-century Martinist Order is
a reconstruction on the model of what its founders thought Saint-Martin's circle had been,
using materials they had assembled themselves. The continuity is doctrinal and literary,
not institutional.

It is the same pattern as everywhere else in this part of the book: a real inheritance of
*texts*, dressed as an inheritance of *organization*.

The French scene also produced Stanislas de Guaita's Kabbalistic Order of the Rose-Cross,
Joséphin Péladan's aesthetic Rosicrucian salons, and a series of public feuds that provided
excellent copy for the Parisian press — which matters more than it sounds, because that
press coverage is the environment in which Léo Taxil would shortly find his market.

---

### The English pipeline

And now the institutional route that this book cares about most.

In 1866, Robert Wentworth Little and a circle of English Masons founded the **Societas
Rosicruciana in Anglia**. Its material was drawn substantially from the German Rosicrucian
tradition of Chapter 28 — its grade names are recognizably those of the Gold- und
Rosenkreuz — and its purpose was the study of esoteric subjects.

Its entry requirement was that a candidate be a Master Mason in good standing.[^2]

That single condition is the pipeline. It means that in Victorian England, the route into
organized esoteric study ran *through the Craft*. A man interested in Kabbalah, alchemy or
Hermetic philosophy who wanted to pursue it in company had to be a Freemason first.

The consequences are visible in the membership. Kenneth Mackenzie, compiler of the *Royal
Masonic Cyclopaedia* and a man who had met Lévi. Frederick Hockley. William Robert Woodman,
a physician, who became Supreme Magus. William Wynn Westcott, a coroner. Samuel Liddell
Mathers.

The three men who founded the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn in 1888 were all
Freemasons, and they met each other in a Rosicrucian society that required Masonic
membership to join.

**This is the documented junction between Freemasonry and modern ceremonial magic.** Not
Egypt. Not the Templars. Not a hidden tradition inside the Craft. A London Masonic
Rosicrucian society founded in 1866, whose members had access to a second-hand book trade
and a great deal of leisure.

---

### The shape of the ecosystem

Set it out and the structure is clear.

**Mesmerism** supplies the theory of subtle influence and the technique of trance.
**Spiritualism** supplies the mass audience and the experiential expectation.
**The publishing and antiquarian trade** supplies the primary texts.
**Lévi** supplies the synthesis — a coherent doctrine linking will, imagination,
correspondence, Kabbalah and tarot.
**Freemasonry** supplies the organizational form: grades, initiation, obligation, officers,
temple, secrecy.

Five inputs. And the thing assembled from them — the Victorian magical order — is the
direct ancestor of everything in Western esotericism today.

Note what is *not* in the list. There is no ancient lineage. There is no unbroken
transmission. There is no surviving secret society from an earlier age.

Every component is identifiable, datable and modern, except the printed texts — and those
were bought in shops.

That is the occult revival, and it is one of the most impressive acts of collective
creative synthesis in modern European cultural history. It deserves to be admired for what
it was, rather than for what its makers claimed it was.

Two of them, though, would spend a great deal of effort on the claim. And one of them, in
Charleston, South Carolina, had already put Lévi's system into a Masonic book without
mentioning where he got it.

---

[^5]: On spiritualism's scale and social reach, see Alex Owen, *The Place of Enchantment:
