---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-SEVEN
## Pike and the Architecture of Morals

He was a very large man — six feet tall and heavy, with hair to his shoulders and a beard
to his chest, in an era when that was a statement. He had been a schoolteacher in
Massachusetts, a wanderer in the Southwest, a newspaper editor in Arkansas, a lawyer who
argued Indian claims before the Supreme Court, an officer in two wars, and a poet of some
local reputation.

He read Latin, Greek, Hebrew, French, Spanish, and taught himself Sanskrit in his fifties
in order to read the Vedas. His personal library ran to thousands of volumes and he had
read them. He kept a commonplace book. He wrote, over a long life, an enormous quantity of
material, most of it about symbols.

He also commanded a brigade of Confederate Indian troops at the battle of Pea Ridge in
March 1862, after which he was accused — by his own side as well as the other — of failing
to control them and of atrocities committed against Union wounded. He resigned his
commission in bitterness, quarrelled with the Confederate authorities, was briefly arrested,
and spent the rest of the war in retreat. He was indicted for treason after it and
pardoned.[^1]

Albert Pike is not a comfortable figure and I am not going to make him one. But he is the
most important single author in the history of American Freemasonry, and what he actually
wrote is almost entirely unknown to the people who argue about him.

---

### The book

Pike became Sovereign Grand Commander of the Ancient and Accepted Scottish Rite, Southern
Jurisdiction, in 1859, and held the office until his death in 1891. He found a degree system
that was, by his own assessment, in poor condition — a set of rituals of French origin,
variously transmitted, inconsistently worked, with thin content.

He rewrote them. And in 1871 he published a commentary on them: *Morals and Dogma of the
Ancient and Accepted Scottish Rite of Freemasonry*, about eight hundred and sixty pages,
one chapter for each of the thirty-two degrees.

For the next century it was given to every initiate in the Southern Jurisdiction. Most, by
universal report, did not read it. It is long, it is dense, it has no index in the original,
and its method is not obvious.

---

### What is actually in it

*Morals and Dogma* is a work of comparative mythology and moral philosophy organized around
Masonic symbols. Its method is the concordance of Chapter 8 — Pico's method, four hundred
years on.

For any given degree, Pike will take its symbol and pursue it across traditions: through
Egyptian religion, Zoroastrianism, the Vedas, Greek philosophy, the Hebrew scriptures,
Gnosticism, Kabbalah, Christianity, Islam, alchemy and Hermeticism, assembling parallels
and drawing a moral conclusion. The learning is genuine and the range is enormous. So is
the credulity: Pike's sources for ancient religion are the comparative mythology of the
mid-nineteenth century, which was frequently wrong, and he had no way to know it.

The conclusions are almost invariably moral and almost invariably unsurprising: the initiate
should pursue truth, resist tyranny, exercise charity, subdue his passions, tolerate the
beliefs of others, and work for the improvement of mankind. Pike was a nineteenth-century
liberal moralist with an immense library, and the book's destination is nearly always a
Victorian virtue.

He also said, in the preface, something that almost nobody quotes: that the work was
prepared for the use of the Rite, that everyone is free to reject any of its opinions, and
that he did not intend to impose them.[^2]

That disclaimer is not decoration. It is a direct consequence of Anderson's formula of
1723. Pike could not promulgate doctrine — no Masonic body can — so he offered
interpretation and expressly disclaimed authority for it.

His readers, and still more his enemies, ignored the disclaimer entirely.

---

### The sources

Now the thing that changes everything, and it is recent.

For a century it was possible to read *Morals and Dogma* as Pike's own synthesis. It is
written in a single voice. It quotes sparingly. It does not, in the original edition,
identify most of its sources.

In 2011 the Scottish Rite published an annotated edition prepared by Arturo de Hoyos, the
Rite's Grand Archivist, containing several thousand critical notes. De Hoyos had gone
through the book against Pike's library and identified, line by line, where the material
came from.[^3]

A great deal of it is unattributed transcription.

Pike took, often verbatim or nearly so, from a range of nineteenth-century authors. And the
single largest identified source is **Éliphas Lévi**.

The astral light is in Pike because it is in Lévi. The Kabbalistic material, the tarot
associations, the pentagram, the interpretation of the pillars as Severity and Mercy, the
discussion of the Sephiroth, substantial passages of comparative religion — these are
Lévi's, published in Paris between 1854 and 1861, appearing in Charleston in 1871 without
a name attached.

---

### What this means

It means that the esoteric content of American Scottish Rite Masonry — which is to say, the
material that generations of American Masons and their critics have taken to be the
fraternity's ancient inner teaching — was written by a French ex-socialist fifteen years
before it appeared in the Masonic book.

Let me put the chronology in a single line, because it is the spine of this book's
argument.

**1854–56:** Lévi publishes *Dogme et rituel* in Paris.
**1871:** Pike publishes *Morals and Dogma* in Charleston, incorporating Lévi extensively
and without attribution.
**1871–1974:** Every initiate of the Southern Jurisdiction receives a copy.
**Thereafter:** Kabbalah, the astral light, Hermeticism and the pentagram are widely
understood — by Masons and anti-Masons alike — to be ancient Masonic doctrine.

The material entered the tradition within living memory of the men who first read it. It
arrived in a book that its own author described as a collection of opinions the reader was
free to reject.

---

### Was it plagiarism?

The question is anachronistic but worth answering.

Nineteenth-century practice on attribution was looser than ours, especially in works of
popular synthesis, and Pike was compiling rather than claiming originality. He did
acknowledge a general debt to earlier writers in places. He was not passing off a novel
theory as his own; he was assembling a commentary.

Against that: the scale is very large, the borrowing is often close, and the effect —
whether intended or not — was that thousands of readers took Lévi's nineteenth-century
synthesis for the ancient wisdom of the Craft.

De Hoyos's own judgment, from inside the institution, is worth noting: he published the
findings. The Scottish Rite's Grand Archivist produced the edition that demonstrates the
extent of its most famous author's unacknowledged borrowing, and the Rite printed it.

That is what intellectual honesty looks like in an institution, and it is worth recording
in a chapter that will otherwise be quoted by people with other purposes.

---

### Pike's actual project

What was he trying to do?

I think the answer is visible in the book's structure and in his career, and it is neither
sinister nor mystical.

Pike inherited a degree system with thin content, in a country whose Masonry had been
nearly destroyed by the Morgan affair forty years earlier and had rebuilt itself on
respectability. He wanted to give it intellectual weight. He believed — with the whole
*prisca theologia* tradition behind him — that the world's religions descended from a
common primitive truth, that Masonry's symbols were fragments of it, and that a man who
studied them across traditions would arrive at a universal and tolerant morality.

That is a nineteenth-century liberal Protestant-deist project dressed in comparative
mythology. It is the same project as Anderson's religion in which all men agree, pursued
with a much larger library.

And it failed in a specific and instructive way. Pike wanted to fill the Craft's vacancy
with content. But he had no authority to make content binding — the institution's own
constitution forbade it — so what he produced was a vast, learned, unauthorised
interpretation that sat inside the tradition without ever being the tradition.

Which meant that it could be quoted by anybody, for any purpose, as though it were.

Within twenty-three years of its publication, a French journalist would print a letter
Pike never wrote, attribute to him a doctrine he never held, and use *Morals and Dogma* as
corroboration.

The passages that made it credible were about a figure Pike did discuss, at length, in
terms that are strange, genuine, and not at all what the accusation says.

---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-EIGHT
## Lucifer and Baphomet

Two words, taken out of two paragraphs, have done more damage to Freemasonry's reputation
than three centuries of anything else.

This chapter reads the paragraphs.

---

### The Latin word

*Lucifer* is Latin and it means light-bearer. In classical usage it is the name of the
morning star, the planet Venus seen before dawn. It has no diabolical content whatever.

It acquires one by a specific route. In Isaiah 14, the prophet mocks the fallen king of
Babylon: *How art thou fallen from heaven, O Lucifer, son of the morning* — in the
Authorised Version's rendering of the Vulgate's rendering of a Hebrew phrase meaning
shining one, or day-star. The passage is about a human king. Early Christian commentators,
reading it alongside a verse in Luke about Satan falling like lightning, applied it to the
devil, and the identification stuck so thoroughly that in English *Lucifer* became simply a
name for Satan.

So the word carries two meanings in the nineteenth century, and educated writers used both.
When a classically trained author writes *Lucifer*, he may mean the devil, or he may mean
the morning star, or he may be deliberately playing on the distance between them.

Pike was a classically trained author.

---

### The passage

The notorious passage occurs in the chapter on the nineteenth degree. Its subject is the
symbolism of light, and it is written in Pike's comparative-mythological manner, discussing
how ancient systems used the morning star and how the meaning of names has shifted.

Its substance — I paraphrase deliberately, and will explain why — is that Lucifer, the
light-bearer, is a strange and mysterious name to give to the spirit of darkness; that it is
the morning star, the bearer of light, whose brightness dazzles feeble souls; and that the
reader should not doubt it, since traditions are full of divine revelations and
inspirations, and inspiration is of no one age and no one creed.

I have paraphrased rather than quoted for a specific reason, and the reader should know
it. This passage exists in the wild in a dozen mutilated forms, some of them produced by
splicing Pike's sentences with sentences from the Taxil forgery. Quoting it accurately
requires the 1871 edition or de Hoyos's annotated text, and the wording should be verified
against those before it appears in print in a book of this kind. I have marked it
accordingly in the notes.[^1]

What can be said with confidence about its content is this.

It is a passage about **the history of a name**. Pike's point is that a word meaning
light-bearer came to designate the spirit of darkness, and that this is odd. He is doing
philology and comparative religion, in a chapter about the symbolism of light, in a book
whose method is precisely the tracing of symbols across traditions.

It is not devotional. Nothing in it asks the reader to venerate anything. Read in its
paragraph and its chapter, it is a scholarly observation of exactly the kind Pike makes
several hundred times in that book about several hundred other symbols.

And its source is substantially Lévi, who had discussed the same material in the 1850s.

---

### Why it was vulnerable

That said, I want to be fair to the passage's critics, because a purely defensive reading
misses something real.

Pike wrote a passage using the word *Lucifer* in a positive register, in a book given to
tens of thousands of Masons, in a nineteenth-century Christian society, without apparently
anticipating how it would look if extracted.

That was a serious misjudgement of his audience, and it was not the only one. The same
chapter and its neighbors contain a great deal of material about dualism, about the
Gnostics, about the equal antiquity of light and darkness, and about the limitations of
dogmatic religion, all written in a tone of Olympian comparison that assumes a reader with
Pike's own detachment.

Most readers did not have it. And the passage was extracted, within twenty-three years, by
somebody who knew exactly what he was doing.

---

### Baphomet: the name

The second word has a stranger history and it is worth doing properly, because it is a
model case of the book's method.

**Stage one, 1307–1312.** In the interrogations of the Knights Templar, conducted under
torture by French royal officials, some Templars confessed to venerating an idol or a head.
The descriptions are wildly inconsistent — a bearded head, a cat, a figure with multiple
faces, a skull. The name recorded in some depositions is *Baphomet*, or variants of it.

The most plausible scholarly explanation of the name is that it is a corruption of
*Mahomet*, Muhammad — a standard medieval slander in which any heresy could be described as
Islamic. It appears in Provençal sources in exactly that sense.

Whether the Templars venerated anything at all is doubtful; confessions under torture
establish what the torturers wanted. No idol was ever produced.

**Stage two, the eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries.** The name survives in
antiquarian discussion of the Templar trials, and is picked up by writers seeking to prove
that the Templars were Gnostics, or Ophites, or the survivors of some mystery tradition.
Joseph von Hammer-Purgstall's writings in the 1810s are the most influential of these, and
were contested at the time.

**Stage three, 1856.** Éliphas Lévi draws his goat-headed figure and captions it the
Sabbatic Goat, the Baphomet of Mendes. It is his own composition, assembled from the
Templar name, a passage in Herodotus about goat-worship at Mendes in Egypt, the iconography
of the witches' sabbath, the alchemical formula *solve et coagula*, and a doctrine of
equilibrium. It is a pantacle summarising his system.

**Stage four, 1871.** Pike discusses Baphomet in the Templar material of the Scottish Rite
degrees, reproducing much of the nineteenth-century antiquarian discussion, including Lévi's.

**Stage five, 1894.** Someone puts stages one, three and four together and tells the world
that Freemasons worship the goat-headed figure.

**Stage six, the twentieth century onward.** Lévi's drawing becomes the standard visual
representation of Satan in Western popular culture, appearing in films, on records, and
eventually as a bronze statue erected by a modern religious organization making a First
Amendment point.

---

### The lesson of the chain

Look at the sequence again.

A name extracted under torture in 1307, probably a corruption of Muhammad, attached to an
idol that was never produced. Antiquarian speculation five centuries later. A drawing by a
French occultist in 1856 illustrating a doctrine of balance. A comparative discussion by an
American Freemason in 1871. A forged accusation in 1894.

At no point does anyone worship anything.

And yet the modern popular understanding — that Baphomet is an ancient demon venerated by
Templars and inherited by Freemasons — has all the appearance of a continuous tradition. It
has a name that recurs across seven centuries. It has documents. It has an image.

Run the six questions on it and it dissolves at every step. The name has no established
referent. The transmission between stages is transmission of *a word and a picture*, not of
a practice. The image is 1856. The accusation is 1894 and was confessed as a fabrication
three years later.

This is what a manufactured tradition looks like from inside. Not a lie told once, but a
series of people each handing on something slightly transformed, most of them acting in good
faith, until the accumulated object has a solidity that none of its components ever had.

It is the same process that produced the Templar descent in Chapter 26, the Rosicrucian
ancestry in Chapter 12, and the Egyptian origins in Chapter 6. And it is the process that
produced the single most successful piece of anti-Masonic propaganda ever written, which
was published in Paris in the 1890s by a man who had already confessed to inventing
everything he wrote, and which is still believed.

---

# CHAPTER THIRTY-NINE
## Taxil's Devil

On 19 April 1897, in a hall in Paris, a crowd assembled to meet a woman who did not exist.

For eleven years, French Catholic opinion had been following the most sensational religious
story of the age. A converted anti-Masonic writer had been publishing revelations of a
satanic cult operating at the summit of Freemasonry — a body called the Palladium, directed
from Charleston, South Carolina, by the American Sovereign Grand Commander Albert Pike, at
which demons were invoked and the Eucharist desecrated. His principal witness was a former
high priestess of the cult named Diana Vaughan, who had converted to Catholicism, gone into
hiding for fear of Masonic assassination, and been publishing her memoirs.

She had been praised by cardinals. A book of Eucharistic devotions published under her name
had reportedly received papal commendation. An international anti-Masonic congress at
Trent in 1896 had debated her existence.

The meeting of 19 April was announced as the occasion on which she would appear.

The man who took the platform was Léo Taxil, and what he delivered instead was a
confession.[^1]

---

### The hoax

Gabriel Jogand-Pagès had been, in the 1870s and early 1880s, an aggressive anticlerical
journalist and pornographer, publishing scurrilous attacks on the Church under the name Léo
Taxil. He had been prosecuted. He had a taste for elaborate practical jokes — an earlier
one involved convincing the press that the bottom of Lake Geneva was full of sunken Roman
ships.

In 1885 he announced his conversion to Catholicism, renounced his former work, and offered
his services against the Church's enemies. He had also, briefly, been initiated into
Freemasonry and expelled.

The timing was excellent. In 1884 Leo XIII had issued *Humanum genus*, the most substantial
papal condemnation of Freemasonry, calling for the faithful to expose it. Taxil supplied
the exposure.

Over the following twelve years he produced, under his own name and several pseudonyms, a
library of anti-Masonic revelation: *Les Mystères de la franc-maçonnerie*, *Les Sœurs
maçonnes*, and — through a collaborator writing as Dr Bataille — an enormous serial called
*Le Diable au XIXe siècle*, full of demons, electrical devices, crocodile-riding and
mechanical marvels, which is very obviously a joke to a modern reader and was not taken as
one.

Then Diana Vaughan. She was, on the evidence of the confession, a real person — an employee
of an American typewriter company with whom Taxil was in contact — whose name was used for
a fictional character. She produced memoirs, devotional works, and letters. She never
appeared in public.

---

### The Pike letter

The element that outlived everything else was a document.

In 1894, Abel Clarin de la Rive published *La Femme et l'enfant dans la franc-maçonnerie
universelle*, drawing on the Taxil material, and printed what purported to be an extract
from instructions issued by Albert Pike to the twenty-three Supreme Councils of the world on
14 July 1889.

The extract has Pike saying that the Masonic religion should be maintained in the purity of
the Luciferian doctrine; that Lucifer is God, and unfortunately Adonay is also God; that
the true and pure philosophic religion is the belief in Lucifer, the equal of Adonay.[^2]

It is a forgery. It has no source, no original, no archival existence. Pike died in 1891 and
the Supreme Councils of the world received no such instruction. De la Rive himself, after
Taxil's confession, publicly acknowledged that he had been deceived.

And it has been quoted continuously ever since — in anti-Masonic tracts, in fundamentalist
publications, in the twentieth-century conspiracy literature, in books that sold in the
millions in the 1980s and 1990s, and now, endlessly, online. It is frequently spliced with
genuine Pike sentences to produce composite quotations that are harder to check.

---

### The confession

Taxil advertised the meeting of 19 April 1897 as the unveiling of Diana Vaughan. Journalists
came. Clergy came. Freemasons came, curious. He had arranged for a stenographer.

He announced that there was no Diana Vaughan of the kind described, no Palladium, no
satanic Masonry, and no Luciferian doctrine. He had invented all of it. He had done it, he
said, to make fools of his opponents and to demonstrate the credulity of the clergy who had
believed him, and he thanked them for their assistance. He had, he noted, been received and
praised at the highest levels on the strength of documents he had written himself.

The confession was printed in full in *Le Frondeur* on 25 April 1897 under the title
"Twelve Years under the Banner of the Church."[^3]

Accounts of the meeting describe uproar. Taxil is said to have left under police protection.

---

### Why it did not die

Here is the part that matters for this book, and it is the reason the Taxil affair earns a
chapter rather than a footnote.

**The confession did not kill the hoax.**

The Palladium continued to be cited. The Pike letter continued to be printed. Anti-Masonic
writers in the twentieth century reproduced the material, sometimes acknowledging Taxil's
confession and arguing that the confession was itself the lie, sometimes not mentioning it
at all. The material passed into English-language fundamentalist publishing in the
mid-twentieth century and from there into the general conspiracy literature, where it
remains.

A fabrication, publicly confessed by its author in front of the press, survived its own
confession by more than a hundred and twenty years.

Why?

**Because the confession is harder to transmit than the claim.** A claim about devil-worship
is memorable, transmissible in one sentence, and emotionally charged. Its refutation
requires context, dates and a name that means nothing to most readers. In any competition
between the two, the claim wins on structure alone.

**Because the claim served needs that the refutation did not.** People who wanted
Freemasonry to be satanic in 1897 still wanted it in 1930 and 1980. The evidence was never
what was doing the work.

**Because corroboration was available, of a sort.** Pike had written about Lucifer. The
passage existed. A reader who checked found something that looked like confirmation, and
the distance between Pike's philological aside and Taxil's invented doctrine required more
attention than most readers gave it. This is the most instructive feature of the whole
affair: **the forgery worked because there was a real text near it.** Fabrications attach
themselves to genuine material, and the genuine material lends them weight.

**And because it entered the record.** Once a quotation is printed, it can be cited. Once it
is cited, the citation can be cited. Within a few iterations the original is unreachable and
the claim is supported by a wall of references, all of which lead back to a book by a man
who confessed he made it up.

That is circular sourcing, and Taxil is the purest specimen of it in the history of this
subject.

---

### What the affair actually demonstrates

Three things, and none of them is about Freemasonry.

**One: about the Church.** Taxil's real target was clerical credulity, and he hit it. The
willingness of substantial parts of French Catholic opinion to accept, without verification,
an increasingly baroque account of satanic ritual from a recent convert with a history of
fraud is the actual scandal, and the Church's more careful minds said so at the time — there
were skeptics throughout, and some of them were vocal.

**Two: about the anti-Masonic tradition.** The most famous evidence in the entire history of
anti-Masonry is a confessed fabrication. Any body of argument whose central document is
Taxil's has a problem that no amount of subsequent material repairs.

**Three: about how false history propagates.** This is the general lesson and it is why the
chapter sits where it does. The Taxil affair is a controlled experiment in the manufacture
of belief, conducted in public, with a full confession at the end — and it still shows a
positive result a century later.

Everything in Chapter 50 about the Illuminati, the Priory of Sion and the rest follows the
same mechanics. Taxil is simply the case where we have the confession.

---

### A closing note on Pike

Albert Pike died on 2 April 1891, in Washington, three years before de la Rive printed the
forged letter and six years before Taxil confessed.

He never saw the accusation. He had no opportunity to answer it. And his reputation in the
general public, for more than a century since, has consisted almost entirely of a doctrine
he did not hold, attributed to him by a man who admitted inventing it, quoting a letter that
does not exist.

He was a difficult and in some respects an unattractive man, and the worst things that can
truthfully be said about him concern a battlefield in Arkansas in 1862, not a devil.

The second most famous thing about him is that he plagiarised Éliphas Lévi.

The most famous thing about him is not true.

---

# CHAPTER FORTY
## Theosophy and the Co-Masons

In November 1875, in New York, a small group founded a society for the study of unexplained
laws of nature and the powers latent in man. Its principal founders were a retired Union
army colonel and lawyer named Henry Steel Olcott, an Irish-American lawyer named William
Quan Judge, and a Russian émigrée of formidable presence and uncertain biography named
Helena Petrovna Blavatsky.

Within twenty years the Theosophical Society had branches on every continent, had relocated
its headquarters to India, had produced two enormous and enormously influential books, had
contributed materially to Indian and Sinhalese nationalist revival, and had become the
single most important channel by which Asian religious ideas entered Western popular
culture.

It had also acquired a relationship with Freemasonry that is more consequential, and much
less discussed, than anything in the Golden Dawn.

---

### Blavatsky's project

*Isis Unveiled* appeared in 1877 in two volumes; *The Secret Doctrine* in 1888, in two
more. Their argument, stated at its simplest, is the *prisca theologia* of Chapter 6 on a
global scale.

There exists, Blavatsky maintained, a single ancient wisdom-religion, of immense antiquity,
from which all the world's religions and philosophies descend as fragments and corruptions.
It has been preserved by a brotherhood of adepts — the Masters, or Mahatmas — who live in
seclusion, principally in Tibet, and who direct the spiritual evolution of humanity from
behind the scenes. She was, she said, their agent, and portions of her books were dictated
or impressed by them.

The structure will be familiar by now. It is the Rosicrucian template of Chapter 12 —
hidden superiors, ancient wisdom, a coming transformation — scaled up and relocated from
Germany to the Himalayas.

The scholarly assessment of her sources has been carried out and is not in serious dispute.
The Asian material derives substantially from the Orientalist scholarship available in her
day, and her presentation of Buddhism and Hinduism is filtered through Victorian
interpretation to a degree that modern scholars of those traditions find striking. The
Western esoteric material comes from the nineteenth-century occult library: Lévi
prominently, along with the standard Hermetic and Kabbalistic sources.[^1]

Some of the Mahatma letters were investigated by the Society for Psychical Research in 1885,
which concluded they were produced by Blavatsky — a finding the SPR substantially revisited
a century later, in a report noting the deficiencies of the original investigation. The
question of what Blavatsky was doing, and how much she believed, remains genuinely open and
I do not propose to settle it here.

---

### What Theosophy took from Masonry

Blavatsky wrote about Freemasonry frequently, and her position was consistent and
characteristic: that the Craft preserved genuine fragments of the ancient wisdom in its
symbols, that its members had almost entirely forgotten what they meant, and that Theosophy
could supply the interpretation.

That formula — *you have the symbols, we have the meaning* — is exactly the vacancy of
Chapter 16 being filled from outside, and it is the same move that Pike had made in
Charleston four years before *Isis Unveiled*.

But Theosophy went further than commentary. It acquired Masonic ritual, and the route by
which it did so is the interesting part.

---

### Co-Masonry

Recall Chapter 29. In 1882 Maria Deraismes was initiated in a French lodge; in 1893, with
Georges Martin, she founded *Le Droit Humain*, which admitted men and women equally in the
same lodges, working a full Masonic ritual.

In 1902 Co-Masonry came to England, and its principal promoter was **Annie Besant**.

Besant was already one of the most remarkable public figures of her generation: a former
clergyman's wife who had lost her faith, a secularist lecturer, a birth-control campaigner
prosecuted for obscenity, a Fabian socialist, the organizer of the 1888 London matchgirls'
strike, and — after reading *The Secret Doctrine* for review — a Theosophist. She became
president of the Theosophical Society in 1907 and, in the following decades, a significant
figure in Indian politics, president of the Indian National Congress in 1917.

Besant took Co-Masonry and made it an instrument. Lodges were established across Britain,
India, Australia, America and elsewhere, frequently in association with Theosophical
branches, and a body of theosophical interpretation was supplied to the ritual — most
influentially by Charles Webster Leadbeater, whose books on Masonic symbolism read the
degrees in terms of chakras, subtle bodies, Masters and rays.[^2]

---

### The point that gets missed

Now the observation this chapter exists for, and it is one that neither Masonic nor occult
historiography likes.

**The most successful transmission of Masonic ritual into the twentieth-century esoteric
world did not run through regular male Freemasonry. It ran through women's and mixed
Masonry, which regular Freemasonry did not recognize.**

Consider what Co-Masonry achieved. It carried full Masonic ritual — not a derivative, not an
imitation, but the working degrees — into an international spiritual movement with tens of
thousands of members on five continents, with a developed esoteric interpretation attached,
during exactly the decades when Western esotericism was taking its modern shape.

Nothing the regular Craft did in the same period had a comparable effect on the esoteric
world, for the straightforward reason that the regular Craft was not trying to have one.

And the irony is precise. The bodies excluded from Masonic history as irregular are the ones
that did the most to spread Masonic ritual beyond the fraternity. The exclusion did not
prevent the transmission. It merely ensured that the transmission happened somewhere the
Craft could not influence it.

---

### Krishnamurti

One episode deserves recording, because it is the best illustration in this book of what
happens when an esoteric organization's own machinery is turned against it.

In 1909 Leadbeater identified a fourteen-year-old Indian boy, Jiddu Krishnamurti, as the
vehicle for a coming World Teacher. The Theosophical Society organized around this: an
Order of the Star in the East was founded, with tens of thousands of members, property, and
an apparatus of expectation. Krishnamurti was educated in England, groomed for the role, and
presented internationally.

In 1929, at a gathering in the Netherlands, he dissolved the Order and resigned from it. His
statement is one of the more remarkable documents of twentieth-century religion. Truth, he
said, is a pathless land, and cannot be approached by any path whatsoever, by any religion,
by any sect. He did not want followers. Organizations, he argued, become crutches, and the
moment you organize a truth you kill it.[^3]

He spent the following sixty years lecturing, to large audiences, against the idea that
anyone should listen to a teacher.

I include this because it is the only case in this book of a person at the center of an
initiatory system looking at the apparatus and declining it on principle — and because his
objection is, in essence, the objection that Saint-Martin had reached in the 1780s and that
Waite would reach in the 1900s, stated more radically than either managed.

---

### Theosophy's legacy

Whatever one concludes about Blavatsky, the Society's effects are enormous and mostly
unacknowledged.

It introduced karma, reincarnation, and a vocabulary of spiritual evolution into Western
popular culture on a scale nothing else approached. It contributed to the revival of
Buddhism in Ceylon and to Indian cultural nationalism, in ways Indian and Sri Lankan
historians have documented. It shaped modern astrology, the New Age movement, and a great
deal of twentieth-century alternative spirituality. Through Rudolf Steiner, who broke from
it, it produced anthroposophy and Waldorf education. Through Besant, it carried Masonic
ritual around the world.

And it did all of this on the strength of a claim — an ancient brotherhood of Masters
directing human evolution from Tibet — for which there has never been a scrap of evidence of
the kind Chapter 5 requires.

Which is, by now, the most familiar finding in this book. The claim is unsupported. The
consequences are real, large, and still with us.

The Masters are the Unknown Superiors again. Everybody in this story wants somebody above
them, and when nobody is there, they are supplied.

Nowhere more elaborately than in a London flat in 1887, where a coroner produced
correspondence with a German adept who did not exist, and founded the most influential
magical order in history on it.

---

# CHAPTER FORTY-ONE
## The Golden Dawn

The Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn existed, in its original form, for about twelve
years. At its peak it had perhaps three hundred members. It fell apart in a series of
quarrels so undignified that one of them ended in a London police court.

It is the most influential magical organization in history, and essentially everything in
modern Western ceremonial magic descends from it.

---

### The founding

The story the Order told about itself ran as follows.

In 1887 a manuscript came into the hands of William Wynn Westcott, a London coroner, Master
Mason, and Secretary General of the Societas Rosicruciana in Anglia. It was written in
cipher — a substitution alphabet taken from a sixteenth-century work by the abbot
Trithemius — and, decoded, contained the outlines of five initiation rituals and a body of
Kabbalistic and symbolic material.

Among the pages was the address of a German adept, Fräulein Anna Sprengel of Nuremberg.
Westcott wrote to her. She replied, granting him authority to found a temple in England, and
conferring honorary grades on him and on his colleagues. In 1888 the Isis-Urania Temple of
the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn was constituted in London, with three Chiefs: Westcott;
Samuel Liddell Mathers, a translator and ritualist of unstable temperament and immense
industry; and William Robert Woodman, physician and Supreme Magus of the SRIA.

In 1890 Fräulein Sprengel died, and the German connection lapsed.[^1]

---

### The warrant

Ellic Howe's documentary study of the Order, published in 1972, examined the Sprengel
correspondence and concluded that it is spurious.

The grounds are straightforward. The German is defective in ways characteristic of an
English speaker writing German rather than of a German writing German. The addresses do not
check out. No such person has been traced. No German order of the kind described has been
identified. And the correspondence conveniently terminated, by death, at the point at which
continued contact would have become difficult to sustain.

Westcott appears to have written the letters himself.[^2]

The Cipher Manuscripts are a harder question. They are real documents; their contents are
substantial; and their origin is genuinely obscure. Proposals have included Kenneth
Mackenzie — a Masonic and occult writer of the previous generation, connected to Lévi, who
died in 1886 — as the compiler, and Frederick Hockley's circle as the milieu. The material
in them is drawn from identifiable printed sources: Agrippa, the Kabbalistic literature,
Lévi, the Masonic and Rosicrucian grade systems of the SRIA, and the tarot.

Alison Butler's contribution was to stop asking where the manuscripts came from and start
asking what the founders had available. Her reconstruction of Hockley's collection and of
Westcott's Hermetic library — two hundred and eighty-six items, weighted toward
seventeenth- and eighteenth-century cabalistic, Rosicrucian, Hermetic and Neoplatonic
sources — shows that the material in the Ciphers was, in its entirety, within reach of the
men who produced them.[^3]

**No German order is required to explain the Golden Dawn. A good library and considerable
talent will do it.**

---

### The audit

So: what did the Golden Dawn take from Freemasonry?

This is the question the whole book has been traveling toward, and it deserves a proper
answer rather than a slogan. Here it is, item by item.

**Borrowed from Masonry.**

*The graded initiatory structure.* A series of degrees, conferred ceremonially, each
admitting to further teaching, with advancement controlled by superiors. The specific
sequence — Neophyte, Zelator, Theoricus, Practicus, Philosophus, then the Second Order —
comes via the SRIA from the Gold- und Rosenkreuz of Chapter 28, which is itself a Masonic-
requiring body.

*The temple.* A room arranged with officers at stations, an altar at the center, cardinal
orientation, and movement between points. Anyone who has read Chapter 17 will recognize the
blocking immediately.

*The officers.* A presiding chief, subordinate officers with defined stations and functions,
a guardian of the door. The Masonic pattern exactly.

*The obligation.* A sworn undertaking, taken kneeling at the altar, with penalties.

*Signs, grips, passwords and the examination of visitors.*

*Secrecy and staged revelation*, including the practice of withholding material until the
appropriate grade.

*Regalia*, sashes and collars, worn to indicate rank.

**Parallel and adapted.**

*The Vault of the Adepti.* The Second Order's central ceremony, the Adeptus Minor grade,
takes place in a seven-sided vault containing a pastos with the body of the founder — which
is, as Chapter 12 showed, a direct construction from the *Fama Fraternitatis* of 1614. The
*form* of a ritual drama in which the candidate encounters a dead founder is Masonic; the
*content* is Rosicrucian and taken from a printed pamphlet.

**New, and not Masonic at all.**

*The Kabbalistic grade structure.* Each grade is assigned to a Sephirah on the Tree of
Life. There is nothing of this in Masonry.

*The system of correspondences.* Elements, planets, zodiacal signs, Hebrew letters, tarot
trumps, colors, gods, plants and perfumes, all mapped onto each other and onto the Tree.
This is the Agrippan tradition of Chapter 9 fused with Lévi's tarot-Kabbalah equation of
Chapter 35 — and it is the Order's greatest single intellectual achievement.

*The practical work.* Ritual invocation and banishing; the Lesser and Greater Rituals of the
Pentagram and Hexagram; scrying; astral travel; talismanic magic; alchemy; geomancy;
divination. **None of this has any Masonic precedent whatever.** The Craft has no operative
magic; the Golden Dawn is almost entirely operative magic.

*The Enochian system.* The angelic language and tables recorded by John Dee and Edward
Kelley in the 1580s, which survived in manuscript in the British Museum and were
elaborated by the Order into a complete magical system. Egil Asprem's study traces the route
precisely — from Dee's papers to the reading room to the Order's curriculum.[^4]

*The Egyptian dress.* God-forms, god-names, the imagery of the Book of the Dead. This comes
from nineteenth-century Egyptology — real Egyptology, newly available, which the Order's
members read with enthusiasm.

*The admission of women on equal terms.* From the beginning, and pointedly. Constance Wilde,
Maud Gonne, Florence Farr, Annie Horniman and Moina Bergson Mathers were members; Farr ran
the London temple.

---

### The formulation

Stated as compactly as I can manage:

**The Golden Dawn is a Masonic machine running non-Masonic software.**

Every structural component — the grades, the temple, the officers, the oath, the staged
revelation, the regalia — is Masonic in derivation, arriving through the SRIA and the
German Rosicrucian tradition. Every operative component — the Kabbalah, the
correspondences, the invocations, the Enochian work, the Egyptian god-forms — comes from
printed books in a private library.

The founders did not find magic hidden in Freemasonry. They took the Masonic apparatus,
which they knew from the inside, because it was the best available technology for
organizing graded initiatory instruction — and they loaded it with material they had
assembled themselves from the antiquarian book trade.

That is the carriage thesis, in its most important single instance, with the components
itemised.

---

### The collapse

The Order fell apart between 1900 and 1903, and the manner of it matters.

Westcott withdrew in 1897 after his occult activities became known to his official
superiors. Mathers, increasingly autocratic and living in Paris, claimed authority from the
Secret Chiefs — invisible superiors with whom he alone was in contact, who authorised his
decisions and could not be consulted.

The London members asked for evidence. Mathers, cornered, told them that the Sprengel
letters had been forged by Westcott.

That admission — from the man with the most to lose by it — is the strongest evidence for
the forgery, and it destroyed the Order's warrant at a stroke. If the German authority was
fabricated, then the Order's claim to Rosicrucian descent rested on nothing, and the Chiefs'
authority rested on nothing.

What followed was a revolt, a schism, a series of splinter orders, and — in April 1900 — an
episode in which a young member named Aleister Crowley attempted to seize the London temple
premises on Mathers's behalf, reportedly in Highland dress and a mask, and the matter ended
in court.

The Order broke into fragments: the Stella Matutina, the Alpha et Omega, A. E. Waite's
Independent and Rectified Rite, and others.

And the mechanism of its destruction is the same mechanism that destroyed the Rite of Strict
Observance a hundred and twenty years earlier. **An organization whose authority rests on
superiors who cannot be produced will eventually be asked to produce them.** Von Hund could
not. Westcott could not. Mathers could not. In each case the demand came from within, and in
each case the organization did not survive it.

---

### Why it mattered anyway

And here is the thing that ought to be surprising and is not, by this point in the book.

The Golden Dawn's foundation was fraudulent, its leadership quarrelsome, its lifespan
twelve years and its membership tiny.

Its *system* is the basis of modern Western magic.

Israel Regardie published the rituals in four volumes between 1937 and 1940, on the
explicit grounds that the material was too valuable to be lost with the dying orders that
held it. Crowley had already published a great deal of it in *The Equinox* from 1909.
Everything that followed — Thelema, Wicca, the whole twentieth-century magical revival, the
contents of any occult bookshop today — descends from those publications.

A tradition can be enormously productive and have a fake pedigree. Those are independent
facts. The Golden Dawn's system works, in the sense that people have used it for a hundred
and thirty years and report that it does something, and none of that depends on Fräulein
Sprengel having existed.

Which raises a question the last part of this book has to answer: if the pedigree does not
matter, why did they all insist on one?

Before that, though, there is a component of the Golden Dawn's system that needs a chapter
of its own — because it is the single most successful piece of invented tradition in modern
esotericism, and its inventors were quite open about assembling it, and within a generation
everyone had forgotten.

---

# CHAPTER FORTY-TWO
## Kabbalah Reconstructed

Take a deck of tarot cards. Find the twenty-two trumps — the Fool, the Magician, the High
Priestess, and so on to the World. Now take the Tree of Life: ten Sephiroth connected by
twenty-two paths.

Twenty-two and twenty-two.

Assign each trump to a path. Assign each path a Hebrew letter, since the Hebrew alphabet
also has twenty-two letters. Now assign the four suits to the four elements, and the court
cards to the elemental subdivisions, and the numbered cards to the Sephiroth in the four
worlds. Add the planets and the zodiac, which distribute across the letters according to a
scheme in the *Sefer Yetzirah*. Add colors, in four scales. Add gods, from every pantheon.
Add perfumes, plants, stones, animals, and diseases.

You now have a system in which everything corresponds to everything else, in which any
symbol can be located on a map, and in which a practitioner can construct a ritual for any
purpose by assembling the appropriate column.

It is magnificent. It is one of the most complete symbolic architectures ever built.

And essentially all of it was constructed between 1854 and 1900, by about five people.

---

### The chain, restated

Chapter 10 laid out the links. They are worth having in front of us again, because this
chapter is where they arrive.

**Jewish Kabbalah** — the *Zohar*, the *Sefer Yetzirah*, Luria — developed within Jewish
law, liturgy and community, from the twelfth century onward.

**Pico, 1486**, extracts material for Christian apologetic use.

**Reuchlin, 1494 and 1517**, makes Christian Cabala a discipline.

**Agrippa, 1533**, folds it into operative magic as Book Three of the *Occult Philosophy*.

**Knorr von Rosenroth, 1677–84**, publishes *Kabbala Denudata*, a Latin anthology — the
decisive act of transmission, because it makes the material available to people who cannot
read Hebrew.

**Mathers, 1887**, publishes *The Kabbalah Unveiled* in English. He is translating Knorr's
Latin. He says so.

**The Golden Dawn, 1888–1900**, builds the system above on it.

Seven stages. Every one documented. Every one a book you can hold.

---

### What was added at the last stage

The Golden Dawn's additions are the substance of Hermetic Qabalah, and they have no Jewish
precedent. It is worth being specific.

**The tarot attribution.** Lévi proposed the twenty-two/twenty-two correspondence in the
1850s. The Golden Dawn made it operational, assigning specific trumps to specific paths —
and, crucially, adopting an attribution that differs from Lévi's at two points, most
famously in the placing of the Emperor and the Star. The order of the trumps on the Tree is
a Victorian editorial decision and it was argued about.

**The elemental and planetary system.** The four elements, the seven planets and the twelve
signs mapped onto the letters and paths, with the "Rose Cross" diagram binding them
together.

**The color scales.** Four scales of color, one per Kabbalistic world, assigned to every
Sephirah and path, used for visualisation and talismanic work. This is entirely the Order's
own.

**The grade system as ascent.** Each initiatory grade corresponds to a Sephirah, so that
advancing through the Order is, formally, climbing the Tree. This is the fusion point: a
*Masonic* structure of degrees mapped onto a *Kabbalistic* diagram. Neither tradition
contains it. It is made where they meet.

**The Egyptian, Greek and Hindu attributions.** God-names from every available pantheon
assigned to positions on the Tree, on the *prisca theologia* assumption that all
mythologies encode one system.

---

### What a Kabbalist would say

I want to put the objection plainly, because it is usually either ignored or made
aggressively, and it deserves neither.

From the standpoint of the Jewish tradition, Hermetic Qabalah is not a variant or a
development. It is a different thing that has taken the name.

The *sefirot* in Jewish Kabbalah are aspects of divine manifestation, held within a
theology, a liturgical practice and a legal framework. They are not a filing system for
correspondences. The letters are the substance of creation within an account of revelation,
not an indexing scheme for tarot cards. Lurianic *tikkun* — the gathering of the scattered
sparks — is a theology of exile and repair, written by and for a community that had been
expelled from Spain. The practice is inseparable from Torah, from Hebrew, from the
commandments, and from a people.

Detach the diagram from all of that, assign Egyptian gods to it, hang a card game on its
paths, and use it to structure the grades of a Victorian magical society, and you have
produced something the tradition's own practitioners would not recognize as theirs.

That is a fair criticism and it is correct.

The complicating fact is that the appropriation was not clandestine. Mathers named his
source in his title. The Golden Dawn's members were, some of them, genuinely learned in the
Hebrew literature available to them. And the chain runs back, link by link, to real Jewish
texts, however transformed.

So it is neither theft in the crude sense nor inheritance in the claimed sense. It is what
Chapter 10 called it: a real lineage delivering, at the far end, a different object.

**The correct formulation, and the one this book will hold to: Hermetic Qabalah is a
nineteenth-century Western esoteric system, built from Christian Cabalistic materials at
several removes from their Jewish sources, and it should never be described as ancient,
Jewish, or a preservation of anything.**

---

### Why the disguise?

Which brings the question this chapter is really about.

The Golden Dawn's system is an achievement. Whatever one thinks of magic, the construction
of a complete, internally consistent symbolic architecture capable of organizing the whole
of Western religious imagery is a serious intellectual feat, and it was performed by
identifiable people in about a decade.

Why did they not say so?

Why the Cipher Manuscripts, the German adept, the Rosicrucian descent, the claim to be
transmitting rather than making?

Three answers, and I think all three are true.

**Because authority in that period required antiquity.** This is the deepest reason and it
runs through the whole book. In a culture formed by the *prisca theologia* — where truth is
old, one, and scattered — a system announced as new is announced as false. Novelty was not
a selling point; it was a disqualification. The founders needed a pedigree not to deceive
but to be *credible*, including to themselves.

**Because the material felt found rather than made.** Anyone who has assembled a large
system knows the experience of discovering that pieces fit. When the twenty-two trumps
match the twenty-two paths, it does not feel like a coincidence you have exploited. It
feels like a structure you have uncovered. The step from *this fits* to *this was always
here* is very short, and a person can take it in complete sincerity.

**And because the initiatory form demands it.** A graded order needs superiors. If the
Chiefs are simply three men in London who wrote the rituals last year, the grades confer
nothing but each other's opinion. Someone must be above them, or the ladder has no top.

That third reason is structural and it is why this keeps happening. Von Hund needed Unknown
Superiors. Blavatsky needed Mahatmas. Westcott needed Fräulein Sprengel. Mathers needed
Secret Chiefs. None of them could produce one. Every one of their organizations broke on the
demand.

**An initiatory hierarchy requires an ultimate authority, and if there is no real one, one
will be manufactured, and the manufacture will eventually be discovered.**

That is as close to a law as this book is prepared to state.

There is one figure who saw the problem clearly and solved it in the only way available. He
did not claim a German adept or a Tibetan master. He claimed that a discarnate intelligence
had dictated a book to him in Cairo in 1904, that he himself was its prophet, and that the
authority was therefore his.

It is not a solution most people can use. But it is, at least, an honest answer to the
question of where the ladder ends.

---

# CHAPTER FORTY-THREE
## Crowley

He was born in 1875 into a family of Plymouth Brethren — a small, rigorous, biblically
literalist sect — in Leamington Spa. His father was a preacher and a former brewer, and by
all accounts the boy adored him. His father died when he was eleven. His mother, dealing
with an increasingly difficult son, reportedly called him the Beast of Revelation.

He took the name and kept it for sixty years.

Edward Alexander Crowley — Aleister, later, because he liked the rhythm — inherited money,
went to Cambridge, climbed mountains at a genuinely world-class level, wrote a great deal of
poetry of which some is good, and joined the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn in November
1898.

He is the terminus of the line this book has been tracing, and the reason to include him is
not sensation. It is that he is the first person in this history to state plainly what the
machinery was for.

---

### What he inherited

Crowley's Golden Dawn membership lasted about eighteen months before the Order tore itself
apart, and he was in the middle of the tearing — Mathers's agent in the London affair of
April 1900 described in Chapter 41.

What he took from it was the entire system: the grade structure on the Tree of Life, the
correspondences, the pentagram and hexagram rituals, the Enochian material, the tarot
attributions, the scrying, the Egyptian god-forms. He spent the rest of his life
elaborating and publishing it.

Two acts of publication matter most. In *The Equinox*, from 1909, he printed Golden Dawn
ritual material that the surviving fragments of the Order regarded as secret — an act
Mathers tried and failed to stop by injunction. And in *Liber 777*, also 1909, he published
the tables of correspondence in extended form, adding Eastern material to the Order's
Western scheme.

Those publications are the reason the Golden Dawn system survived. Regardie's four volumes
of the 1930s completed the job, but Crowley opened it, and he did so on the explicit ground
that a secret which dies with an order is worth nothing.

---

### A∴A∴ and the problem of authority

In 1907 he founded his own order, the A∴A∴, with George Cecil Jones. Its structure is the
Golden Dawn's, rationalized: a sequence of grades ascending the Tree, with a curriculum, a
reading list, and — the significant innovation — a rule that each member knows only the
person who initiated him and the people he initiates.

That rule is a direct response to the failure of Chapter 41. If there is no collective
body, there is no collective revolt. If nobody knows who the Chiefs are, nobody can demand
that they be produced.

It is an elegant solution to a structural problem, and it is worth noting that Crowley,
having watched the Golden Dawn destroy itself over the authority question, designed his own
order specifically so that it could not happen the same way.

His own answer to the authority question was different again, and we will come to it.

---

### The O.T.O.

In 1912 Crowley was contacted by Theodor Reuss.

Reuss was a German with a long involvement in fringe-Masonic bodies: rites of Memphis and
Misraim, Scottish Rite bodies of irregular provenance, and a network of high-degree
patents that circulated in the murkier reaches of continental Masonry. He had assembled
these into the **Ordo Templi Orientis**, an order with a ladder of degrees built on
Masonic models, whose higher grades taught a secret which Reuss held to be the key to all
magic: a technique of sexual magic.

Reuss's account — which Crowley enjoyed repeating — was that he confronted Crowley over a
passage in *The Book of Lies*, published that year, which he believed disclosed the order's
highest secret. Crowley claimed to have written it without knowing. Reuss made him head of
the O.T.O. for Great Britain and Ireland.[^1]

This is Crowley's Masonic connection, and it needs stating precisely because it is the
single most-abused fact in the anti-Masonic literature.

The O.T.O. is built on a Masonic degree template. Its early rituals derive from irregular
continental high-degree systems. Its terminology is Masonic. Reuss possessed patents of
various provenance for rites that most regular Grand Lodges regarded as clandestine.

The O.T.O. is not, and never was, recognized by any regular Grand Lodge. Crowley's Masonic
initiations — there were several, of different kinds, including one in Mexico in 1900 and
later contacts with Co-Masonry and with irregular bodies — did not give him a standing that
regular Freemasonry acknowledged, and he knew it. He wrote about the distinction himself,
sometimes with irritation and sometimes with amusement.[^2]

So the correct statement is: **Crowley was deeply involved with Masonic *forms*, through
irregular and co-Masonic channels, and had no recognized standing in regular
Freemasonry.**

Both halves of that sentence are load-bearing. Anti-Masonic writing drops the second half.
Masonic writing sometimes drops the first, which is equally misleading, because the O.T.O.'s
Masonic derivation is real and documented.

---

### Thelema

In April 1904, in a flat in Cairo, Crowley wrote out — or, on his account, took dictation
of — a short text in three chapters, delivered by a discarnate intelligence called Aiwass
over three days.

*Liber AL vel Legis*, the Book of the Law, announces a new aeon. Its central formulations
are: *Do what thou wilt shall be the whole of the Law*, and *Love is the law, love under
will*, and *Every man and every woman is a star*.

The first of these is the most misunderstood sentence in modern esotericism. It is not
permission. In Crowley's elaboration, the True Will is not what a person happens to want; it
is the deepest orientation of their nature, the thing they exist to do, and discovering it
is the work of a lifetime. Most of what people want, on this account, is precisely what
obstructs it. The formula is closer to *find your nature and follow it without deviation*
than to *do as you please*, and Crowley was explicit on the point.

This is also, structurally, his answer to the authority problem of the last chapter. He did
not claim a German adept or a Tibetan master. He claimed that the source had spoken to him
directly, that he was its prophet, and that the authority therefore rested in the book and
in his relation to it.

It is not a solution anybody else can borrow. It is, at least, not a forgery.

---

### The definition

Crowley's contribution to this book's central question is a single sentence, and it is worth
taking seriously rather than rhetorically.

He defined Magick — with the *k*, to distinguish it from conjuring — as *the Science and Art
of causing Change to occur in conformity with Will*.

And he immediately noted that this definition includes an enormous range of ordinary
activity. Writing a book is magick: the writer conceives a change and effects it by
arranging causes. So is baking a cake. He made the point deliberately, and it is the key to
what he was actually proposing.

Because notice what the definition removes. It contains no spirits. It contains no
supernatural mechanism. It contains no claim about non-physical entities, and although
Crowley worked constantly with such entities in practice, he repeatedly offered a
psychological account of them as a legitimate alternative — the spirits of the grimoires, he
suggested in one well-known passage, may be portions of the operator's own brain.[^3]

What is left, when the metaphysics is bracketed, is a technology of the will: ritual,
symbol, concentration, imagination and discipline, applied to the transformation of the
operator.

Which is precisely where this book's argument has been going since Chapter 2.

---

### The end of the line

He spent his later decades in decline: heroin addiction that began with a doctor's
prescription for asthma, bankruptcy, expulsion from Italy, the tabloid campaigns that made
him the Wickedest Man in the World, and a long tail of quarrels. He died in Hastings in
1947, in a boarding house, aged seventy-two.

He was, by most accounts including those of people who liked him, frequently cruel, a poor
custodian of the people who depended on him, and the author of a good deal of racial and
sexual invective that is genuinely unpleasant to read. I do not want to launder that.

He was also a serious scholar of the material, a ritualist of real ability, a translator, a
formidable mountaineer, and — the thing that matters here — the person who took the whole
apparatus this book has been tracing and said out loud what it was.

---

### The audit

What did Crowley take from Freemasonry?

**The O.T.O.'s degree structure**, via Reuss, via irregular continental high degrees, via
the eighteenth-century explosion of Chapter 24. Documented.

**The A∴A∴'s graded ascent**, via the Golden Dawn, via the SRIA, via the Gold- und
Rosenkreuz. Documented.

**The ritual grammar** — temple, officers, obligation, staged revelation, initiation as
transformation. Documented, and by this point in the book unremarkable.

**Nothing doctrinal.** There is not a single element of Thelemic doctrine that derives from
Masonic ritual. The Craft has no cosmology to lend.

Which is the same finding as Chapter 41, the same finding as Chapter 28, the same finding as
Chapter 27, and the same finding as Chapter 29.

**Freemasonry supplied the form. Every time. To everybody. And never the content.**

Part VI is complete. We have followed the Masonic apparatus from a Charleston degree system
to a Parisian magus to a London coroner's library to a Cairo hotel room, and at every stage
the transmission has been the same: the structure travels, and whoever receives it fills it
with what they have.

What remains is to ask why the structure works — which is the subject of Part VII — and then
to put the whole thing on trial.

---

and Marco Pasi, *Aleister Crowley and the Temptation of Politics* (Durham: Acumen, 2014).
**[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED — dates, bodies and jurisdictions; the record is
complicated and the claims should be given individually.]**

---

# PART VII — THE HIDDEN ENGINE

# CHAPTER FORTY-FOUR
## Ritual as Technology

We have followed the apparatus for three hundred years and six countries. It is time to ask
what it does.

I want to be careful about the register of this part of the book. Parts I to VI were
history: documents, dates, transmission. Part VII is analysis, and analysis of a different
kind — it uses modern theoretical frameworks to describe a mechanism, and those frameworks
were not available to the people being described.

The rule established in Chapter 2 governs everything that follows. I will make historical
claims, sociological claims and carefully hedged psychological claims. I will not make
metaphysical claims, and I will not diagnose any dead person's inner life. Where I use a
theory, I will say whose it is and what it was built for.

---

### The word "technology"

I have used it throughout and it deserves defending, because it sounds like a metaphor and
I mean it more literally than that.

A technology is a reliable arrangement of means to produce an effect. It does not have to
be mechanical. A legal contract is a technology; so is double-entry bookkeeping; so is the
sonnet form. What makes something a technology rather than a practice is that it can be
*transmitted* — written down, taught, reproduced in a different place by different people,
and expected to work again.

By that standard, the Masonic initiation is unambiguously a technology. It was specified in
detail. It was taught. It was carried to four continents by men who had learned it in one
room and reproduced it in another. It was adopted wholesale by organizations with entirely
different aims — friendly societies, Rosicrucian orders, magical societies, women's orders,
Black fraternal bodies — on the evident assumption that it would go on working.

And it did. That is the interesting part. A ritual designed in eighteenth-century London for
English gentlemen produced recognizably similar effects on free Black Bostonians in 1787,
Parisian duchesses in 1775, Bengali clerks in 1860, Prussian alchemists in 1780, and
Victorian magicians in 1890.

Something in the arrangement is doing work independent of what anybody believes it means.

---

### The components

Strip the initiation of Chapter 4 to its mechanism and it has seven parts. Every one is
present in the eighteenth-century sources; every one recurs in every derivative system in
this book.

**Separation.** The candidate is removed from ordinary company and placed alone. He waits.

**Divestment.** Money and metals removed; clothing disarranged; the ordinary markers of
status suspended.

**Sensory restriction.** The blindfold. He cannot see, which means the room must be
constructed from sound, touch and movement.

**Ordeal.** Not injury, but controlled stress: the point at the breast, the rope, the
questions he cannot answer, the awareness that he does not know what happens next.

**Commitment.** The oath, spoken aloud, in his own voice, kneeling, before witnesses, with
consequences named.

**Revelation.** The blindfold removed at a prepared instant, on a prepared image.

**Reincorporation.** He is given a place, a title, a badge, and a body of people who now
address him differently.

Van Gennep's tripartite scheme of 1909 — separation, liminality, incorporation — maps onto
this cleanly, and Victor Turner's elaboration of the middle phase supplies the vocabulary
for what happens in it: the stripping of status, the equality of initiands, the openness of
the person in transition.[^1]

I offer those as descriptive tools. Van Gennep was generalizing from ethnographic reports
of other societies; Turner was working with Ndembu material. Neither had a London tavern in
mind, and the fit — while genuinely good — is a fit between a general pattern and an
instance, not evidence of any historical relationship.

Catherine Bell's caution is the right one to hold alongside them: "ritual" is not a natural
kind, and treating it as a universal category with universal properties smuggles in more
than it explains.[^2]

---

### What the components do

Taking them one at a time, in the most deflationary terms available.

**Waiting** raises arousal. The candidate's attention is fully engaged before anything has
happened, which means the ceremony begins with a subject already primed.

**Divestment** removes the markers by which he ordinarily knows his position relative to
others. Whatever else he is in that room, he is not what his coat says he is.

**Sensory restriction** does two things. It forces him to build the space from non-visual
information, which is effortful and therefore memorable. And it removes his capacity to
anticipate, which means every event arrives without warning.

**Ordeal** produces physiological arousal, and arousal is the best-established modulator of
memory encoding we have. This is not a mystical claim; it is the reason people remember
where they were during disasters and forget entire ordinary Tuesdays.

**Commitment** engages a mechanism social psychology has documented extensively: a public,
effortful, voluntary declaration changes subsequent behavior and self-description far more
than a private or costless one. The candidate has spoken the words himself, in front of
people, having gone to trouble to get there.

**Revelation** places the content at the point of maximum receptivity. And the content is
deliberately modest — a book and two tools — which matters, because an extravagant
revelation would invite evaluation, and a modest one invites the recipient to supply the
significance himself. What a person constructs is held more firmly than what he is given.

**Reincorporation** converts the whole event into a durable social fact. He now has a status
that other people confirm every time they use it.

Seven components, each doing an identifiable job, assembled into a sequence in which each
prepares the next.

I do not think anyone designed this. There is no memorandum, no committee, no theorist. It
appears to have been arrived at by accretion and practical refinement over a long period —
which is how most technologies are arrived at, and which is entirely compatible with its
being extremely well made.

---

### What it cannot do

Here is where the honesty of the last part of this book has to start.

The mechanism above describes how an initiation produces **memory, commitment and status
change**. Those are well-evidenced effects and the sociological ones leave records: we can
see, in membership rolls and correspondence and charitable accounts and business
partnerships, that Masonic obligation had practical consequences for how people behaved.

It does not describe **moral transformation**, which is what the ritual claims to produce.

And on that, the evidence is simply not there. We have no way to determine whether men who
went through Masonic initiation became more upright, more charitable or more temperate than
comparable men who did not. There is no control group, no pre-registration, no measurement.
There is a great deal of testimony, and testimony from people who have undergone an
expensive commitment ceremony is precisely the testimony one should treat most carefully.

What we can say is narrower and still substantial: **the ritual reliably produces people who
report having been changed, who behave as members of a bounded group, and who remember the
event for the rest of their lives.**

Whether it made them better men is not a historian's question, and I am not going to pretend
to answer it.

---

### Bogdan's argument

The scholar who has done most with this material for the study of Western esotericism is
Henrik Bogdan, and his argument deserves setting out because it is the theoretical spine of
this book's thesis.[^3]

Bogdan's contention is that rituals of initiation are the principal *transmission vehicle*
of Western esotericism in the modern period — that the reason esoteric currents survived
from the Renaissance into the twentieth century is not a chain of secret teachers but the
existence of initiatory organizations capable of carrying them.

Put that beside the finding of Parts III to VI and the picture completes itself.

The esoteric currents of Part II — Hermetic, Kabbalistic, alchemical, Rosicrucian — had no
institutional carrier. They existed in books. Books are a poor transmission medium for a
tradition: they preserve content but not practice, community, authority or continuity. A
book can sit unread for two centuries.

Freemasonry supplied what the books could not: an *organizational form* for graded
initiatory instruction, reproducible anywhere, requiring no central authority, capable of
holding any content.

And from the moment it existed, the currents began using it. The Élus Coëns in 1761. The
Gold- und Rosenkreuz in the 1770s. The SRIA in 1866. The Golden Dawn in 1888. The O.T.O. in
1912. Wicca in the 1950s.

**The Western esoteric tradition survived into modernity because Freemasonry gave it a body
to travel in.**

That is the thesis, and this is the chapter where its mechanism is stated. The rest of Part
VII examines the three components that make the body work: symbol, secrecy, and the
initiate's own physical presence in a dark room.

---
