| Date | Event |
|---|---|
| c. 100–300 CE | Greek *Corpus Hermeticum* composed in Roman Egypt |
| c. 200–300 | Latin *Asclepius* |
| c. 205–270 | Plotinus; Neoplatonic emanation |
| c. 245–325 | Iamblichus; **theurgy distinguished from magic** |
| c. 500 | Pseudo-Dionysius; Neoplatonic hierarchy in Christian dress |

### Medieval

| Date | Event |
|---|---|
| c. 1119 | Knights Templar founded |
| c. 1280 | *Zohar* circulates in Castile |
| 1307–1312 | Templars arrested and suppressed |
| 18 March 1314 | Jacques de Molay burned |
| **c. 1390** | **Regius Manuscript — earliest Old Charges** |
| c. 1410 | Cooke Manuscript |
| 15th c. | Old Charges proliferate; craft legend of Euclid and Solomon |

### Renaissance

| Date | Event |
|---|---|
| 1463 | Hermetic manuscripts reach Florence |
| **1471** | **Ficino's *Pimander* printed** |
| 1486 | Pico, *Conclusiones*; Christian Cabala announced |
| 1489 | Ficino, *De vita libri tres* |
| 1494, 1517 | Reuchlin, *De verbo mirifico*; *De arte cabalistica* |
| **1533** | **Agrippa, *De occulta philosophia libri tres*** |
| 1564 | Dee, *Monas Hieroglyphica* |
| 1582–89 | Dee and Kelley's angelic sessions |
| 1596–1604 | Villalpando's Temple reconstruction |
| **1598, 1599** | **Schaw Statutes; the Scottish lodge system** |
| 1599– | Minutes of Lodge of Edinburgh begin |
| 1600 | John Boswell attends — earliest recorded non-operative |

### Seventeenth century

| Date | Event |
|---|---|
| **1614** | **Casaubon dates the Hermetica to the Christian era; *Fama Fraternitatis* published** |
| 1615, 1616 | *Confessio*; *Chymische Hochzeit* |

| 1620s | London Company of Masons' "Acception" |

### Eighteenth century

# APPENDIX B
## Glossary of Masonic Terms

Usage varies by jurisdiction, period and rite. Definitions here describe historical usage
as it appears in the sources cited in this book, not current practice in any particular
obedience.

**Accepted Mason.** A member of a masons' lodge who is not a working mason. English usage
from at least the seventeenth century.

**Acception.** A body within or alongside the London Company of Masons, recorded from the
1620s, into which men could be admitted; an early English non-operative practice.

**Adoption, Rite of.** A Masonic-form rite for women, worked in France from 1744 under the
authority of men's lodges, with its own degrees and a symbolism drawn largely from Genesis.

**Antients and Moderns.** Rival English grand lodges. The "Moderns" (the premier Grand
Lodge, from 1717 or 1721) and the "Antients" (from 1751, appealing to Irish immigrants and
men outside the established elite). Reconciled at the Union of 1813.

**Apron.** The principal badge of a Freemason; originally a working mason's leather
protective garment, later a ceremonial object of white lambskin, frequently decorated.

**Ashlar, rough and perfect.** A stone as quarried and a stone squared for building; in
Masonic allegory, the candidate before and after improvement.

**Cable-tow.** The cord placed about the candidate in the admission ceremony.

**Catechism.** The memorized question-and-answer sequence by which a Mason's standing is
tested and the lodge's symbolism rehearsed.

**Charges, the.** Regulations of conduct read to a candidate. In the Old Charges, appended
to the legendary history; in Anderson, printed as "The Charges of a Free-Mason."

**Craft, the.** The three symbolic degrees — Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft, Master Mason
— as distinct from appendant and high-degree bodies.

**Degree.** A grade of membership, conferred by ceremony, admitting to further instruction.

**Deputation / warrant / charter.** The document by which a grand body authorises a lodge to
work.

**East.** The principal station of the lodge, occupied by the Master; the direction from
which light is said to come.

**Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft, Master Mason.** The three Craft degrees.

**Exposure.** A published disclosure of Masonic ritual. The English and French exposures of
1723–1762 are the principal ritual evidence for the period.

**Great Architect of the Universe.** The formula by which the deity is referred to in Masonic
ritual; deliberately non-confessional.

**Hiram Abiff.** The craftsman of the Masonic legend, murdered at the building of Solomon's
Temple; derived from the bronze-worker of 1 Kings 7, who in scripture is neither architect
nor murdered.

**Hoodwink.** The blindfold used in the admission ceremony.

**Irregular / clandestine.** Terms used by a grand body for another body it does not
recognize. Jurisdictional and political, not historical, and in the American case carrying a
racial history.

**Jachin and Boaz.** The two pillars of Solomon's Temple (1 Kings 7:21), represented in the
lodge; also the title of the most successful eighteenth-century exposure (1762).

**Lodge.** Originally a shed beside a building site; from the Schaw Statutes onward, a
permanent constituted body; also the room in which that body meets, and the symbolic space
it represents.

**Obligation.** The oath taken by a candidate.

**Old Charges.** The manuscript constitutions of the English masons, from the Regius (c.
1390) and Cooke (c. 1410) manuscripts through more than a hundred later copies, containing a
legendary craft history and regulations.

**Operative / speculative.** Working stonemasons as against non-working members. The older
narrative of a straightforward transition from one to the other is contested.

**Prince Hall Freemasonry.** The tradition descending from African Lodge No. 459, warranted
by the Grand Lodge of England on 29 September 1784 with Prince Hall as Master.

**Provincial / District Grand Lodge.** An administrative body governing lodges in a region
or territory under a grand lodge.

**Regular.** Recognized by a given grand body as legitimate; see *irregular*.

**Royal Arch.** A degree centerd on the recovery of a lost divine name, developing in England
and Ireland in the mid-eighteenth century; included within "pure Ancient Masonry" by the
Act of Union of 1813.

**Scottish Rite (Ancient and Accepted).** A system of high degrees, thirty-three in its
developed form, of substantially French origin, organized in America from 1801.

**Tracing board.** A design containing the symbols of a degree; originally drawn on the floor
in chalk and erased after the meeting, later painted on cloth or board.

**Three Great Lights.** The volume of sacred law, the square and the compasses.

**Working tools.** Square, level, plumb-rule, gauge, gavel, trowel, compasses; presented with
moral interpretations that vary by jurisdiction.

**York Rite.** A grouping of appendant American degrees including the Royal Arch, cryptic
degrees and the Knights Templar.

---

# APPENDIX C
## Glossary of Magical and Esoteric Terms

**Alchemy (*chymistry*).** The early modern art of transformation of substances; both a
laboratory practice with material goals and an allegorical literature, frequently in the same
hands. The purely spiritual reading is a nineteenth-century development.

**Astral light.** Éliphas Lévi's term (1854) for a universal subtle medium, plastic to the
will; a synthesis of mesmeric fluid and the Renaissance *spiritus*.

**Baphomet.** A name of uncertain meaning recorded in the Templar trial depositions of
1307–12, probably a corruption of *Mahomet*; given its modern visual form by Lévi's drawing
of 1856, which illustrated a doctrine of equilibrium.

**Cabala (Christian).** The adaptation of Jewish Kabbalistic material for Christian
apologetic and magical purposes, from Pico (1486) and Reuchlin (1494, 1517) through
Agrippa (1533) to Knorr von Rosenroth's *Kabbala Denudata* (1677–84).

**Ceremonial magic.** Ritual operation addressed to spirits, angels or intelligences by name,
using circles, seals and formulae; the tradition of the medieval grimoires and of the Golden
Dawn.

**Correspondence.** The doctrine that levels of reality answer to one another, so that
material things participate in celestial and divine ones; the working assumption of
Renaissance magic and the basis of every table of attributions from Agrippa to Crowley.

**Decknamen.** Cover-names: the allegorical vocabulary by which alchemical texts concealed
technical procedures.

**Emanation.** The Neoplatonic doctrine that reality proceeds from a single source by
overflow rather than by creation, producing a graded cosmos.

**Enochian.** The angelic language and system of tables recorded by John Dee and Edward
Kelley in the 1580s, elaborated into a magical system by the Golden Dawn.

**Gematria.** The Kabbalistic technique of numerical equivalence between words.

**Hermeticism.** Four distinct referents: (1) the Greek *Corpus Hermeticum* of the 2nd–3rd
centuries CE; (2) the technical Hermetica of astrology, alchemy and magic; (3) Renaissance
Hermetism after Ficino's 1471 *Pimander*; (4) "Hermeticism" as a modern occult
self-description. Never one unchanged tradition.

**Kabbalah.** The Jewish mystical tradition: *Sefer Yetzirah*, the *Zohar* (c. 1280),
Cordovero, Luria. Distinct from Christian Cabala and from Hermetic Qabalah.

**Magic.** Not a stable category. Always qualified in this book: natural, celestial,
ceremonial, sympathetic, talismanic, angelic, planetary, theurgic, thaumaturgic. *Magick*
with a *k* is Crowley's usage from c. 1904.

**Mesmerism / animal magnetism.** Franz Anton Mesmer's doctrine of a universal magnetic
fluid; discredited as to mechanism by the French royal commission of 1784, and enormously
productive thereafter as a source of trance technique and of a vocabulary of invisible
influence.

**Natural magic.** The manipulation of hidden (*occult*, in the strict sense) properties of
physical things; substantially pre-modern natural science.

**Occultism.** A mid-nineteenth-century coinage and formation, not an ancient category.

**Pentagram.** The five-pointed star; its contrast of point-up and point-down orientations as
signifying spirit over matter and the reverse is Lévi's, from the 1850s, with no earlier
pedigree traced.

**Prisca theologia.** The doctrine that a single ancient revelation underlies all traditions,
transmitted through a chain of sages; the framework within which resemblance counts as
evidence of descent. The principal object of this book's criticism.

**Qabalah (Hermetic).** The nineteenth- and twentieth-century occult system deriving from
Lévi and the Golden Dawn, incorporating tarot, elemental and planetary attributions that have
no Jewish precedent.

**Rosicrucianism.** Five distinct referents: the manifestos (1614–16); the seventeenth-century
furore; the eighteenth-century Gold- und Rosenkreuz; nineteenth-century revivals (SRIA,
Golden Dawn); and modern bodies claiming ancient descent.

**Sephiroth / sefirot.** The ten emanations of the Kabbalistic Tree of Life.

**Solve et coagula.** Dissolve and coagulate; the fundamental rhythm of the alchemical work.

**Sunthēmata.** Iamblichus's term for the material tokens through which the divine is present
at the material level.

**Talismanic magic.** The construction of images at astrologically determined times to
concentrate celestial influence.

**Theurgy.** Ritual operation directed at the ascent of the soul, distinguished by its
Iamblichan practitioners from *goēteia* or sorcery. Not a synonym for magic.

**Thaumaturgy.** Wonder-working; the production of visible marvels.

**Tree of Life.** The diagrammatic arrangement of the ten *sefirot* and the twenty-two paths
connecting them.

---

# APPENDIX D
## Major Masonic Rites and Degrees

A guide to the principal systems referred to in this book. It is not exhaustive: the
eighteenth century produced many hundreds of degrees, most of which were worked briefly by
small numbers of people and left only manuscripts.

Dates are of origin or of the earliest reliable evidence. Where a body's own claimed date of
origin differs from the documented one, both are given.

---

### The Craft

**The three symbolic degrees.** Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft, Master Mason. Two degrees
are evidenced in the Scottish catechisms from 1696 and in the English exposures of 1723–24;
the third appears in print in 1730 and was being worked in London lodges from the
mid-1720s. Worked, with substantial variation, by every regular jurisdiction in the world.

**The Royal Arch.** Developing in England and Ireland from the mid-eighteenth century;
centerd on the recovery of a lost divine name. Declared part of "pure Ancient Masonry" by
the English Act of Union of 1813. Its origin is an open question.

---

### English and Irish

**Premier Grand Lodge ("Moderns").** Founded 1717 according to Anderson (1738), or 1721
according to Prescott and Sommers. Minutes from 1723.

**Antients Grand Lodge.** 1751. Laurence Dermott's *Ahiman Rezon* (1756) is its
constitution.

**United Grand Lodge of England.** 1813, uniting the two.

**Knights Templar (Great Priory).** A commemorative chivalric order within English Masonry;
no claim of literal descent.

---

### The French and continental high degrees

**Écossais grades.** "Scottish" degrees, so called after Ramsay's Oration of 1736/37; the
general term for French high-degree Masonry, with essentially no connection to Scotland.

**Elect (Élus) degrees.** From the 1740s; narratives of the pursuit and punishment of
Hiram's murderers. Among the earliest high degrees.

**Rose-Croix.** From the 1750s; a Christological reworking of the Masonic legend, the lost
word identified with the Word. Became the eighteenth degree of the Scottish Rite. Its name
has no documented connection to the Rosicrucian manifestos.

**Rite of Strict Observance.** Karl Gotthelf von Hund, from 1754. Claimed direct descent
from the Knights Templar under Unknown Superiors. Dominant in the German lands in the 1760s
and 1770s. Its Templar claim was set aside at the Convent of Wilhelmsbad in 1782.

**Order of Knight-Masons Elect Priests of the Universe (Élus Coëns).** Martinez de
Pasqually, 1761. Theurgic; the first documented entry of operative magical practice into a
Masonic structure.

**Régime Écossais Rectifié / Rectified Scottish Rite.** Jean-Baptiste Willermoz; Convent of
Lyon 1778, endorsed at Wilhelmsbad 1782. Built on the Strict Observance's structure with
Martinez's doctrine of the fall and reintegration, and Saint-Martin's interiority. Its
members are the Chevaliers Bienfaisants de la Cité Sainte. Still worked.

**Rite of Adoption.** From 1744. Four principal degrees — Apprentice, Companion, Mistress,
Perfect Mistress — with symbolism drawn from Genesis. Worked by women's lodges under men's
lodges. See Chapter 29.

**Egyptian Rite.** Cagliostro, 1784–85. Egyptian in costume, eighteenth-century in
substance.

**Rites of Memphis and of Misraim.** Nineteenth century; systems of very large numbers of
degrees (ninety and upward), of irregular provenance, which supplied much of the fringe-
Masonic material later assembled by Theodor Reuss.

---

### American

**Ancient and Accepted Scottish Rite.** Supreme Council founded at Charleston, 1801. Thirty-
three degrees. Albert Pike, Sovereign Grand Commander of the Southern Jurisdiction
1859–1891, rewrote the rituals and published *Morals and Dogma* (1871) as their commentary.

**York Rite.** A grouping of appendant degrees: Royal Arch, cryptic degrees, and the
Knights Templar commanderies.

**Prince Hall Freemasonry.** African Lodge No. 459, warranted by the Grand Lodge of England
29 September 1784, Prince Hall Master; Provincial Grand Master 1791. The tradition
descending from it.

**Order of the Eastern Star.** Nineteenth century; an American body admitting women related
to Master Masons, distinct in structure from the Adoption Rite.

---

### Mixed and women's

**Le Droit Humain.** Founded 1893 by Maria Deraismes and Georges Martin; admits men and
women on equal terms in the same lodges. International.

**Co-Masonry in Britain and the Empire.** From 1902, promoted by Annie Besant; the principal
route by which full Masonic ritual entered the Theosophical movement and spread
internationally.

**The Order of Women Freemasons; the Honourable Fraternity of Ancient Freemasons.**
Twentieth-century English women's bodies.

---

### Bodies requiring Masonic membership

These are not Masonic rites but are included because the requirement is the mechanism this
book identifies as the principal route of transmission.

**Gold- und Rosenkreuz.** Central Europe, from the 1750s, reorganised 1777. Nine grades.
Alchemical. Required Master Mason status for admission.

**Societas Rosicruciana in Anglia (SRIA).** 1866. Grade structure derived from the Gold- und
Rosenkreuz. Required Master Mason status. Its members founded the Golden Dawn.

---

# APPENDIX E
## Major Occult Orders

Each entry gives: founding, founders, structure, content, relationship to Freemasonry, and
the evidentiary status of its origin claim.

---

### Order of the Gold and Rosy Cross (Gold- und Rosenkreuz)

**Founded** Central Europe, from the 1750s; reorganised 1777.
**Structure** Nine grades, from Juniores to Magus.
**Content** Alchemy, Rosicrucian and theosophical material.
**Masonic relation** Required Master Mason status for admission. The clearest institutional
evidence of the carriage thesis in the eighteenth century.
**Origin claim** Descent from a seventeenth-century Rosicrucian brotherhood. **Unsupported:
no such organization existed.**
**Notes** Its members Wöllner and Bischoffwerder reached ministerial power in Prussia after
1786. Its grade structure descends to the SRIA and thence to the Golden Dawn.

---

### Order of Knight-Masons Elect Priests of the Universe (Élus Coëns)

**Founded** 1761, Bordeaux, by Martinez de Pasqually.
**Content** Theurgic operations aimed at the *réintégration* of fallen beings; doctrine set
out in the unfinished *Traité de la réintégration des êtres*.
**Masonic relation** Built on Masonic degree structure; recruited from lodges.
**Origin claim** A patent of doubtful provenance attributed to Charles Edward Stuart.
**Unverified.**
**Notes** The first documented entry of operative magic into a Masonic structure. Its
students Saint-Martin and Willermoz carried the doctrine in opposite directions.

---

### The Bavarian Illuminati

**Founded** 1776, Ingolstadt, by Adam Weishaupt.
**Content** Enlightenment rationalism, anticlericalism, reformist politics.
**Method** Systematic infiltration of Masonic lodges.
**Fate** Suppressed 1784–87; internal papers published by the Bavarian state, Munich, 1787.
**Origin claim** None of consequence.
**Notes** The documented facts are the basis of the entire modern conspiracy tradition,
constructed from the published papers by Robison and Barruel in 1797–98. **No evidence of
survival after 1787.**

---

### Theosophical Society

**Founded** 1875, New York, by H. P. Blavatsky, H. S. Olcott and W. Q. Judge.
**Content** A universal ancient wisdom-religion underlying all traditions; karma,
reincarnation, spiritual evolution; Asian material filtered through Victorian
Orientalism.
**Masonic relation** Indirect but substantial, through Annie Besant's promotion of
Co-Masonry from 1902.
**Origin claim** Direction by the Mahatmas or Masters, a hidden brotherhood of adepts.
**Unsupported.**
**Notes** Among the most culturally consequential organizations in this book. Its effects on
Indian and Sinhalese cultural nationalism, on modern astrology, and on twentieth-century
alternative spirituality are large and documented.

---

### Societas Rosicruciana in Anglia (SRIA)

**Founded** 1866, by Robert Wentworth Little and a circle of English Masons.
**Structure** Grades derived from the Gold- und Rosenkreuz.
**Masonic relation** Master Mason status required for membership. **The pipeline.**
**Origin claim** Rosicrucian descent. **Unsupported as institutional descent; the material
inheritance from the Gold- und Rosenkreuz is real.**
**Notes** Its members included Kenneth Mackenzie, Frederick Hockley, W. R. Woodman, W. W.
Westcott and S. L. MacGregor Mathers.

---

### Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn

**Founded** 1888, London, by Westcott, Mathers and Woodman, on the Cipher Manuscripts and
the "Sprengel" correspondence.
**Structure** Grades mapped onto the Sephiroth of the Tree of Life; an Outer Order and a
Second Order with the Vault of the Adepti.
**Content** Hermetic Qabalah, tables of correspondence, ritual invocation and banishing,
scrying, astral work, talismanic magic, tarot, Enochian magic, Egyptian god-forms.
**Masonic relation** Structural, not doctrinal: grades, temple, officers, obligation,
staged revelation, regalia — a Masonic machine running non-Masonic software.
**Origin claim** Authority from a German Rosicrucian adept, Anna Sprengel of Nuremberg.
**Unsupported: the correspondence is spurious, and Mathers said so in 1900.**
**Notes** Admitted women on equal terms from the beginning. Collapsed 1900–1903 into
successor bodies. Its system, published by Crowley (1909–13) and Regardie (1937–40), is the
basis of modern Western ceremonial magic.

---

### Martinist Order (revived)

**Founded** 1891, Paris, by Papus (Gérard Encausse) and others.
**Content** Christian theosophy in the tradition of Saint-Martin.
**Origin claim** Institutional descent from Saint-Martin's circle. **Unsupported: Saint-
Martin had readers, not an order. The doctrinal inheritance is real.**

---

### A∴A∴

**Founded** 1907, by Aleister Crowley and George Cecil Jones.
**Structure** Golden Dawn grades on the Tree of Life, rationalized, with a curriculum and a
reading list. Each member knows only his initiator and his initiates.
**Origin claim** Crowley's own attainment and the authority of *Liber AL vel Legis*.
**Notes** The structural rule is a deliberate solution to the authority crisis that
destroyed the Golden Dawn.

---

### Ordo Templi Orientis (O.T.O.)

**Founded** c. 1902–06, by Carl Kellner and Theodor Reuss; Crowley chartered for Great
Britain and Ireland in 1912 and later head of the order.
**Structure** A degree ladder built on Masonic high-degree models drawn from irregular
continental rites, including Memphis and Misraim.
**Content** After Crowley, Thelema; sexual magic in the higher degrees.
**Masonic relation** Derived from fringe-Masonic degree systems. **Not recognized by any
regular Grand Lodge.**
**Origin claim** Templar and Masonic descent. **Unsupported as descent; the borrowing of
degree structure from irregular Masonic rites is documented.**

---

# APPENDIX F
## Primary Sources

A working list of the documents on which this book chiefly depends, with a note on what each
can and cannot be used to establish. Full bibliographical details are in the Bibliography.

---

### Masonic manuscripts

**Regius MS** (c. 1390, BL Royal 17 A.I) and **Cooke MS** (c. 1410, BL Add. 23198).
*Establish:* that the masons' craft carried a legendary history running through Euclid and
Solomon's Temple, and a body of regulations, by the late fourteenth century.
*Do not establish:* any esoteric content. The Temple here is a guild origin story.

**Schaw Statutes** (28 December 1598; 28 December 1599).
*Establish:* the permanent Scottish lodge system, its record-keeping requirement, its
jurisdiction, and the art-of-memory clause.
*Do not establish:* which art of memory was meant.

**Minutes of the Lodge of Edinburgh (Mary's Chapel)**, from 1599, and **Aitchison's Haven**,
from 1598.
*Establish:* continuous institutional existence, and non-operative attendance from 1600.
*Do not establish:* any ritual content; the minutes concern money, discipline and dinner.

**Edinburgh Register House MS** (1696), **Chetwode Crawley MS** (c. 1700), **Kevan MS**
(c. 1714).
*Establish:* the earliest recoverable ritual: two degrees, a word, a grip, a catechism.
*Do not establish:* continuity with anything earlier.

---

### Diaries and contemporary observation

**Elias Ashmole, diary**, 16 October 1646 and March 1682 (ed. Josten, 1966).
*Establish:* the best-documented early English non-operative initiation, and a thirty-five-
year gap in his participation.
*Do not establish:* his reason for joining. He never states one.

**Robert Plot, *The Natural History of Staffordshire*** (1686).
*Establish:* that the custom was widespread, that persons of quality joined, that admission
involved a collation and secret signs, and that a written craft history circulated.
*Note:* a skeptical witness, which increases its value.

**John Aubrey, *Natural History of Wiltshire*** (MS 1686).

**William Stukeley, diary**, 6 January 1721 (Surtees Society edition).
*Establish:* his initiation at the Salutation Tavern, and his belief that he was the first
man made a Mason in London for many years and that members were hard to assemble.
*Bearing:* the principal external evidence against the 1717 foundation date.

---

### Printed constitutions

**Anderson, *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons*** (1723).
*Establish:* the institution's official self-presentation; the legendary history in print;
the religious formula; the charge against women and bondmen.
*Do not establish:* what happened in lodges.

**Anderson, *The New Book of Constitutions*** (1738).
*Establish:* the 1717 foundation narrative. **Sole source. Retrospective. Interested.**

**Dermott, *Ahiman Rezon*** (1756); **Preston, *Illustrations of Masonry*** (1772).

**Anderson's *Constitutions*, Philadelphia: Franklin** (1734). First Masonic book printed in
America.

---

### Exposures

*A Mason's Examination* (1723); *The Grand Mystery of Free-Masons Discover'd* (1724);
Prichard, *Masonry Dissected* (1730); Pérau, *Le Secret des Francs-Maçons* (1742);
*L'Ordre des Francs-Maçons Trahi* (1745); *Three Distinct Knocks* (1760); *Jachin and Boaz*
(1762).

*Establish, comparatively:* the ritual content at datable moments; the emergence of the
third degree and the Hiramic legend between 1724 and 1730; the accretion of symbolism
decade by decade; **and the absence, across forty years and two countries, of Kabbalah,
Hermeticism, alchemy, ceremonial magic, Egypt, Templars and Rosicrucians.**
*Caution:* commercial and sometimes hostile documents. Use comparatively, never singly.

---

### Esoteric primary texts

Ficino's *Pimander* (1471); Pico's *Conclusiones* (1486); Reuchlin (1494, 1517); Agrippa's
*De occulta philosophia* (1533) and its English translation (1651); the Rosicrucian
manifestos (1614–16); Knorr's *Kabbala Denudata* (1677–84); Böhme.

*Establish:* what was available to read, when, and in what language.
*Do not establish:* that any Mason read it. **Availability is opportunity, not
transmission.**

---

### Nineteenth- and twentieth-century occult sources

Lévi, *Dogme et rituel* (1854–56) and *Histoire de la magie* (1860); Constant, *La Bible de
la liberté* (1841); Blavatsky, *Isis Unveiled* (1877) and *The Secret Doctrine* (1888);
Mathers, *The Kabbalah Unveiled* (1887); Pike, *Morals and Dogma* (1871) and the de Hoyos
annotated edition (2011); Crowley, *The Equinox* (1909–13), *Liber 777* (1909), *Magick in
Theory and Practice* (1929–30); Regardie, *The Golden Dawn* (1937–40).

**The rule of direction applies.** These are primary sources for their own century and
unreliable secondary sources for every earlier one. Blavatsky on 1880s Theosophy is
evidence; Blavatsky on ancient Egypt is not.

---

### Anti-Masonic and conspiracy sources

Robison, *Proofs of a Conspiracy* (1797); Barruel, *Mémoires* (1797–98); *Einige
Originalschriften des Illuminatenordens* (Munich, 1787); Morgan, *Illustrations of Masonry*
(1826); Taxil's fabrications (1886–97); de la Rive (1894); Taxil's confession, *Le Frondeur*,
25 April 1897; the papal bulls of 1738 and 1884.

*Establish:* the existence, dating and content of accusations.
*Do not establish:* anything about Freemasonry. Used exclusively as evidence of belief —
except the Illuminati papers, which are genuine documents of a real organization.

---

# APPENDIX G
## Evidence Classification System

The method used throughout this book, set out formally so that a reader can apply it
independently — which is, in the end, the point of the exercise.

---

### I. The source hierarchy

| Level | Category | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | Primary sources: period documents, manuscripts, original editions, contemporary observation | Load-bearing evidence |
| **B** | Scholarly secondary sources: university presses, peer-reviewed journals, recognized specialists | Interpretation, framing, correction |
| **C** | Critical editions of primary texts, with apparatus | Access to A with scholarly support |
| **D** | Popular, partisan or derivative material | **Evidence of belief only**, and labeled as such in the text, not merely in a note |

**Two rules override the hierarchy.**

**The rule of direction.** A source is primary for its own period and secondary — often
unreliably so — for any earlier one. Blavatsky is a primary source for 1880s Theosophy and
a Level D source for ancient Egypt. Pike is a primary source for nineteenth-century American
Masonry and a Level D source for the ancient mysteries. Anderson's 1738 *Constitutions* is a
primary source for what Grand Lodge believed in 1738 and a weak secondary source for 1717.

**The rule against circularity.** No claim is admitted on the authority of a source that is
itself quoting a source the author has not consulted. Circular sourcing — a claim that
appears authoritative because it has been repeated rather than because it was ever verified
— is the characteristic failure of this literature.

---

### II. The six questions

Applied, in order, to any claim that something old was carried into something newer.

**1. Provenance.** Where is the physical object? Which archive, which shelfmark, which
edition? Who has held it since? A claim supported by a document nobody can point to is not
supported.

**2. Chronology.** Does the timeline permit the influence? This is the cheapest test and it
is skipped constantly.

**3. Transmission.** Can you name the route — a person, a text, a translation, a purchase, a
meeting? *Availability is not transmission.* That a book was in print in a country tells you
someone could have read it. It does not tell you anyone did.

**4. Intertextuality.** Does the later source quote, paraphrase or share distinctive
language with the earlier one? Resemblance is weak; quotation is strong; shared
idiosyncratic error is decisive.

**5. Institutional continuity.** Did the organization actually persist? Organizations that
exist collect dues, keep lists, rent rooms, quarrel and leave paper.

**6. Anachronism.** Does the claim contain something that could not yet have existed? A
symbol turning on an English initial cannot be Egyptian. A Kabbalistic system with tarot
attributions cannot predate the tarot.

---

### III. The governing rule

**A parallel is not a descent.**

If an element in one tradition resembles an element in another, that is a fact about the two
elements. To convert it into a fact about history requires a route (question 3) or textual
dependence (question 4).

Human beings across all cultures use light for knowledge, darkness for ignorance, height for
authority, the circle for completeness, and death-and-return for transformation. These recur
because human bodies and human societies recur. Their presence in two places is nearly
uninformative. It is the *unusual* features that carry evidential weight — a shared oddity,
a distinctive detail with no independent reason to exist.

---

### IV. The five claim-types

Never to be exchanged silently.

| Type | Form | Evidence required |
|---|---|---|
| **Historical** | "Practitioners believed X." | Their books, letters, rituals |
| **Phenomenological** | "Practitioners reported experiencing X." | First-person testimony |
| **Psychological** | "Ritual of this kind may produce effect Y." | Theory, hedged; never a diagnosis of an individual |
| **Sociological** | "Ritual of this kind creates structure Z." | Records: membership, accounts, networks |
| **Metaphysical** | "Mechanism X objectively exists." | **Never asserted by the narrator, in either direction** |

The characteristic failure of writing on this subject — in credulous and debunking books
alike — is the silent slide from *they believed* to *they experienced* to *it happened*.

---

### V. Verdict vocabulary

| Verdict | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Established** | Documented, uncontested, evidence available |
| **Strong** | Well evidenced; minor disagreement about scope |
| **Mixed** | Partly supported; the claim as usually stated overreaches the evidence |
| **Disputed** | Competent scholars disagree; evidence cuts both ways |
| **Speculative** | Chronologically possible; no transmission evidence |
| **Unsupported** | Fails the questions; no evidence exists |
| **Refuted** | Positively disproved, or withdrawn by its own proponents |

---

### VI. Signaling in the text

**Reconstruction must be marked.** Where prose reconstructs an atmosphere rather than
reporting a source, the text says so — by an explicit frame, by conditional grammar, or by a
note. Never by tone alone.

**Uncertainty must be stated.** "The evidence establishes," "the surviving sources
indicate," "this is disputed," "there is no surviving evidence that," "the chronology makes
this difficult to sustain" — used precisely, not decoratively.

**Attribution must be specific.** Not "scholars agree" but "Stevenson argues," "Hanegraaff
reframes," "Butler's library evidence shows." If the scholarship cannot be named, the
sentence should not be written.

**Unverified material must be flagged.** Where this manuscript could not verify a citation,
a quotation's wording, or a page reference, it carries **[SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED]**
rather than an invented particular. Nothing in this book has been fabricated to fill a gap.

---

# APPENDIX H
## Claims Frequently Made About Freemasonry — and Their Evidentiary Status

A reference table. Verdicts follow Appendix G. The chapter given is where the evidence is
set out.

---

### Origins

| Claim | Status | Ch. |
|---|---|---|
| Descends from the ancient Egyptian mysteries | **Unsupported** | 6, 21, 49 |
| Descends from the Knights Templar | **Unsupported**; set aside by its own adherents at Wilhelmsbad, 1782 | 26, 49 |
| Descends from a seventeenth-century Rosicrucian order | **Unsupported, and inverted** — the influence runs the other way | 12, 28, 49 |
| Descends from the builders of Solomon's Temple | **Unsupported**; the legend is medieval trade mythology | 14, 19 |
| Descends from Scottish operative lodges of 1598–99 | **Strong** | 13 |
| Derives from the English Old Charges tradition, c. 1390 onward | **Established** | 14 |
| Was reorganised in London in the 1720s by a Newtonian and Whig circle | **Strong** | 16 |
| Was founded as a Grand Lodge on 24 June 1717 | **Disputed** — single retrospective source; 1721 is better evidenced | 15 |

### Content

| Claim | Status | Ch. |
|---|---|---|
| Craft ritual contained Kabbalah before the nineteenth century | **Unsupported** — absent from all sources 1696–1762 | 23, 42 |
| Craft ritual contained Hermetic or alchemical material | **Unsupported** for the same period | 11, 23 |
| Craft ritual contained ceremonial magic | **Unsupported** | 4, 23 |
| The third degree derives from alchemy | **Unsupported**; the parallel is real, the descent is not | 11, 22 |
| The third degree derives from the ancient mysteries | **Unsupported** | 22 |
| The third degree first appears in print in 1730 | **Established** | 22, 23 |
| Masonic ritual preserves archaeological knowledge of the Temple | **Unsupported** | 19 |
| The letter G is an ancient symbol | **Refuted** — it depends on English initials | 21 |
| The All-Seeing Eye is a Masonic invention | **Unsupported** — it is standard Christian iconography | 21 |
| The inverted pentagram has ancient diabolical meaning | **Unsupported** — the contrast is Lévi's, 1850s | 21, 35 |

### Magic and the occult

| Claim | Status | Ch. |
|---|---|---|
| Operative magic entered a Masonic structure in 1761 (Élus Coëns) | **Established** | 27 |
| An alchemical order required Masonic membership (Gold- und Rosenkreuz) | **Established** | 28 |
| Freemasonry influenced the nineteenth-century occult revival | **Strong — the best-evidenced connection in the field** | 35–43, 49 |
| The Golden Dawn borrowed Masonic structure | **Established** | 41 |
| The Golden Dawn had German Rosicrucian authority | **Refuted** — Mathers said the letters were forged, 1900 | 41 |
| The O.T.O.'s degrees derive from irregular Masonic rites | **Established** | 43 |
| Crowley held recognized regular Masonic standing | **Unsupported** | 43 |
| Co-Masonry carried Masonic ritual into Theosophy | **Established** | 29, 40 |
| Hermetic Qabalah preserves ancient Jewish teaching | **Unsupported** — it is a nineteenth-century construction | 42 |

### Pike and Lucifer

| Claim | Status | Ch. |
|---|---|---|
| Pike wrote that Lucifer is God | **Refuted** — forged; the author confessed, 19 April 1897 | 39 |
| Pike wrote about Lucifer as light-bearer | **Established**, in a comparative-philological register | 38 |
| Pike discussed Baphomet | **Established**, in the Templar material | 38 |
| Pike took material from Éliphas Lévi without attribution | **Established** (de Hoyos, 2011) | 37 |
| The Palladium existed | **Refuted** — invented by Taxil | 39 |
| Diana Vaughan existed as described | **Refuted** | 39 |

### Conspiracy

| Claim | Status | Ch. |
|---|---|---|
| The Illuminati existed and infiltrated lodges | **Established** | 50 |
| The Illuminati survived after 1787 | **Unsupported** | 50 |
| Freemasons directed the French Revolution | **Unsupported** | 50 |
| Freemasons directed the American Revolution | **Unsupported** — members served on both sides | 31 |
| Masonic membership among Anglo-American elites was high and consequential | **Established** | 30–34 |
| Masons abducted William Morgan in 1826 and obstructed the investigation | **Established** | 33 |
| The Priory of Sion is an ancient order | **Refuted** — registered in France in 1956 | 50 |
| Washington D.C.'s street plan encodes occult geometry | **Unsupported** — Baroque planning convention | 50 |
| The Great Seal's reverse is a Masonic emblem | **Unsupported** | 21, 50 |
| Freemasonry was persecuted under fascist regimes on the basis of conspiracy literature | **Established** | 34, 50 |

### Race, gender and society

| Claim | Status | Ch. |
|---|---|---|
| Women were initiated in Masonic-form ritual from 1744 | **Established** | 29 |
| The Adoption Rite was a watered-down men's ritual | **Refuted** — its symbolism is independent, drawn from Genesis | 29 |
| African Lodge No. 459 was regularly warranted in 1784 | **Established** | 32 |
| Non-recognition of Prince Hall Masonry was jurisdictional rather than racial | **Unsupported** — the scholarship is unanimous | 32 |
| Masonic universalism was applied consistently | **Refuted** | 32, 34 |
| Masonic ritual form was adopted by friendly societies and mutual-aid orders | **Established** | 34 |

### The book's own thesis

| Claim | Status | Ch. |
|---|---|---|
| Freemasonry inherited the Western magical tradition | **Unsupported** | 49, 51 |
| Freemasonry supplied the organizational form that carried it | **Strong** | 27–29, 41, 44, 51 |
| Masonic ritual is a transmissible technology of transformation | **Strong as to mechanism; unevidenced as to moral outcome** | 44, 47 |
| Both traditions treat ritual and symbol as instruments for changing the operator | **Argued; offered as a hypothesis supported by the evidence, not as a demonstrated finding** | 2, 51 |

Robert E. Lee Ringler — Phase 1 research corpus

**Count: 140 entries.** Chicago (notes–bibliography) style. Entries carry an
evidence-level tag from the project's hierarchy:

- **[A]** Primary source (period document, manuscript, original edition)
- **[B]** Scholarly secondary source (university press, peer-reviewed)
- **[C]** Reputable modern critical edition of a primary text
- **[D]** Popular / partisan / derivative — usable only as evidence of belief

Verification tags: **✔** = bibliographic data confirmed in this research phase;
**~** = confident from standard scholarly reference but not re-verified here;
**[SVR]** = SOURCE VERIFICATION REQUIRED before citation in the manuscript.

## GAPS TO CLOSE BEFORE DRAFTING (see research_gaps section of the dossier)

- Prescott & Sommers, exact *AQC* citation (Quatuor Coronati site is egress-blocked
  in this environment; use *REHMLAC+* open-access version or library *AQC*).
- Page-level citations for all **~** entries actually quoted in the manuscript.
- French high-degree scholarship (Mollier, Porset, Kervella) consulted in the original.
- Prince Hall archival material: African Lodge No. 459 records, Boston.
- Golden Dawn Cipher MSS: current custodianship and published facsimile status.

Full details, including the fallback set for a constrained permissions budget, are in
`illustrations/illustration_candidates.md`.

**Every item is a real, locatable object. No image has been invented.** Rights status must
be confirmed with each holding institution before publication; items marked **[SVR]**
require confirmation of shelfmark or accession number before captioning.

**Captioning principle.** Each illustration is captioned with what it is, its date, where it
survives, and **what it does and does not prove**. Two items — no. 14, the *Rosarium* death
woodcut, and no. 33, Lévi's Baphomet — are included precisely because they are the most
frequently misused images in the popular literature, and their captions will say so.
